# InfectiousDisease

## HIV & AIDS

**Q:** A 32-year-old has fever, pharyngitis, lymphadenopathy, and a maculopapular rash 3 weeks after unprotected sex. HIV viral load is 850,000 copies/mL, CD4 count 540. What is the diagnosis?

**A:** Acute HIV seroconversion (primary HIV infection); viral load peaks, CD4 transiently drops; standard HIV ELISA/Western blot may be negative — use 4th generation test (HIV Ag/Ab combo assay)

Acute HIV presents 2-4 weeks after transmission as a mononucleosis-like illness. 4th generation HIV Ag/Ab tests detect p24 antigen before antibody seroconversion. AMMI Canada/PHAC guidelines recommend immediate ART regardless of CD4 count.

**Q:** What is the first-line ART regimen for treatment-naive HIV patients per DHHS/CIHR guidelines?

**A:** Preferred: bictegravir/tenofovir alafenamide/emtricitabine (BIC/TAF/FTC, Biktarvy) — single tablet regimen; alternative: dolutegravir + TAF/FTC (Dovato) or other INSTI-based regimens

INSTI (integrase strand transfer inhibitor)-based regimens are preferred for treatment-naive patients due to high potency, tolerability, and high barrier to resistance. Goal: undetectable viral load (<50 copies/mL) within 3-6 months. Resistance testing before starting ART.

**Q:** A 40-year-old with HIV and CD4 count of 85 cells/μL develops progressive dyspnea, bilateral interstitial infiltrates, and SpO2 88%. LDH is elevated. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PCP); treat with TMP-SMX (15-20 mg/kg/day of TMP) + prednisone if PaO2 <70 mmHg; adjunctive steroids reduce mortality

PCP is the most common AIDS-defining opportunistic infection when CD4 <200. Serum beta-D-glucan elevated. BAL with immunofluorescence or PCR confirms diagnosis. Primary prophylaxis with TMP-SMX when CD4 <200.

**Q:** What are the opportunistic infection prophylaxis thresholds in HIV?

**A:** CD4 <200: PCP prophylaxis (TMP-SMX DS daily); CD4 <100: toxoplasmosis prophylaxis (TMP-SMX DS also covers); CD4 <50: MAC prophylaxis (azithromycin weekly)

AMMI Canada/PHAC guidelines recommend OI prophylaxis based on CD4 count. Prophylaxis can be discontinued when CD4 consistently >200 (PCP), >100 (toxo), >100 (MAC) on ART. CMV retinitis occurs at CD4 <50 — ganciclovir.

**Q:** What is HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) and who should be offered it?

**A:** PrEP: daily oral tenofovir/emtricitabine (TDF/FTC or TAF/FTC) for HIV-negative individuals at high risk; reduces transmission by >99% if adherent

PHAC and CATIE recommend offering PrEP to HIV-negative individuals with ongoing high-risk sexual behavior (MSM with multiple partners, serodiscordant couples, people who inject drugs). On-demand PrEP (2-1-1 protocol) is an alternative for MSM. Baseline and 3-monthly HIV testing required.

**Q:** A patient on ART has a rising HIV viral load after being undetectable. What is the approach?

**A:** Confirm with repeat VL; assess adherence (most common cause); resistance testing; review drug interactions; switch to fully active regimen based on resistance profile

Treatment failure is defined as confirmed viral load >200 copies/mL on ART. AMMI guidelines recommend resistance testing before switching. Switch to regimen with ≥2 fully active drugs. Adherence counseling is crucial — 95% adherence required to suppress HIV.

**Q:** What AIDS-defining conditions occur at CD4 <50 cells/μL?

**A:** Disseminated MAC (Mycobacterium avium complex), CMV retinitis/disease, CNS lymphoma, disseminated histoplasmosis, disseminated coccidioidomycosis, Cryptosporidium

AMMI guidelines define AIDS as CD4 <200 or the presence of AIDS-defining conditions. CMV retinitis presents with visual floaters/blind spots — ganciclovir treatment. MAC presents with fever, night sweats, weight loss, diarrhea — azithromycin + ethambutol.

**Q:** How is HIV transmitted and what are the relative transmission risks?

**A:** Receptive anal intercourse (1.4%/act highest risk), insertive anal (0.11%), receptive vaginal (0.08%), needle sharing (0.63%); negligible: oral sex, intact skin, casual contact; NOT transmitted by saliva, tears, hugging

Understanding transmission guides prevention counseling. Viral load strongly influences transmission risk. Undetectable = untransmittable (U=U) when viral load suppressed on ART. Condoms reduce risk by ~80-85% for sexual transmission.

**Q:** A healthcare worker has a needlestick injury from an HIV-positive source patient (viral load 40,000). What should be done?

**A:** Immediate post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP): within 2 hours (no later than 72h) with 28 days of BIC/TAF/FTC or DTG + TDF/FTC; report incident; baseline HIV, HBV, HCV testing

PEP reduces HIV transmission by ~80%. Baseline and 4-6 week repeat HIV testing. Source patient hepatitis B status: give HBIG + vaccine if source HBsAg+ and worker not immune. AMMI/PHAC guidelines for occupational exposure.

**Q:** What are the immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome (IRIS) and when does it occur?

**A:** IRIS: paradoxical worsening or unmasking of opportunistic infections within weeks-months of starting ART as immune system recovers; risk highest when starting ART with low CD4 and concurrent OI

IRIS occurs in ~17% of patients starting ART with CD4 <100. Most common IRIS: TB-IRIS, MAC-IRIS, cryptococcal IRIS. Management: continue ART in most cases; NSAIDs or corticosteroids for severe symptoms. Delay ART if active OI being treated (except TB, CMV — start ART).

**Q:** What is the recommended HIV testing approach in Canada for screening?

**A:** 4th generation HIV Ag/Ab combination test for screening; reactive result confirmed with HIV differentiation assay; routine testing offered to all adults 15-65, all pregnant women, and targeted testing for high-risk groups

PHAC/CTFPHC recommend opt-out HIV testing in high-prevalence settings. HIV testing in pregnancy: routine universal testing at first prenatal visit. Reporting requirements: positive HIV test is reportable in all Canadian provinces/territories.

**Q:** A 45-year-old with HIV has a CD4 count of 50 and starts ART. What timing is recommended relative to treating concurrent cryptococcal meningitis?

**A:** Delay ART for 4-6 weeks after starting antifungal treatment for cryptococcal meningitis — early ART associated with increased mortality from IRIS

The COAT trial demonstrated that immediate ART with cryptococcal meningitis increases mortality versus deferred ART. Treat with amphotericin B + flucytosine induction for 2 weeks, then fluconazole consolidation before starting ART.

**Q:** What monitoring is recommended for patients on HIV ART?

**A:** At initiation: baseline CBC, BMP, LFTs, HBV/HCV, urinalysis, lipid panel, resistance testing, HLA-B*5701 (abacavir hypersensitivity); Follow-up: viral load q3 months until undetectable, then q6 months; CD4 when VL detectable; annual lipid, renal function

AMMI HIV guidelines outline monitoring schedule. HLA-B*5701 testing mandatory before prescribing abacavir to prevent potentially fatal hypersensitivity reaction. CD4 monitoring less essential once virally suppressed.

**Q:** What hepatitis co-infections are common in HIV and how do they affect management?

**A:** HIV/HBV co-infection (use TAF/FTC or TDF/FTC which treats both); HIV/HCV co-infection (treat HCV first with DAAs, then ART — check for drug interactions)

HBV co-infection requires HIV regimen containing tenofovir (treats both viruses). Never treat HBV with lamivudine or emtricitabine alone in HIV-positive patients (promotes HBV resistance). HCV DAAs are highly effective in HIV/HCV co-infection (similar SVR rates to HIV-negative).

**Q:** What antiretroviral drug class is associated with lipodystrophy, dyslipidemia, and insulin resistance?

**A:** Older protease inhibitors (lopinavir/ritonavir, indinavir) and older NRTIs (stavudine d4T, zidovudine AZT) cause metabolic complications — modern ART (INSTI, TAF-based) has much improved metabolic profile

HIV-associated lipodystrophy: peripheral lipoatrophy (fat wasting from face, limbs) and central lipohypertrophy (visceral fat, buffalo hump). Modern ART agents (TAF > TDF for renal/bone; INSTIs metabolically neutral). Cardiovascular risk monitoring important in HIV patients.

**Q:** When should ART be initiated?

**A:** Immediately, same day if possible, regardless of CD4 or viral load

Rapid ART improves outcomes. U=U. First-line: integrase inhibitor + 2 NRTIs.

**Q:** What OI prophylaxis at different CD4 counts?

**A:** <200: PJP (TMP-SMX). <100: toxo (TMP-SMX). <50: MAC (azithromycin). All: TB testing

Discontinue prophylaxis when CD4>200 for >=3 months on ART.

**Q:** What is the current recommended initial ART regimen for treatment-naive HIV?

**A:** Preferred: integrase inhibitor (INSTI)-based: bictegravir/emtricitabine/TAF (Biktarvy) or dolutegravir + emtricitabine/TAF (or TDF). Alternative: dolutegravir/lamivudine (Dovato) if HBV negative and VL <500,000.

INSTI-based regimens are preferred for potency, tolerability, and high barrier to resistance. Start ART on same day as diagnosis (rapid/same-day start) per current guidelines unless: opportunistic infection needing urgent treatment (cryptococcal meningitis, TB — start ART 2-8 weeks later to avoid IRIS).

**Q:** At what CD4 counts do specific opportunistic infections occur?

**A:** <500: Kaposi sarcoma, TB, bacterial pneumonia. <200: PCP (Pneumocystis), toxoplasmosis, cryptococcosis, histoplasmosis. <100: CMV retinitis, MAC (M. avium complex), cryptosporidiosis. <50: MAC disseminated, CMV.

PCP prophylaxis: TMP-SMX DS daily when CD4 <200 (or CD4% <14%). Toxo prophylaxis: TMP-SMX DS also covers toxo if IgG+. MAC prophylaxis: azithromycin 1200 mg weekly when CD4 <50 (less common now with effective ART). Discontinue prophylaxis when CD4 >200 for ≥3 months on ART.

**Q:** What is HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) and who should receive it?

**A:** PrEP: emtricitabine/TDF (Truvada) or emtricitabine/TAF (Descovy) daily, or cabotegravir LA (IM q2 months). Indicated for: MSM with inconsistent condom use, serodiscordant couples, PWID sharing equipment, individuals with recurrent STIs or frequent PEP use.

PrEP reduces HIV acquisition by >99% when taken consistently. Requires negative HIV test before starting and q3 months. Monitor renal function (TDF-associated nephrotoxicity) and screen for STIs q3 months. Canadian guideline endorses PrEP for all at substantial risk. Event-driven (2-1-1) PrEP validated for MSM only.

**Q:** What is the post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) protocol for HIV?

**A:** Start within 72h of exposure (ideally <2h). 28-day course: preferred regimen = dolutegravir + emtricitabine/TDF (or TAF). Baseline HIV test (must be negative), then repeat at 6 weeks and 3 months. Also test for HBV, HCV, STIs, pregnancy.

PEP indicated for: needlestick (hollow bore higher risk), sexual assault, unprotected sex with known/suspected HIV+ partner. Risk assessment guides decision: receptive anal sex highest risk (~1.4%), needle sharing (~0.63%), needlestick (~0.3%). Source viral load undetectable = negligible risk (U=U).

**Q:** What is immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome (IRIS)?

**A:** Paradoxical worsening or unmasking of opportunistic infections after ART initiation due to recovering immune system. Common with: TB (most common), cryptococcal meningitis, MAC, CMV, PML. Occurs within 4-8 weeks of ART start.

Risk factors: low baseline CD4 (<50), high baseline VL, early ART initiation with active OI. Management: continue ART (unless life-threatening), treat the OI aggressively, NSAIDs/steroids for severe inflammation. Delay ART 2-8 weeks for cryptococcal and TB meningitis to reduce IRIS risk.

**Q:** What is the U=U (Undetectable = Untransmittable) concept?

**A:** HIV+ individuals on ART with sustained viral load <200 copies/mL for ≥6 months have effectively zero risk of sexual transmission. Supported by PARTNER, PARTNER2, and HPTN 052 trials (zero linked transmissions).

U=U has transformed HIV prevention messaging and reduces stigma. Does not apply to vertical transmission (though risk is very low <1% with suppressive ART). Does not protect against other STIs. Viral load monitoring q3-6 months confirms ongoing suppression. Canadian consensus supports U=U counseling.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for patients on ART?

**A:** Baseline: CD4, VL, genotype resistance, HLA-B*5701 (if considering abacavir), HBV/HCV serology, CBC, Cr, lipids, urinalysis, STI screen, Pap, pregnancy test. Follow-up: VL at 4 weeks then q3-6 months (goal <40-50), CD4 q3-6 months until >350 then annually.

Virologic failure: VL >200 copies/mL on 2 occasions while on ART. Perform resistance testing while on failing regimen (need virus replicating). Switch to ≥2 active agents based on resistance testing. HLA-B*5701 positive: abacavir contraindicated (hypersensitivity reaction). Screen for metabolic syndrome, cardiovascular risk, bone density.

**Q:** What is PCP (Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia) presentation and treatment?

**A:** Subacute onset: progressive dyspnea, dry cough, fever, hypoxemia (especially exercise-induced desaturation). CXR: bilateral diffuse interstitial/ground-glass opacities. LDH elevated. Treatment: TMP-SMX (15-20 mg/kg/day TMP) × 21 days. Add prednisone if PaO2 <70 or A-a gradient >35.

PCP is the most common AIDS-defining illness. Diagnosis: induced sputum or BAL with silver stain/DFA. Beta-glucan elevated (sensitive). Corticosteroids (prednisone 40 mg BID × 5 days, taper over 21 days) reduce mortality in moderate-severe PCP. Pentamidine IV for TMP-SMX-intolerant. Secondary prophylaxis until CD4 >200 × 3 months.

**Q:** What is the approach to HIV-associated cryptococcal meningitis?

**A:** Induction: amphotericin B liposomal + flucytosine × 2 weeks. Consolidation: fluconazole 400 mg/day × 8 weeks. Maintenance: fluconazole 200 mg/day × ≥1 year (until CD4 >200 × 6 months). Delay ART 4-6 weeks (IRIS risk). Manage elevated ICP with serial LPs.

Cryptococcal meningitis presents with headache, fever, cranial nerve palsies. India ink or CSF CrAg (cryptococcal antigen) diagnostic. Opening pressure often elevated (>20 cmH2O)→serial therapeutic LPs essential (reduce pressure by 50% or to <20). Serum CrAg screening recommended when CD4 <100 before ART initiation.

**Q:** What cervical and anal cancer screening is recommended in HIV?

**A:** Cervical: Pap smear at diagnosis, repeat at 6 months, then annually if normal. HPV co-testing if ≥30. Anal cancer: digital anorectal exam annually for all HIV+; anal Pap (cytology) for MSM, those with anal HPV/condylomata, or cervical dysplasia history.

HIV+ individuals have increased risk of HPV-related cancers (cervical, anal, oropharyngeal). HPV vaccination recommended up to age 26 (or 45 per shared decision). HSIL on anal Pap→high-resolution anoscopy. Treatment of high-grade anal dysplasia reduces progression to cancer (ANCHOR trial).

**Q:** What are the ART drug class side effects?

**A:** NRTIs: TDF (nephrotoxicity, osteoporosis), TAF (weight gain, lipids), AZT (anemia, myopathy), abacavir (hypersensitivity if HLA-B*5701+). INSTIs: dolutegravir (insomnia, weight gain, neural tube defects controversy), raltegravir (myopathy). PIs: metabolic syndrome, GI upset, drug interactions. NNRTIs: efavirenz (CNS, teratogenic), nevirapine (hepatotoxicity, rash).

TAF has less renal and bone toxicity than TDF but more weight gain and lipid effects. INSTI-associated weight gain is a growing concern (dolutegravir, bictegravir > raltegravir). PI/r interactions via CYP3A4 are extensive — always check drug interactions. Cobicistat is a pharmacokinetic booster (CYP3A4 inhibitor) without antiretroviral activity.

**Q:** What is the approach to HIV in pregnancy?

**A:** ART for all pregnant women regardless of CD4/VL (treat+prevent transmission). Preferred: dolutegravir + emtricitabine/TAF (after 1st trimester) or atazanavir/r-based. VL near delivery guides mode: VL >1000→scheduled C-section at 38 weeks + IV zidovudine. VL <1000→vaginal delivery acceptable. Infant: zidovudine ×4-6 weeks (add nevirapine+lamivudine if high risk).

Without treatment: 25-30% vertical transmission. With ART + VL <50: <1% transmission. Breastfeeding: in Canadian guidelines, formula feeding recommended (though in resource-limited settings, breastfeeding with ART is acceptable). Resistance testing at entry. Avoid efavirenz (teratogenic), dolutegravir now considered safe in pregnancy.

**Q:** What is the HIV diagnostic algorithm in Canada?

**A:** 4th generation Ag/Ab combo test (detects p24 antigen + anti-HIV-1/2 antibodies) → if reactive → confirmatory: HIV-1/2 differentiation immunoassay → if indeterminate → NAT (nucleic acid test/RNA PCR). Window period: 4th gen 2-4 weeks. Point-of-care rapid tests also available.

4th gen testing has nearly eliminated the window period problem. Acute HIV: suspect if mononucleosis-like illness with risk factors — order RNA PCR (Ag/Ab may be negative in first 1-2 weeks). All positive results require confirmatory testing before disclosure. Opt-out HIV screening recommended in Canadian prenatal care and high-prevalence settings.

**Q:** What vaccines are recommended and contraindicated in HIV?

**A:** Recommended: influenza annually, PCV20 (or PCV15+PPSV23), HBV (high-dose if CD4<200 or non-responder), HPV (up to age 45), COVID-19, Tdap, meningococcal, HAV. CONTRAINDICATED: live vaccines if CD4 <200 (BCG, live attenuated influenza, yellow fever, oral polio, varicella/MMR — but MMR/varicella may be given if CD4 >200).

Live vaccines generally avoided in immunocompromised HIV. MMR can be given if CD4 >200 cells/μL. Shingrix (recombinant, not live) is safe and recommended ≥18 in HIV. HBV vaccine response is impaired at low CD4 — consider double-dose series. Anti-HBs titers should be checked post-vaccination.

**Q:** What are the long-acting injectable ART options?

**A:** Cabotegravir + rilpivirine (Cabenuva): IM injection every 2 months (after oral lead-in). For virologically suppressed patients (VL <50) with no NNRTI/INSTI resistance. Also: lenacapavir SC every 6 months (for multidrug-resistant HIV).

Long-acting ART improves adherence and reduces pill burden. Cabotegravir LA also available as PrEP (IM q2 months — superior to oral TDF/FTC in HPTN 083/084 trials). Lenacapavir is a first-in-class capsid inhibitor for heavily treatment-experienced patients with resistance to other classes.

**Q:** A patient with HIV develops purple plaques on the legs and mouth. What diagnosis should be suspected and how is it confirmed?

**A:** Kaposi sarcoma (AIDS-related type). Confirmed by skin biopsy and PCR for HHV-8 DNA sequences.

AIDS-related KS is an AIDS-defining illness occurring with severe immunodeficiency (CD4 <200). Antiretroviral therapy is the cornerstone of treatment; systemic chemotherapy or radiation is added for disseminated disease.

**Q:** What are infectious causes of odynophagia, and which populations are most at risk?

**A:** Infectious odynophagia: Candida, HSV, CMV. These are most common in immunosuppressed patients (HIV/AIDS, transplant recipients, chemotherapy patients).

Odynophagia (painful swallowing) in immunosuppressed patients should prompt consideration of infectious esophagitis. Candida esophagitis is most common and responds to fluconazole. HSV and CMV esophagitis require antiviral therapy (acyclovir/ganciclovir respectively). Drug causes (tetracycline, bisphosphonates) should also be considered.

## Sepsis & Septic Shock

**Q:** A 68-year-old has altered consciousness, fever 39.5°C, BP 88/52, HR 124, RR 28, lactate 3.2 mmol/L. What is the diagnosis and the Surviving Sepsis 1-hour bundle?

**A:** Septic shock (sepsis + vasopressor requirement OR lactate ≥2 mmol/L); 1-hour bundle: blood cultures, IV broad-spectrum antibiotics, 30 mL/kg IV crystalloid, vasopressors for MAP <65 if no response

Sepsis-3 definition: life-threatening organ dysfunction from infection. SOFA score ≥2 acute change. Quick-SOFA (altered mental status, RR ≥22, SBP ≤100) identifies high-risk patients outside ICU. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021: 1-hour bundle.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for sepsis vs. septic shock per Sepsis-3?

**A:** Sepsis: suspected infection + acute SOFA score ≥2; Septic shock: sepsis + vasopressor requirement to maintain MAP ≥65 AND serum lactate >2 mmol/L despite adequate fluid resuscitation

Sepsis-3 eliminated SIRS criteria for sepsis definition. SOFA (Sequential Organ Failure Assessment) assesses respiratory, coagulation, liver, cardiovascular, CNS, renal. Previous definitions (severe sepsis) are no longer used.

**Q:** What is the first-line vasopressor in septic shock and what is the MAP target?

**A:** Norepinephrine is first-line vasopressor; target MAP ≥65 mmHg — higher targets (80-85) may benefit patients with chronic hypertension

Surviving Sepsis guidelines recommend norepinephrine as first-line due to lower arrhythmia risk vs. dopamine (SOAP II trial). Vasopressin (0.03 units/min) added as second agent to reduce norepinephrine dose or for refractory shock. Epinephrine as third-line. Dopamine only if low tachyarrhythmia risk.

**Q:** How should blood cultures be obtained in sepsis and when should antibiotics be given?

**A:** 2-3 sets of blood cultures (aerobic + anaerobic) from 2 different sites before antibiotics; antibiotics within 1 hour for septic shock, 3 hours for sepsis without shock

Surviving Sepsis 2021: delay antibiotics to get cultures should not exceed 45 minutes. Each hour delay in antibiotics in septic shock increases mortality ~7%. Give broad-spectrum empiric coverage, then narrow based on cultures and sensitivities (antibiotic stewardship).

**Q:** What IV fluid is recommended in early sepsis resuscitation?

**A:** 30 mL/kg balanced crystalloid (Lactated Ringer preferred, or NS); SMART trial showed LR reduces mortality and AKI compared to normal saline

Surviving Sepsis recommends 30 mL/kg IV crystalloid in first 3 hours. Albumin may be added if large volumes of crystalloid required. Reassess after each fluid bolus using dynamic measures (passive leg raise, pulse pressure variation). Avoid fluid overload — associated with worse outcomes.

**Q:** When are corticosteroids indicated in septic shock?

**A:** IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/day (50mg q6h or 200mg continuous infusion) in septic shock refractory to adequate fluids and vasopressors (norepinephrine >0.25 μg/kg/min)

APROCCHSS trial showed hydrocortisone + fludrocortisone reduces mortality in severe septic shock. Surviving Sepsis recommends IV hydrocortisone for vasopressor-refractory septic shock. ACTH stimulation test is NOT required before giving steroids.

**Q:** What empiric antibiotic coverage is recommended for community-acquired sepsis of unknown source?

**A:** Broad-spectrum coverage: piperacillin-tazobactam or a carbapenem (meropenem/imipenem) + coverage for community MRSA (vancomycin) if risk factors (IV drug use, prior MRSA, healthcare contact)

Antibiotic choice depends on suspected source, local resistance patterns, and patient risk factors. De-escalation after 24-48h based on culture results. Duration should be as short as effective (7-10 days for most community sepsis). Source control (drain abscesses, remove infected lines) is essential.

**Q:** What is source control in sepsis and what are examples?

**A:** Rapid intervention to control the infectious source: drain abscess (percutaneous or surgical), remove infected IV line/catheter, debride infected tissue, restore bowel continuity for perforated viscus

Surviving Sepsis guidelines recommend source control within 6-12 hours. Infected central venous catheters should be removed as soon as possible. ECMO or surgical intervention for severe necrotizing fasciitis or gas gangrene. Antibiotic therapy alone is insufficient when a nidus of infection remains.

**Q:** What is DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation) in the context of sepsis?

**A:** DIC: systemic activation of coagulation cascade leading to microthrombi and consumption coagulopathy; labs: low platelets, prolonged PT/INR, low fibrinogen, elevated D-dimer; treat underlying sepsis

DIC complicates severe sepsis, trauma, and obstetric emergencies. Paradoxically causes both thrombosis (organ failure) and bleeding (factor consumption). Treat underlying cause. Replace blood products for active bleeding: platelets (PLT <50), FFP, cryoprecipitate (low fibrinogen <1.5 g/L).

**Q:** What is the Surviving Sepsis Bundle for mechanical ventilation in septic shock?

**A:** Lung-protective ventilation: tidal volume 6 mL/kg ideal body weight, plateau pressure <30 cmH2O, PEEP titration, permissive hypercapnia; prone positioning if PaO2/FiO2 <150

ARDSNET and PROSEVA trials support low-tidal volume ventilation and prone positioning in ARDS (common in severe sepsis). Avoid high plateau pressures — cause ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI). Target SpO2 94-98%.

**Q:** What is procalcitonin (PCT) and how is it used in sepsis management?

**A:** PCT rises with bacterial infections (but not viral); elevated PCT supports bacterial sepsis diagnosis; serial PCT used to guide antibiotic discontinuation (PCT-guided de-escalation reduces antibiotic exposure)

PRORATA trial showed PCT-guided antibiotic discontinuation reduces antibiotic days without worsening outcomes. PCT is most useful for guiding antibiotic duration (stop when PCT falls to 80% of peak or <0.5 ng/mL). Less useful for diagnosis as it can be elevated in non-infectious conditions.

**Q:** What glycemic target is recommended in ICU patients with sepsis?

**A:** Maintain blood glucose 7.8-10 mmol/L with insulin infusion — avoid both hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia

NICE-SUGAR trial showed moderate glucose control (≤10 mmol/L) reduces mortality compared to tight control (≤6.1 mmol/L), which increased hypoglycemia. Intensive insulin therapy associated with increased severe hypoglycemia events. Surviving Sepsis recommends insulin when glucose >10 mmol/L x2.

**Q:** What is lactate-guided resuscitation in sepsis?

**A:** Measure serum lactate at presentation; if ≥4 mmol/L indicates severe hypoperfusion — resuscitate to achieve lactate clearance ≥10-20% per 2 hours; repeat lactate 2h after initial resuscitation

LACTATE trial showed early lactate measurement and lactate-guided resuscitation reduces mortality. Lactate ≥2 mmol/L = criterion for septic shock. Persistently elevated lactate despite resuscitation suggests occult hypoperfusion.

**Q:** What is the FAST-HUG mnemonic for daily ICU care in sepsis patients?

**A:** Feeding (enteral nutrition), Analgesia, Sedation (minimizing), Thromboprophylaxis, Head-of-bed elevation (30-45°), Ulcer prophylaxis (stress ulcer), Glucose control

Surviving Sepsis guidelines recommend light sedation (target RASS 0 to -1), early enteral nutrition, DVT prophylaxis (LMWH preferred), HOB elevation to prevent VAP, stress ulcer prophylaxis with PPI (if high risk), glycemic control.

**Q:** What is the qSOFA score and how is it used?

**A:** qSOFA: RR ≥22/min (1), altered mentation (1), SBP ≤100 mmHg (1); score ≥2 identifies patients with suspected infection at risk of poor outcomes — use outside ICU to identify high-risk patients

qSOFA is a bedside screening tool that does not require labs. Positive qSOFA (≥2) should prompt full SOFA assessment and consideration of ICU-level care. Not as sensitive as SIRS for identifying all sepsis, but more specific for poor outcomes.

**Q:** What are Sepsis-3 criteria?

**A:** Suspected infection + SOFA increase >=2. Septic shock: vasopressors for MAP>=65 + lactate>2 despite fluids

qSOFA for screening: RR>=22, altered mentation, SBP<=100. Hour-1 bundle critical.

**Q:** What is the Sepsis-3 definition and how is septic shock defined?

**A:** Sepsis: life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by dysregulated host response to infection. Operationally: suspected infection + SOFA score increase ≥2. Septic shock: sepsis + vasopressors to maintain MAP ≥65 + lactate >2 mmol/L despite adequate fluid resuscitation.

qSOFA (bedside screen): RR ≥22, altered mentation, SBP ≤100 — ≥2 suggests possible sepsis (not diagnostic, prompts further assessment). SIRS criteria no longer define sepsis in Sepsis-3 but remain useful for screening. Septic shock mortality: 40-50%.

**Q:** What is the Surviving Sepsis Campaign Hour-1 bundle?

**A:** Within 1 hour: 1) Measure lactate (remeasure if >2). 2) Blood cultures before antibiotics (do NOT delay ABx for cultures). 3) Broad-spectrum antibiotics. 4) Crystalloid 30 mL/kg for hypotension or lactate ≥4. 5) Vasopressors if hypotension persists during/after fluid resuscitation (target MAP ≥65).

SSC 2021 update emphasizes time-zero = triage time, not ICU admission. Each hour delay in antibiotics increases mortality ~4-8%. Balanced crystalloids (LR, Plasmalyte) preferred over NS. Norepinephrine is first-line vasopressor. Do not delay antibiotics to obtain cultures if cultures cannot be drawn promptly.

**Q:** What is the vasopressor hierarchy in septic shock?

**A:** First-line: norepinephrine (target MAP ≥65). Second-line: add vasopressin 0.03 U/min (allows NE dose reduction). Third-line: add epinephrine. Inotrope: dobutamine if cardiac dysfunction (low CO despite adequate filling). Avoid dopamine (more arrhythmias).

SSC 2021 recommends norepinephrine over dopamine. Vasopressin added at NE 0.25-0.5 μg/kg/min to reduce NE requirements (not as rescue). Angiotensin II (Giapreza) for refractory shock. Phenylephrine only for tachyarrhythmia limiting NE use. Monitor with arterial line, consider echocardiography for hemodynamic assessment.

**Q:** When should corticosteroids be used in septic shock?

**A:** IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/day (50 mg q6h) if shock is refractory to fluids + vasopressors (NE ≥0.25 μg/kg/min for ≥4 hours). Do NOT use random cortisol or cosyntropin test to guide decision. Taper when vasopressors discontinued.

SSC 2021 suggests corticosteroids for refractory shock. ADRENAL and APROCCHSS trials showed faster shock resolution and possible mortality benefit. Fludrocortisone optional (APROCCHSS used it). Stress-dose steroids should NOT be given to all sepsis patients — only vasopressor-dependent shock.

**Q:** What empiric antibiotic strategies are used for common sepsis sources?

**A:** Unknown source: pip-tazo or meropenem ± vancomycin. Urinary: ceftriaxone or pip-tazo. Pulmonary (CAP): ceftriaxone + azithromycin. Intra-abdominal: pip-tazo or meropenem (covers anaerobes). Skin/soft tissue: cefazolin ± clindamycin; necrotizing fasciitis: meropenem + vancomycin + clindamycin.

De-escalate antibiotics based on culture results within 48-72h. SSC recommends daily reassessment of antibiotic need. Procalcitonin-guided discontinuation can safely reduce antibiotic duration. Duration: 7-8 days for most infections (shorter courses non-inferior). MRSA coverage if risk factors present. Antifungal if candidiasis risk (TPN, broad-spectrum ABx, surgery).

**Q:** What is the role of lactate in sepsis management?

**A:** Initial lactate guides resuscitation intensity (≥4 mmol/L = aggressive fluids + vasopressors). Serial lactate monitoring: target clearance ≥20% every 2 hours. Persistent elevation despite treatment = poor prognosis. Normal lactate with infection does NOT exclude sepsis.

Lactate in sepsis reflects tissue hypoperfusion and anaerobic metabolism, but also accelerated aerobic glycolysis (stress response). Other causes of elevated lactate: liver failure, seizures, medications (metformin, epinephrine), thiamine deficiency. Lactate clearance is a dynamic resuscitation target alongside MAP, UO, and clinical assessment.

**Q:** What fluid resuscitation strategy is recommended in sepsis?

**A:** Initial bolus: 30 mL/kg crystalloid within first 3 hours for hypotension or lactate ≥4. Balanced crystalloids (LR, Plasmalyte) preferred over NS. After initial resuscitation: use dynamic measures of fluid responsiveness (PLR, pulse pressure variation) to guide further fluids. Avoid fluid overload.

SMART trial showed balanced crystalloids may reduce AKI and death vs NS. Albumin can be used as adjunct if large volumes required. SSC 2021 discourages hydroxyethyl starch (HES) — increases AKI and mortality. After initial resuscitation, liberal fluids are harmful — assess fluid responsiveness before each bolus.

**Q:** What is the SOFA score and how is it calculated?

**A:** Sequential Organ Failure Assessment: 6 organ systems, each 0-4 points. Respiration (PaO2/FiO2), Coagulation (platelets), Liver (bilirubin), Cardiovascular (MAP/vasopressors), CNS (GCS), Renal (Cr/UO). Score ≥2 increase from baseline = sepsis. Higher scores = higher mortality.

SOFA ≥2 has ~10% mortality risk. Used both for sepsis diagnosis (Sepsis-3) and for tracking organ dysfunction over time in ICU. Baseline SOFA assumed 0 if no pre-existing organ dysfunction. Cannot be calculated at bedside quickly → qSOFA used for bedside screening.

**Q:** What blood glucose target is recommended in sepsis?

**A:** Target glucose <10 mmol/L (180 mg/dL). Avoid tight glucose control (<6.1/110 — increases hypoglycemia without mortality benefit per NICE-SUGAR trial). Use insulin infusion protocol. Monitor q1-2h.

NICE-SUGAR trial (2009) ended the intensive insulin therapy era in ICU. Hypoglycemia (<3.9) independently increases mortality. Stress hyperglycemia is common in sepsis (counter-regulatory hormones). Nutrition should be initiated early (enteral preferred) alongside glucose management.

**Q:** What is the transfusion threshold in sepsis?

**A:** Restrictive: Hb <70 g/L for most sepsis patients. Exception: active hemorrhage, acute myocardial ischemia, or severe hypoxemia may warrant higher threshold (Hb <90-100). Avoid routine platelet transfusion unless <10,000 or <20,000 with active bleeding.

TRICC and TRISS trials showed no benefit of liberal (Hb <100) vs restrictive (Hb <70) transfusion in critically ill/sepsis. Restrictive approach reduces transfusion-related complications (TACO, TRALI, infection). FFP: only for active bleeding or invasive procedures with coagulopathy, not for lab correction alone.

**Q:** What is the source control concept in sepsis?

**A:** Identify and control the infection source within 6-12 hours: drain abscesses, debride necrotic tissue, remove infected devices/catheters, repair perforated viscus. Source control is ESSENTIAL — antibiotics alone may be insufficient without it.

Common sources requiring intervention: intra-abdominal abscess (percutaneous drainage), cholangitis (ERCP), necrotizing fasciitis (emergent surgical debridement), infected central line (remove catheter), empyema (chest tube), urinary obstruction (catheterize/stent). Delayed source control independently increases mortality.

**Q:** What VTE prophylaxis is recommended in sepsis?

**A:** LMWH (enoxaparin 40 mg SC daily) preferred over UFH for VTE prophylaxis in all critically ill sepsis patients unless contraindicated (active bleeding, platelets <50,000, severe coagulopathy). Mechanical prophylaxis (compression devices) if anticoagulation contraindicated.

SSC 2021 recommends pharmacologic VTE prophylaxis for all sepsis/septic shock patients. LMWH has lower DVT rates and possibly lower PE rates vs UFH. Combine with mechanical prophylaxis when possible. Do not use pharmacologic prophylaxis if active DIC with major bleeding. Monitor for HIT.

**Q:** What is the role of procalcitonin in sepsis management?

**A:** PCT >0.5 μg/L supports bacterial infection (not specific). Serial monitoring: PCT decline guides antibiotic de-escalation/discontinuation — stop antibiotics when PCT <0.5 or ≥80% decline from peak. NOT useful for initial antibiotic decision (treat first, use PCT to guide duration).

PCT is more specific for bacterial infection than CRP or WBC. Rises within 4-6h, peaks at 24h. NOT elevated in viral infections (usually). Elevated in: bacterial sepsis, surgery, trauma, burns, cardiogenic shock. SSC 2021 suggests using PCT + clinical assessment to guide antibiotic duration. Can safely reduce duration by 2-3 days.

**Q:** What are the major pathophysiologic mechanisms of septic shock?

**A:** Infection→PAMPs activate innate immunity (TLR)→pro-inflammatory cascade (TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6)→endothelial dysfunction→vasodilation (distributive shock)+capillary leak→relative hypovolemia+tissue edema. Coagulation activation→DIC. Mitochondrial dysfunction→cytopathic hypoxia.

Septic shock is distributive: low SVR, high CO initially (warm shock), progressing to cold shock (myocardial depression, low CO). Microcirculatory dysfunction explains why tissue hypoxia persists despite normalized macro-hemodynamics. Multi-organ dysfunction results from combination of hypoperfusion, cytokine storm, and microvascular thrombosis.

**Q:** What is the approach to antibiotic de-escalation in sepsis?

**A:** Review cultures at 48-72h: narrow to targeted therapy based on organism and susceptibilities. Discontinue empiric MRSA/pseudomonal coverage if not isolated. Use PCT to guide duration. Most infections: 7-8 days sufficient (some exceptions: endocarditis, osteomyelitis, S. aureus bacteremia ≥4 weeks).

Antibiotic stewardship in sepsis reduces resistance emergence, C. difficile, and adverse effects without increasing mortality. SSC 2021 recommends daily reassessment. Infectious disease consultation for complex infections (S. aureus bacteremia, candidemia) improves outcomes. Blood cultures should be repeated 48-72h for documented bacteremia to confirm clearance.

**Q:** What is the classic definition of fever of unknown origin (FUO), and what are the three main diagnostic categories?

**A:** Classic FUO (Petersdorf & Beeson criteria): fever >38.3°C on several occasions, lasting >3 weeks, with no diagnosis after 1 week of inpatient investigation (or 3 outpatient visits). Three main categories: Infections (25-30%, e.g., TB, endocarditis, abscess), Malignancy (20-25%, e.g., lymphoma, RCC, leukemia), Autoimmune/inflammatory (15-20%, e.g., adult Still's disease, SLE, vasculitis).

Despite extensive workup, 10-20% of FUO cases remain undiagnosed, and these generally have a favorable prognosis. The relative proportion of causes has shifted over time, with fewer infections (due to better diagnostics) and more autoimmune causes being identified. Drug fever is an important and often overlooked cause.

**Q:** What is the systematic workup approach for a patient meeting criteria for fever of unknown origin?

**A:** Tier 1: Detailed history (travel, exposures, medications, family history), CBC with differential, blood cultures (x3 from different sites), ESR/CRP, LDH, urinalysis + culture, CXR, liver enzymes, ANA, RF. Tier 2: CT chest/abdomen/pelvis, echocardiography (endocarditis), peripheral blood smear. Tier 3: PET-CT (localizing occult infection/malignancy), temporal artery biopsy (>50 yo), bone marrow biopsy, liver biopsy.

The FUO workup should be systematic and guided by clinical clues from the history and physical exam. PET-CT has emerged as a valuable tool, especially for identifying occult abscesses, vasculitis, and lymphoma. Empiric antibiotics should be avoided unless the patient is deteriorating, as they may mask the diagnosis.

**Q:** A 45-year-old IV drug user presents with FUO, new cardiac murmur, splinter hemorrhages, and Janeway lesions. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Infective endocarditis. Diagnosis is based on the modified Duke criteria. Major criteria: positive blood cultures (typical organisms in 2 separate cultures) and evidence of endocardial involvement on echocardiography (vegetation, abscess, new valvular regurgitation). TEE is more sensitive than TTE for detecting vegetations.

Endocarditis is one of the classic infectious causes of FUO. The peripheral manifestations (Janeway lesions, Osler nodes, splinter hemorrhages, Roth spots) result from septic emboli and immune complex deposition. At least 3 sets of blood cultures from different sites should be drawn before starting antibiotics.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of sepsis and organ dysfunction?

**A:** Dysregulated host response: PAMPs activate innate immunity (TLRs, cytokines TNF/IL-1/IL-6), causing endothelial dysfunction, microvascular thrombosis, vasodilation, capillary leak, and mitochondrial dysfunction

Organ failure results from tissue hypoperfusion and cellular metabolic crisis — not just hypotension. Coagulopathy (DIC), ARDS, AKI, hepatic dysfunction, and encephalopathy are common organ failures.

**Q:** What is the approach to fever in a returning traveller?

**A:** Always rule out malaria first (thick/thin smear x3). DDx by incubation: <2wk (dengue, chikungunya, typhoid, malaria). 2-6wk (malaria, TB, hepatitis, HIV seroconversion). >6wk (TB, HIV, visceral leishmaniasis)

Key history: destination, activities, prophylaxis, sexual exposure, animal/insect contact. Dengue: thrombocytopenia, rash. Typhoid: relative bradycardia, rose spots. Malaria: cyclic fevers, P. falciparum can be fatal.

**Q:** What malaria prophylaxis options are available?

**A:** Atovaquone-proguanil (Malarone): start 1-2d before, daily, 7d after. Doxycycline: start 1-2d before, daily, 28d after. Mefloquine: start 2wk before, weekly, 4wk after. Chloroquine: only for P. vivax/sensitive areas

Choice depends on destination resistance patterns, duration, side effects, cost. Mefloquine: neuropsychiatric side effects. Doxycycline: photosensitivity. All travellers: mosquito bite prevention (DEET, permethrin-treated nets).

**Q:** What vaccines and prophylaxis should be discussed at a pre-travel consultation for a Canadian patient travelling to sub-Saharan Africa?

**A:** Routine vaccines: ensure up-to-date (Tdap, MMR, varicella, influenza, COVID-19, polio). Travel-specific: hepatitis A, hepatitis B (or Twinrix), typhoid (oral Ty21a or injectable Vi polysaccharide), yellow fever (required for entry to many African countries), meningococcal ACWY. Malaria prophylaxis: atovaquone-proguanil (Malarone — start 1-2 days before, daily, continue 7 days after), OR doxycycline (start 1-2 days before, daily, continue 4 weeks after), OR mefloquine (start 2 weeks before, weekly, continue 4 weeks after). Also: traveller's diarrhea counseling, insect bite prevention (DEET ≥20%), travel insurance.

CATMAT (Committee to Advise on Tropical Medicine and Travel) provides Canada-specific travel medicine guidelines. Yellow fever vaccine is a live vaccine — contraindicated in immunocompromised patients. Malarone is preferred for short trips due to fewer side effects and shorter post-travel course. Mefloquine is contraindicated in psychiatric illness and seizure disorders.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of traveller's diarrhea and how should it be managed?

**A:** Most common cause: enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC). Other: Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, norovirus. Management: Mild (tolerable, not distressing): oral rehydration, loperamide as needed. Moderate (distressing but not incapacitating): azithromycin 1000 mg single dose OR 500 mg daily x 3 days (preferred in South/Southeast Asia due to fluoroquinolone-resistant Campylobacter). Severe (incapacitating/bloody/febrile): azithromycin + seek medical care. Prevention: boil it, cook it, peel it, or forget it; bismuth subsalicylate prophylaxis for high-risk travellers.

CATMAT recommends azithromycin as first-line for self-treatment of traveller's diarrhea, replacing fluoroquinolones due to increasing resistance. Loperamide should be avoided in bloody/febrile diarrhea (risk of toxic megacolon with invasive pathogens). Most traveller's diarrhea is self-limited within 3-5 days.

**Q:** How do you diagnose malaria and what distinguishes P. falciparum from P. vivax?

**A:** Diagnosis: thick smear (sensitive — detects parasites), thin smear (specific — identifies species and quantifies parasitemia), rapid diagnostic test (RDT — detects antigens, useful when microscopy unavailable). P. falciparum: most lethal, no hypnozoites, can infect RBCs of all ages (high parasitemia), causes cerebral malaria/severe disease, banana-shaped gametocytes, chloroquine-resistant in most areas. P. vivax: causes relapsing malaria (hypnozoites in liver), infects only reticulocytes (lower parasitemia), rarely fatal, requires primaquine for radical cure (check G6PD first), chloroquine-sensitive in most areas.

Any febrile patient returning from a malaria-endemic area must have malaria ruled out — it is a medical emergency. P. falciparum can progress to severe malaria within hours. In Canada, malaria is a reportable disease. CATMAT recommends artemisinin-based combination therapy (ACT) for P. falciparum and chloroquine + primaquine for P. vivax.

**Q:** What is the treatment of malaria for P. falciparum vs P. vivax?

**A:** P. falciparum (uncomplicated): artemisinin-based combination therapy (ACT) — artemether-lumefantrine (Coartem) or atovaquone-proguanil (Malarone). Severe P. falciparum (parasitemia >2%, cerebral malaria, organ failure): IV artesunate (first-line), then oral ACT when able to take PO. P. vivax/ovale: chloroquine (still sensitive in most regions) PLUS primaquine 14-day course for radical cure (eliminates liver hypnozoites). Must check G6PD before primaquine — causes hemolytic anemia in G6PD-deficient patients. Alternative: tafenoquine (single dose, also requires G6PD testing).

IV artesunate replaced IV quinine as first-line for severe malaria per WHO and CATMAT guidelines — faster parasite clearance and lower mortality. Artesunate is available in Canada through the Canadian Malaria Network (special access). Primaquine without G6PD testing is a common exam trap.

**Q:** A traveller returns from Southeast Asia with high fever, severe headache, retro-orbital pain, myalgia, and a maculopapular rash. Platelets are 85. What is the diagnosis and what are the warning signs for severe disease?

**A:** Dengue fever. Warning signs for severe dengue (occurring around defervescence, days 3-7): abdominal pain/tenderness, persistent vomiting, clinical fluid accumulation (ascites, pleural effusion), mucosal bleeding, lethargy/restlessness, hepatomegaly >2 cm, rising hematocrit with rapid decline in platelets. Severe dengue: severe plasma leakage (shock, respiratory distress), severe bleeding, severe organ impairment (liver AST/ALT >1000, CNS, cardiac). Management: supportive — aggressive IV fluid resuscitation for shock, NO aspirin/NSAIDs (bleeding risk), monitor hematocrit and platelets closely.

Dengue is caused by a flavivirus transmitted by Aedes aegypti mosquitoes. There are 4 serotypes — secondary infection with a different serotype increases risk of severe dengue via antibody-dependent enhancement. There is no specific antiviral treatment. Dengue is an increasingly common diagnosis in Canadian travellers returning from tropical regions.

**Q:** How do you diagnose and treat Giardia lamblia infection?

**A:** Presentation: foul-smelling, fatty, watery diarrhea (steatorrhea), bloating, flatulence, abdominal cramps, nausea; can cause chronic diarrhea and malabsorption. Acquired from contaminated water (campers/travellers). Diagnosis: stool ova and parasites (O&P) x3 (cysts shed intermittently), stool Giardia antigen (more sensitive), stool PCR. Treatment: metronidazole 250 mg TID x 5-7 days (first-line in Canada) or tinidazole 2g single dose (higher cure rate, better tolerated). Alternative: nitazoxanide.

Giardia is the most common parasitic cause of diarrhea in Canada — found in contaminated lakes/streams (beaver fever). Cysts survive chlorination. Stool O&P has sensitivity ~50-70% for a single sample; send 3 samples on different days. Giardia antigen testing has >90% sensitivity and is preferred for diagnosis.

**Q:** What parasites should be considered in a returning traveller and how are Entamoeba histolytica, hookworm, and Strongyloides diagnosed and treated?

**A:** Entamoeba histolytica: bloody diarrhea (amoebic dysentery), liver abscess; Dx: stool antigen, serology for abscess, O&P (must distinguish from non-pathogenic E. dispar); Tx: metronidazole (tissue) THEN paromomycin (luminal cyst eradication). Hookworm (Ancylostoma/Necator): iron-deficiency anemia, eosinophilia; Dx: stool O&P (eggs); Tx: albendazole 400 mg x1. Strongyloides stercoralis: eosinophilia, larva currens (serpiginous urticaria), can cause hyperinfection syndrome in immunosuppressed (fatal dissemination); Dx: stool O&P (larvae, not eggs), serology; Tx: ivermectin 200 μg/kg x 2 days.

Strongyloides screening (serology) is mandatory before immunosuppression (e.g., corticosteroids, chemotherapy) in anyone from endemic areas — hyperinfection syndrome has >70% mortality. CATMAT recommends screening refugees and immigrants from tropical regions for Strongyloides. Amoebic liver abscess is treated with metronidazole — aspiration only if large or failing medical therapy.

**Q:** A 35-year-old returns from India with 2 weeks of fever, and blood cultures grow Salmonella typhi. What is the diagnosis, management, and public health significance?

**A:** Typhoid (enteric) fever. Presentation: stepwise rising fever, relative bradycardia, rose spots (salmon-colored macules on trunk), hepatosplenomegaly, constipation (more common than diarrhea), leukopenia. Dx: blood culture (gold standard — positive in 40-80%), bone marrow culture (most sensitive — 80-95%). Tx: azithromycin (first-line for uncomplicated, due to fluoroquinolone resistance in South Asia) or ceftriaxone for severe/complicated disease. Complications: intestinal perforation, GI hemorrhage, encephalopathy. Public health: reportable disease in Canada; household contacts require screening.

Typhoid vaccine (injectable Vi polysaccharide or oral Ty21a) is only 50-80% effective — travelers should still practice food/water precautions. Fluoroquinolone resistance is widespread in South/Southeast Asia, making azithromycin first-line per CATMAT. Chronic carriers (e.g., gallbladder) can be a source of outbreaks.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis and workup for fever in a returning traveller?

**A:** Differential by incubation: Short (<2 wk): malaria, dengue, Zika, chikungunya, traveller's diarrhea, typhoid, rickettsia. Medium (2-6 wk): malaria (P. vivax can be delayed), typhoid, hepatitis A/E, acute HIV, leptospirosis, acute schistosomiasis (Katayama fever). Long (>6 wk): malaria (P. vivax/ovale), TB, visceral leishmaniasis, amoebic liver abscess, brucellosis. Workup: malaria smears x3 (STAT — cannot wait), CBC with differential, blood cultures x2, LFTs, urinalysis, CXR, stool O&P if diarrhea, dengue/Zika serology if epidemiologically appropriate, HIV test.

The #1 rule: always rule out malaria first in any febrile returning traveller from an endemic area — it can kill within 24-48 hours. Three negative smears 12-24 hours apart are needed to exclude malaria. In Canada, Tropical Disease Units (Toronto, Montreal, Ottawa, Vancouver) provide expert consultation for complex cases.

**Q:** What are the malaria prophylaxis options and their key side effects and contraindications?

**A:** Atovaquone-proguanil (Malarone): start 1-2 days before, daily during, 7 days after travel. SE: GI upset, headache. CI: severe renal impairment (CrCl <30), pregnancy. Preferred for short trips. Doxycycline: start 1-2 days before, daily during, 28 days after. SE: photosensitivity, esophagitis, vaginal candidiasis. CI: pregnancy, children <8 years. Cheapest option. Mefloquine: start 2-3 weeks before (to test tolerance), weekly during, 4 weeks after. SE: neuropsychiatric (vivid dreams, anxiety, psychosis — rare), dizziness. CI: psychiatric history, seizure disorder, cardiac conduction abnormalities. Best for long trips (weekly dosing).

CATMAT recommends starting mefloquine 2-3 weeks before travel to identify neuropsychiatric side effects before departure. Chloroquine is only used for travel to the few remaining chloroquine-sensitive areas (Central America, Caribbean). No prophylaxis is 100% effective — insect bite prevention (DEET, permethrin-treated clothing, bed nets) is always essential.

**Q:** What did the CLASSIC trial find about IV fluid restriction in septic shock?

**A:** In patients with septic shock, IV fluid restriction (median 1798 mL) did NOT result in fewer deaths within 90 days compared to standard IV fluid therapy (median 3811 mL). Mortality was 42.3% vs. 42.1% respectively, with similar serious adverse events.

The CLASSIC trial challenged aggressive fluid restriction in septic shock, showing it is safe but not superior to standard fluid management. Earlier trials (FEAST, ProCESS, ARISE) have also challenged aggressive resuscitation protocols. The current approach favors individualized fluid therapy guided by dynamic measures of fluid responsiveness.

**Q:** Define SIRS criteria.

**A:** Two or more of: Temperature >38°C or <36°C, HR >90 bpm, RR >20 breaths/min, WBC >12 × 10⁹/L.

SIRS criteria identify a non-specific systemic inflammatory response that may be due to infection or non-infectious causes (trauma, pancreatitis). When SIRS is present with a suspected infection source, it meets the older definition of sepsis. Current definitions emphasize organ dysfunction (SOFA score ≥2).

**Q:** What defines septic shock in current definitions?

**A:** Sepsis plus either: initial lactate >4 mmol/L OR hypotension (MAP <65 mmHg) requiring vasopressors despite adequate fluid resuscitation.

Septic shock indicates severe circulatory failure with tissue hypoperfusion. Lactate ≥4 mmol/L indicates anaerobic metabolism even without hypotension ('cryptic shock'). Both hypotension and elevated lactate independently increase mortality and warrant aggressive management.

**Q:** What are the key components of sepsis bundle management?

**A:** 1) Early recognition + lactate measurement (remeasure if >2 mmol/L), 2) Blood cultures before antibiotics, 3) Broad-spectrum antibiotics early, 4) IV fluid resuscitation (30 mL/kg crystalloid for hypotension or lactate ≥4), 5) Vasopressors for persistent hypotension to maintain MAP ≥65 mmHg, 6) Source control.

The Surviving Sepsis Campaign bundle emphasizes early, time-sensitive interventions. Updated bundles consolidated the 3-hour and 6-hour bundles into a 1-hour bundle. Goal-directed management should not be delayed for laboratory values in septic shock.

**Q:** When should glucocorticoids be considered in septic shock?

**A:** In treatment-refractory hypotension (septic shock not responding to vasopressors), to treat relative adrenal insufficiency.

Critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency (CIRCI) occurs in severe sepsis/septic shock. Hydrocortisone (200 mg/day IV) is recommended by some guidelines when shock is not responsive to fluids and vasopressors. The ADRENAL trial showed no mortality benefit but did show faster shock reversal.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology and presentation of Toxic Shock Syndrome (TSS)?

**A:** Caused by superantigen from S. aureus or Group A Streptococcus (GAS) that activates T-cells and cytokines non-specifically. Presents with: shock, multi-organ failure, fever, diffuse erythematous macular rash, ≥3 organ systems involved (CNS, respiratory, GI, muscular, mucous membranes, renal, liver, hematologic, skin). May have vesiculobullous lesions or necrotizing fasciitis.

Superantigens bypass normal antigen presentation, causing massive T-cell activation and cytokine storm (TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6). TSS can be menstrual (tampon-associated S. aureus) or non-menstrual. Streptococcal TSS is associated with necrotizing fasciitis and has higher mortality. Treatment: aggressive fluid resuscitation, source control, IV antibiotics, IVIG in refractory cases.

**Q:** What are the SOFA score criteria and what score indicates high risk for in-hospital mortality in sepsis?

**A:** SOFA ≥2 indicates high risk for in-hospital mortality in sepsis. Quick SOFA (qSOFA) screening: altered mental status + RR ≥22/min + sBP ≤100 mmHg (2 of 3 = positive screen). qSOFA should not be used as a sole screening tool due to poor sensitivity.

The Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) score evaluates respiratory (PaO2/FiO2), coagulation (platelets), liver (bilirubin), cardiovascular (vasopressor requirement/MAP), CNS (GCS), and renal (creatinine/urine output) function. A change in total SOFA ≥2 points from baseline represents organ dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the common causes of bloody acute diarrhea (inflammatory)?

**A:** Bacterial (bloody diarrhea): Shigella, Salmonella, Campylobacter, Yersinia, E. coli (EHEC O157:H7), ischemia. Protozoal: E. histolytica (amoebiasis). Also: IBD.

Bloody diarrhea suggests mucosal invasion or toxin-mediated damage. EHEC O157:H7 is particularly important due to risk of hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS). E. histolytica causes amoebic dysentery. IBD (Crohn's/UC) is a non-infectious cause of bloody diarrhea.

**Q:** What distinguishes inflammatory from non-inflammatory diarrhea clinically and on stool examination?

**A:** Inflammatory diarrhea: damage to mucosal lining → passive protein-rich fluid loss, decreased fluid reabsorption; associated with abdominal pain, fever, chills; stool positive for white blood cells (WBCs). Non-inflammatory diarrhea: no mucosal damage; nausea/vomiting may occur; NO fever, chills, blood, or severe pain; stool negative for WBCs.

Differentiating inflammatory from non-inflammatory diarrhea guides investigation and empiric management. Inflammatory causes require targeted therapy (e.g., antibiotics for Shigella, specific antifungals for amoebiasis), while non-inflammatory causes are often self-limited.

## Sexually Transmitted Infections

**Q:** A 22-year-old woman has mucopurulent cervical discharge and cervical motion tenderness. Cervical NAAT is positive for Chlamydia trachomatis. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Chlamydial cervicitis (or pelvic inflammatory disease if upper tract involvement); treat with doxycycline 100mg BID x7 days (or azithromycin 1g single dose for uncomplicated)

PHAC/AMMI STI guidelines recommend doxycycline as preferred treatment for chlamydial infections; azithromycin single-dose acceptable for uncomplicated urethral/cervical infection but inferior for rectal chlamydia. Test-of-cure not routinely required. Treat partners within 60 days.

**Q:** What is the treatment for gonorrhea per current Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM single dose (1g if weight >150kg); test-of-cure recommended at 1 week; treat all partners and retest for chlamydia

PHAC STI guidelines updated due to rising fluoroquinolone and azithromycin resistance in Neisseria gonorrhoeae. Ceftriaxone remains effective. Dual therapy (with azithromycin) no longer recommended routinely. Disseminated gonococcal infection (septic arthritis, skin lesions): IV ceftriaxone.

**Q:** A pregnant woman has a positive RPR (1:64) and positive FTA-ABS. She has no prior treatment. What is the treatment?

**A:** Primary/secondary/early latent syphilis in pregnancy: benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM single dose — penicillin allergy: desensitize and give penicillin (no safe alternatives in pregnancy)

PHAC STI guidelines: all pregnant women should be screened for syphilis at first prenatal visit and at 28-32 weeks (and delivery) in high-prevalence areas. Congenital syphilis is preventable with timely treatment. Penicillin is the only proven treatment to prevent congenital transmission.

**Q:** What are the stages of syphilis and their clinical features?

**A:** Primary: painless chancre (2-3 weeks post-exposure); Secondary: diffuse maculopapular rash including palms/soles, condylomata lata, mucous patches (6-8 weeks); Latent: asymptomatic; Tertiary: gummas, cardiovascular (aortitis), neurosyphilis

All stages require treatment. Late latent or unknown duration syphilis: benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM x3 weeks. Neurosyphilis: IV penicillin G for 10-14 days. Diagnosis: RPR/VDRL (non-treponemal) + FTA-ABS/TPPA (treponemal confirmatory).

**Q:** What is pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) and how is it treated?

**A:** PID: infection of upper female genital tract (endometrium, fallopian tubes, ovaries); treat outpatient with ceftriaxone 500mg IM single dose + doxycycline 100mg BID x14 days + metronidazole 500mg BID x14 days

PID is usually polymicrobial (N. gonorrhoeae, C. trachomatis, anaerobes). Laparoscopy is gold standard for diagnosis but not required. Sequelae: chronic pelvic pain, infertility, ectopic pregnancy. Hospitalise if: severe illness, tubo-ovarian abscess, pregnancy, surgical emergency cannot be excluded.

**Q:** A 27-year-old man has painful penile vesicles that ulcerate. Tzanck smear shows multinucleated giant cells. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Genital herpes (HSV-2 most common, HSV-1 also possible); treat with oral acyclovir 400mg TID x7-10 days for primary episode, or valacyclovir 1g BID x5-10 days

PHAC STI guidelines recommend antiviral therapy for all primary genital herpes episodes regardless of symptom duration. Suppressive therapy (valacyclovir 500mg OD daily) reduces recurrences and transmission. PCR is more sensitive than Tzanck smear for diagnosis.

**Q:** What is HIV post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) for sexual exposure and what are the criteria?

**A:** PEP: TDF/FTC + bictegravir (or DTG/RAL) for 28 days; start within 72 hours of exposure; indicated for high-risk exposures (unprotected anal/vaginal sex with HIV-positive source or unknown status in high-prevalence setting)

PHAC guidelines recommend PEP for receptive anal sex with HIV-positive partner (highest risk). Risk assessment using estimated transmission probability. Document HIV-negative status of recipient before starting. Follow-up HIV testing at 4-6 weeks and 3 months. Discuss PrEP transition.

**Q:** What is the recommended screening frequency for STIs in sexually active MSM?

**A:** Syphilis, gonorrhea (pharynx, rectum, urethra), chlamydia every 3 months if high-risk (multiple partners, no consistent condom use, illicit drug use during sex, partner with STI/HIV)

PHAC STI guidelines recommend more frequent screening for MSM at high risk. Regular HIV and hepatitis C testing recommended. HPV vaccine recommended for MSM up to age 45. Hepatitis A and B vaccination.

**Q:** A 25-year-old woman has a thin homogeneous vaginal discharge with fishy odor (whiff test positive). Clue cells seen on wet mount. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Bacterial vaginosis; treat with metronidazole 500mg BID x7 days or metronidazole 0.75% vaginal gel x5 days or clindamycin vaginal cream x7 days

Bacterial vaginosis results from overgrowth of anaerobes (Gardnerella vaginalis) replacing normal Lactobacillus flora. Amsel criteria (3 of 4): thin homogeneous discharge, clue cells, vaginal pH >4.5, positive whiff test. Partners do not require treatment (not an STI).

**Q:** What is the treatment for Trichomonas vaginalis infection?

**A:** Metronidazole 2g PO single dose (or 500mg BID x7 days) for both patient and sexual partners simultaneously

Trichomoniasis is the most common curable STI. Presentations: yellow-green frothy discharge, vulvar pruritus, "strawberry cervix" (punctate hemorrhages). PHAC guidelines: treat all sexual partners. Test-of-cure recommended if symptoms persist.

**Q:** What is uncomplicated gonorrhea treatment in Canada?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 250-500mg IM single dose

Test of cure 2-4 weeks. Treat partners. Report to public health. Screen concurrent CT/HIV.

**Q:** What is the recommended treatment for uncomplicated gonorrhea per Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM × 1 dose (if <150 kg; 1g if ≥150 kg). If cephalosporin allergy: gentamicin 240 mg IM + azithromycin 2g PO. Always treat empirically for concurrent chlamydia: doxycycline 100 mg BID × 7 days (preferred over azithromycin 1g).

Canadian STI guidelines 2024 no longer recommend dual therapy with azithromycin for gonorrhea (ceftriaxone alone sufficient). Doxycycline added for chlamydia co-treatment. Test of cure at 2 weeks for pharyngeal GC (harder to eradicate). NAAT at 3-6 months for reinfection. Report to public health.

**Q:** What is the screening protocol for STIs in Canada?

**A:** Screen all sexually active individuals: gonorrhea/chlamydia NAAT (urine or swab), syphilis serology, HIV (opt-out). High-risk (MSM, multiple partners, PWID): screen q3-6 months including pharyngeal/rectal sites. Pregnant: syphilis + HIV + HBV + GC/CT at first prenatal visit.

PHAC STI screening guidelines prioritize NAAT over culture for GC/CT (higher sensitivity). Extragenital screening (pharyngeal, rectal) important for MSM — many infections asymptomatic at these sites. Syphilis screening: RPR or VDRL (non-treponemal) → confirm with TP-PA or FTA-ABS (treponemal). Reverse screening algorithm increasingly used.

**Q:** What are the stages of syphilis and their treatment?

**A:** Primary (painless chancre): benzathine penicillin G 2.4 MU IM × 1. Secondary (rash, condylomata lata, mucous patches): same. Early latent (<1 yr): same. Late latent (>1 yr)/unknown duration: 2.4 MU IM weekly × 3 weeks. Neurosyphilis: IV penicillin G 3-4 MU q4h × 10-14 days.

Tertiary syphilis: gummatous (skin/bone), cardiovascular (aortitis), neurosyphilis (tabes dorsalis, general paresis, Argyll Robertson pupil). All syphilis patients should be tested for HIV. Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction: fever, rigors within 24h of treatment (cytokine release from spirochete lysis) — warn patients, supportive care. PCN allergy: desensitize (no adequate alternative for neurosyphilis).

**Q:** What is the approach to genital herpes (HSV) management?

**A:** First episode: valacyclovir 1g BID × 10 days or acyclovir 400 mg TID × 10 days. Recurrent: valacyclovir 500 mg BID × 3 days or 1g daily × 5 days. Suppressive therapy (≥6 recurrences/year or serodiscordant partner): valacyclovir 500 mg daily.

HSV-2 causes most genital herpes; HSV-1 increasing. Type-specific serology (HSV-1/2 IgG) can confirm but not recommended as routine screening. Viral shedding occurs even when asymptomatic. Suppressive therapy reduces transmission by ~50%. Condoms further reduce risk. Pregnancy: acyclovir suppression from 36 weeks if history of genital HSV; C-section if active lesions at delivery.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chlamydia and its complications?

**A:** Uncomplicated: doxycycline 100 mg BID × 7 days (first-line, superior to azithromycin for rectal CT). Alternative: azithromycin 1g PO × 1. Pregnancy: azithromycin 1g × 1. Complications: PID, epididymitis, reactive arthritis, neonatal conjunctivitis/pneumonia, infertility.

Chlamydia is the most commonly reported STI in Canada. Often asymptomatic (70% women, 50% men). PID: doxycycline + ceftriaxone + metronidazole × 14 days. Test of cure not routine except pregnancy. Retest at 3-6 months for reinfection. Treat all partners within 60 days. Expedited partner therapy available in some provinces.

**Q:** What is the management of pelvic inflammatory disease (PID)?

**A:** Outpatient (mild-moderate): ceftriaxone 500 mg IM × 1 + doxycycline 100 mg BID × 14 days + metronidazole 500 mg BID × 14 days. Inpatient (severe, TOA, pregnant, surgical emergency): cefoxitin IV + doxycycline → step-down to oral.

PID: ascending infection from cervix (GC, CT, BV-associated organisms, anaerobes). Clinical diagnosis: lower abdominal pain + cervical motion/adnexal/uterine tenderness. Low threshold to treat (prevent infertility, ectopic pregnancy, chronic pelvic pain). TOA: antibiotics ± IR drainage if >6 cm or not improving. Remove IUD only if no improvement at 48-72h.

**Q:** What are the HPV-related diseases and vaccination schedule?

**A:** HPV causes: cervical (16,18→70%), anal, oropharyngeal, penile, vulvar cancer, genital warts (6,11). Gardasil-9: covers 6,11,16,18,31,33,45,52,58. Schedule: age 9-14: 2 doses (0,6 months). ≥15: 3 doses (0,2,6). NACI recommends for all 9-26 years; shared decision up to 45.

HPV vaccine prevents ~90% of HPV-related cancers. Males and females equally benefit. Catch-up vaccination encouraged even if prior HPV infection (protects against other types). Cervical screening continues despite vaccination. Canadian provinces fund school-based programs (grade 4-7 typically).

**Q:** What causes genital ulcer disease and how is it differentiated?

**A:** Painful: HSV (most common, vesicles→shallow ulcers, grouped), chancroid (H. ducreyi, ragged purulent ulcer + tender LAD). Painless: syphilis (chancre, indurated, clean base), LGV (Chlamydia L1-3, inguinal buboes). Granuloma inguinale: painless, beefy red, Donovan bodies.

HSV is by far the most common cause of genital ulcers in Canada. Multiple causes can coexist. Always test for syphilis and HIV with any genital ulcer. LGV: increasingly reported in MSM, causes proctitis. Treat with doxycycline 100 mg BID × 21 days. Chancroid rare in Canada.

**Q:** What is expedited partner therapy (EPT)?

**A:** Providing prescriptions/medications to the sexual partners of STI-positive patients without direct clinical assessment of the partner. Used for GC and CT to improve partner treatment rates and reduce reinfection.

EPT is legal/permitted in several Canadian provinces. Index patient gives medication/prescription to partner(s). Limitations: cannot assess for allergies, drug interactions, pregnancy. Not appropriate for syphilis (needs staging) or MSM (should be screened for HIV/syphilis). PHAC supports EPT as a public health strategy for CT/GC.

**Q:** What is the approach to rectal STIs in MSM?

**A:** Screen all MSM: rectal NAAT for GC/CT at each visit (even if asymptomatic). Rectal GC: ceftriaxone 500 mg IM. Rectal CT: doxycycline 100 mg BID × 7 days (superior to azithromycin rectally). LGV proctitis: doxycycline 100 mg BID × 21 days. Also screen for syphilis, HIV, mpox.

Rectal GC/CT are often asymptomatic and missed without site-specific testing. LGV proctitis mimics IBD (bloody discharge, tenesmus). Doxycycline 100 mg post-exposure (doxyPEP) reduces STI incidence in MSM by 60-70% — emerging prevention strategy, not yet standard of care in Canadian guidelines but under review.

**Q:** What are the key differences between HSV-1 and HSV-2 infections?

**A:** HSV-1: typically 'cold sores' at mucocutaneous junction of lips/face/hard palate; NOT on soft non-keratinized mucous membranes. HSV-2: usually sexually transmitted (incubation 2–20 days); causes gingivostomatitis, vulvovaginitis (edematous, extremely tender, profuse vaginal discharge), urethritis; recurrent on vulva, vagina, penis for 5–7 days.

Both subtypes are biologically and immunologically different. HSV remains latent in dorsal root ganglia after primary infection and reactivates with triggers such as fever, sun exposure, menstruation, and stress.

**Q:** What is the management of first-episode genital herpes (HSV-2)?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID for 7–10 days. Concurrent STIs should be tested and treated. Pain control with sitz baths and analgesia. Famciclovir and valacyclovir may be substituted (better enteric absorption, less frequent dosing).

Recurrent genital herpes can be managed with daily suppressive therapy (acyclovir 400 mg PO BID) or episodic therapy (acyclovir 800 mg PO TID for 5 days or TID for 2 days, started during prodrome or within 1 day of lesion onset).

**Q:** What HPV types are associated with genital warts (condyloma acuminata) and which types cause cervical cancer?

**A:** Genital warts: commonly HPV 6 and 11 (low-risk, non-oncogenic). Cervical dysplasia, SCC, and invasive cancer: HPV 16, 18, 31, 33 (high-risk, oncogenic).

Condyloma acuminata are skin-coloured papules to cauliflower-like masses in genital/perianal areas. They are highly contagious, transmitted sexually and non-sexually (Koebner phenomenon). Acetowhitening (acetic acid 5% × 5 min) reveals subclinical lesions.

**Q:** What are the three stages of syphilis and their key clinical features?

**A:** Primary: single painless indurated chancre at inoculation site after 3 weeks, heals 4–6 weeks, with non-tender regional lymphadenopathy. Secondary (2–6 months later): generalized lymphadenopathy, maculopapular rash (non-pruritic), condyloma lata, mucous patches, systemic symptoms. Tertiary (3–7 years later): gumma (granulomatous non-tender nodule), rare.

Syphilis is caused by Treponema pallidum. VDRL is negative in primary but positive in secondary stage. FTA-ABS has greater sensitivity and can detect disease earlier. Treatment: penicillin G 2.4 million units IM (single dose for primary/secondary; weekly × 3 for tertiary).

**Q:** How do you distinguish primary syphilis from chancroid and HSV on clinical grounds?

**A:** Primary syphilis: single, painless, indurated ulcer (chancre) with non-tender lymphadenopathy. Chancroid: painful ulcer. HSV: multiple painful vesicles/ulcers. Definitive distinction requires laboratory testing (dark-field examination for spirochetes, VDRL, FTA-ABS, PCR).

Clinical features alone are insufficient to diagnose primary syphilis. Dark-field examination of chancre fluid or lymph node material may reveal spirochetes. VDRL may be negative in primary syphilis, so FTA-ABS should be performed.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of disseminated gonococcemia and how is it managed?

**A:** Features: hemorrhagic tender pustules on a purpuric/petechial background on distal extremities; fever, arthritis, urethritis, proctitis, pharyngitis, tenosynovitis; neonatal conjunctivitis if infected via birth canal. Management: notify public health, screen for other STIs, ceftriaxone 1 g IM or IV every 24 hours.

Gonococcemia is caused by Neisseria gonorrhoeae. Tests are often negative, requiring a high index of suspicion. Bacterial culture of blood, joint fluid, and skin lesions should be obtained.

**Q:** What is the photosensitivity and secondary syphilis rash, and what are the differential diagnoses?

**A:** Secondary syphilis rash: flat-topped scaling, non-pruritic, sharply defined, circular/annular maculopapules appearing 2–6 months after primary infection. Also: condyloma lata (wart-like moist papules in genital/perianal region) and mucous patches (oral mucosa). Differential: pityriasis rosea, tinea corporis, drug eruptions, lichen planus.

Secondary syphilis is also associated with generalized lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, headache, fever, arthralgias, and malaise. Lesions heal in 1–5 weeks but may recur for 1 year. VDRL and FTA-ABS are both positive in secondary syphilis.

**Q:** What antiviral agents are used for genital herpes, and what are the recommended dosing regimens for first episode vs. recurrent disease?

**A:** For genital herpes: Famciclovir 250 mg TID x7–10 days (1st episode) or 125 mg BID x3 days (recurrence). Valacyclovir 1000 mg BID x3 days (1st episode) or 500 mg BID x5 days (recurrence). Both require dose reduction with renal impairment.

These antivirals reduce severity and duration of outbreaks and can be used for prophylaxis to reduce transmission. Drug interactions include cladribine and varicella/zoster vaccines. Valacyclovir is a prodrug of acyclovir with improved oral bioavailability.

**Q:** What is the management of pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) in the ED?

**A:** Broad-spectrum antibiotics: ceftriaxone 500 mg IM x 1 dose, PLUS doxycycline 100 mg PO/IV q12h x 2 weeks, PLUS metronidazole 500 mg PO/IV q12h x 2 weeks. Low threshold to treat empirically based on clinical suspicion (cervical motion tenderness/'chandelier sign').

PID is caused by ascending infection (often N. gonorrhoeae and C. trachomatis). Untreated PID can lead to chronic pelvic pain, infertility, and ectopic pregnancy. The 'chandelier sign' — severe pain with cervical manipulation — is classic. Patients with TOA or who fail outpatient therapy require admission.

**Q:** What STI prophylaxis is given after sexual assault?

**A:** Assume positive for gonorrhea and chlamydia: ceftriaxone IM x1 dose (500 mg if <150 kg; 1 g if >150 kg) AND doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x7 days (alternative: azithromycin 1 g PO x1 dose). May also start post-exposure prophylaxis for hepatitis B, syphilis, and HIV. Offer pregnancy prophylaxis: levonorgestrel 1.5 mg PO STAT (Plan B).

Empiric STI treatment is recommended because culture results take days and follow-up is uncertain. Emergency contraception is most effective when given immediately after assault. VDRL (syphilis serology) should be repeated at 3 months if initially negative. HIV PEP must be started within 72 hours.

**Q:** What is the pediatric antibiotic treatment for streptococcal pharyngitis?

**A:** Group A beta-hemolytic Streptococcus (GAS): penicillin/amoxicillin (first line), cefalexin, or erythromycin (can cause GI upset).

Penicillin remains the drug of choice for GAS pharyngitis and has never developed resistance. Treatment prevents rheumatic fever (but not post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis). A 10-day course is recommended. Amoxicillin is preferred by some due to better taste compliance. Macrolides are used for penicillin allergy but resistance is increasing.

**Q:** What is Disseminated Gonococcal Infection (DGI) and who is most at risk?

**A:** DGI presents with: fever, skin lesions (pustules/vesicles on erythematous base, ~5 mm), arthritis (joint swelling and tenderness), and septic arthritis in larger joints (knees, ankles, elbows). Most common in gonococcus-positive women during menstruation or pregnancy. Skin lesions appear in extremities and resolve quickly (<7 days).

DGI occurs when N. gonorrhoeae disseminates hematogenously. The triad of migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis, and dermatitis is classic. Treatment: IV ceftriaxone 1g daily. All sexual partners within 60 days should be tested and treated. Screen for co-infection with chlamydia, syphilis, HIV.

**Q:** What are the PHE recommendations for STI screening in high-risk groups?

**A:** High-risk groups: voluntary HIV antibody screening (Grade A), gonorrhea screening (Grade A), chlamydia screening in women (Grade B), syphilis screening (Grade A), VDRL test for syphilis high-risk groups (Grade A). General counselling on protection against STIs (Grade B) for all adults.

STI screening in high-risk populations (MSM, sex workers, multiple partners, history of STI) allows early detection and treatment to prevent transmission and complications. Chlamydia screening in women is critical to prevent pelvic inflammatory disease and infertility.

**Q:** What are the signs and symptoms of urethritis and how does it differ from cystitis in presentation?

**A:** Urethritis: INITIAL dysuria (at start of micturition), urethral/vaginal discharge, history of STI. Organisms: C. trachomatis, N. gonorrhoeae, Trichomonas spp., Candida spp., herpes. Cystitis: INTERNAL dysuria throughout micturition, frequency, urgency, hematuria, nocturia, suprapubic discomfort.

The distinction between initial vs. throughout micturition dysuria helps localize infection. Positive urethral swab or PCR for chlamydia or gonorrhea must be reported to Public Health. All urethritis patients should return 4-7 days after completing therapy.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for vulvovaginal candidiasis in a symptomatic patient?

**A:** Fluconazole 150 mg PO x 1 dose OR miconazole 2% cream (Monistat 7%): one applicator (5 g) intravaginally QHS x 7 days. Treat only if patient is symptomatic.

Vulvovaginal candidiasis is caused by Candida species. Oral fluconazole is convenient; topical azole preparations (e.g., miconazole) are equally effective. Canadian guidelines emphasize treating only symptomatic patients.

**Q:** What are the organisms responsible for bacterial vaginosis (BV) and what is the first-line treatment?

**A:** BV is caused by overgrowth of Gardnerella vaginalis, Mycoplasma hominis, and anaerobes. First-line: metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 7 days OR metronidazole 0.75% gel one applicator (5 g) intravaginally QHS x 5 days OR clindamycin 2% cream one applicator (5 g) intravaginally QHS x 7 days.
