BV results from disruption of normal vaginal flora. Treatment is indicated for symptomatic patients and for asymptomatic high-risk cases (e.g., high-risk pregnancy, prior to IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery). Metronidazole remains the cornerstone of Canadian guideline-recommended therapy.

**Q:** In what clinical circumstances should asymptomatic bacterial vaginosis be treated?

**A:** Asymptomatic BV should be treated in: high-risk pregnancy, prior to IUD insertion, prior to gynecologic surgery, prior to induced abortion, or prior to upper tract instrumentation.

BV increases the risk of ascending infection and preterm labour. Treatment in these specific scenarios reduces procedural and obstetric complications even without symptoms.

**Q:** What is the treatment regimen for a first episode of genital herpes (HSV)?

**A:** First episode: acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 5–10 days OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID x 5 days OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID x 10 days.

Antiviral therapy initiated early reduces the duration and severity of the primary episode. Valacyclovir and famciclovir have improved bioavailability compared with acyclovir. All three are acceptable per Canadian STI guidelines.

**Q:** What is the recommended suppressive therapy for genital herpes in pregnancy at 36 weeks gestation?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID starting at 36 weeks gestation.

Suppressive antiviral therapy near term reduces viral shedding and the risk of neonatal herpes transmission. It also reduces the likelihood of clinical recurrence necessitating caesarean section.

**Q:** What is the recommended treatment for gonorrhea/chlamydia co-infection in Canada?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 250 mg IM x 1 dose PLUS azithromycin 1 g PO x 1 dose. No intercourse for 1 week after treatment.

Dual therapy is recommended to cover both Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis due to frequent co-infection. Ceftriaxone addresses gonorrhea (including fluoroquinolone-resistant strains) while azithromycin covers chlamydia.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for uncomplicated Chlamydia trachomatis infection?

**A:** Doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 7 days. No intercourse for 1 week after treatment.

Doxycycline is recommended over azithromycin as monotherapy for chlamydia based on superior cure rates. Abstaining from intercourse for 1 week ensures treatment completion and prevents re-transmission.

## Tuberculosis

**Q:** A 35-year-old immigrant from a TB-endemic country has a positive IGRA (QuantiFERON-TB Gold). CXR is normal. No symptoms. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Latent TB infection (LTBI); treat with isoniazid 9 months (INH 9H) or rifampin 4 months (RIF 4R) or isoniazid + rifapentine (1HP) once weekly x12 weeks (DOTS)

AMMI Canada and CATSS guidelines recommend LTBI treatment for all immunocompetent adults <50 years with positive IGRA/TST. Rifampin 4 months (4R) has equivalent efficacy to INH 9 months with better completion rates. INH requires pyridoxine to prevent peripheral neuropathy.

**Q:** A 40-year-old with productive cough, hemoptysis, weight loss, and night sweats for 3 months. CXR shows upper lobe cavitation. AFB smear is positive. Diagnosis and initial treatment?

**A:** Active pulmonary tuberculosis; initiate 4-drug therapy: RIPE (Rifampin + Isoniazid + Pyrazinamide + Ethambutol) for 2 months, then Rifampin + Isoniazid for 4 months (total 6 months)

AMMI Canada TB guidelines recommend standard 6-month DOTS therapy (directly observed therapy short-course). RIPE initiation phase x2 months, followed by 4-month RI continuation phase. Drug sensitivity testing guides modifications. Isolate patient in negative-pressure room during infectious period.

**Q:** What is the role of drug susceptibility testing (DST) in TB management?

**A:** All initial Mycobacterium tuberculosis isolates should have DST performed; guides treatment modifications; MDR-TB (resistant to INH + RIF) requires specialized regimens

MDR-TB (multidrug-resistant) requires 18-20+ month treatment with second-line drugs. XDR-TB additionally resistant to fluoroquinolones and second-line injectables. Bedaquiline, linezolid, and pretomanid are newer agents for MDR-TB. All TB in Canada is reportable.

**Q:** What are the side effects of isoniazid (INH) and how are they monitored?

**A:** Peripheral neuropathy (prevented by pyridoxine 25-50mg/day), hepatotoxicity (hold if ALT >3-5x ULN), drug-induced lupus

INH hepatotoxicity risk increases with age, alcohol use, and pre-existing liver disease. Monitor LFTs monthly in patients at risk. INH inhibits CYP2E1 and is a MAO inhibitor — dietary interactions (tyramine). INH inhibits metabolism of phenytoin and warfarin.

**Q:** A patient with HIV and CD4 count 80 cells/μL is diagnosed with active TB. When should ART be initiated?

**A:** Start ART within 2-8 weeks of starting TB therapy (regardless of CD4 count) — early ART reduces mortality in HIV/TB co-infection

AMMI Canada guidelines recommend early ART in HIV-TB co-infection (SAPIT trial). Exception: CD4 >50 and extrapulmonary TB other than meningitis — can delay 8 weeks. TB meningitis: delay ART 8 weeks due to higher IRIS risk (worsens mortality). Rifampin induces CYP3A4 — dose-adjust certain ARVs.

**Q:** How is TB infection diagnosed in Canada — when to use TST vs. IGRA?

**A:** IGRA (QuantiFERON-TB Gold, T-SPOT.TB) preferred for: BCG-vaccinated, immunocompromised; TST (tuberculin skin test) preferred for: children <5, prior positive IGRA; either acceptable for most adults

CATSS/AMMI guidelines recommend IGRA over TST in BCG-vaccinated individuals as BCG causes false-positive TST. IGRA is also preferred in immunocompromised patients. Two-step TST used in healthcare worker surveillance programs.

**Q:** What are the public health obligations when a new case of active TB is identified?

**A:** Mandatory reporting to public health within 24h; contact investigation to identify exposed individuals; LTBI testing and treatment for close contacts; DOT (directly observed therapy) for high-risk patients

AMMI Canada and PHAC guidelines require that all active TB cases be reported to the local health unit. Contact investigations use the concentric-circle approach. Priority contacts: household, close contacts. High-risk contacts (children <5, immunocompromised) receive LTBI prophylaxis.

**Q:** What radiographic findings are typical of primary vs. post-primary (reactivation) TB?

**A:** Primary: lower or mid-lobe consolidation, hilar lymphadenopathy, pleural effusion; Reactivation: upper lobe cavitation, fibrosis, calcified nodules (Ghon complex)

Primary TB occurs with initial infection — lymphadenopathy and lower lobe infiltrate from haematogenous spread. Reactivation TB: upper lobe predilection (high O2 tension) with classic cavitation and apical scarring. Miliary TB: hematogenous dissemination, diffuse 1-2mm nodules.

**Q:** What is extensively drug-resistant TB (XDR-TB) and what treatment options exist?

**A:** XDR-TB: MDR-TB + resistance to fluoroquinolones; newer regimens: BPaL (bedaquiline + pretomanid + linezolid) — ~90% treatment success in clinical trials (ZeNix, TB-PRACTECAL trials)

XDR-TB was previously almost untreatable. BPaL regimen (ZeNix trial) showed 90% success at 26 weeks — revolutionizing MDR/XDR-TB treatment. Bedaquiline + delamanid combinations also used. All cases require specialized MDR-TB centres for management.

**Q:** What are the indications for LTBI treatment in immunocompromised patients in Canada?

**A:** All HIV-positive patients (regardless of IGRA/TST result if from endemic country), patients starting anti-TNF therapy, organ transplant recipients, patients starting high-dose steroids — treat LTBI before immunosuppression when possible

CATSS guidelines recommend LTBI testing and treatment before biologic therapies (especially anti-TNF). TNF inhibitors increase TB reactivation risk 5-25 fold. Repeat LTBI testing annually in high-risk groups. Rifampin preferred over INH when on biologic therapy (shorter duration).

**Q:** What is standard TB treatment?

**A:** 2 months RIPE then 4 months RI. Total 6 months. DOT recommended

INH: hepatotoxicity, neuropathy (give B6). Rifampin: CYP450 inducer, orange secretions.

**Q:** What is the standard treatment regimen for active pulmonary TB?

**A:** Intensive phase: 2 months of RIPE (rifampin + isoniazid + pyrazinamide + ethambutol). Continuation phase: 4 months of rifampin + isoniazid. Total: 6 months. DOT (directly observed therapy) recommended for all.

Canadian Tuberculosis Standards recommend DOT. Add pyridoxine (vitamin B6) 25-50 mg daily with INH to prevent peripheral neuropathy. Monitor LFTs monthly (INH, RIF, PZA all hepatotoxic). Ethambutol: baseline and monthly visual acuity/color vision (optic neuritis). Extend to 9 months if cavitary disease + 2-month culture-positive.

**Q:** What is latent TB infection (LTBI) and how is it treated?

**A:** Positive TST (≥5mm, ≥10mm, or any induration depending on risk) or positive IGRA with no clinical/radiographic active TB. Treatment options: isoniazid 9 months daily, rifampin 4 months daily (preferred for INH-resistant or INH intolerance), or isoniazid+rifapentine weekly × 12 weeks (3HP).

Canadian TB Standards: TST ≥5mm positive in HIV, close contacts, immunosuppressed, fibronodular CXR. ≥10mm for other risk groups (recent immigrants <5 yr, healthcare workers, homeless, PWID). Rifampin 4 months has best completion rates and fewer hepatotoxic events than 9-month INH. 3HP (DOT or self-administered) increasingly preferred.

**Q:** What infection control measures are required for suspected active pulmonary TB?

**A:** Airborne precautions: negative pressure isolation room, N95 respirator for HCWs, surgical mask on patient during transport. 3 negative sputum AFB smears (8-24h apart, at least one early morning) to discontinue isolation. Report to public health.

TB is transmitted via aerosolized droplet nuclei (1-5 μm). Contact tracing is public health responsibility. Smear-positive patients are most infectious. Smear-negative but culture-positive patients are less infectious but can still transmit. Respiratory isolation until: 3 negative smears AND clinically improving AND on effective therapy for ≥2 weeks.

**Q:** What are the side effects of anti-TB medications?

**A:** INH: hepatotoxicity (#1), peripheral neuropathy (prevent with B6), drug-induced lupus. RIF: hepatotoxicity, orange discoloration (fluids), drug interactions (CYP inducer — reduces warfarin, OCPs, antiretrovirals). PZA: hepatotoxicity, hyperuricemia, arthralgias. EMB: optic neuritis (dose-dependent, reversible).

INH hepatotoxicity increases with age, alcohol, and concurrent rifampin. Monitor ALT monthly; stop if >3× ULN with symptoms or >5× ULN asymptomatic. Rifampin: most important drug interactions — induces CYP3A4, 2C9, etc. Reduces efficacy of OCPs (advise alternative contraception), warfarin, PIs, methadone. Rifabutin used instead with certain ARTs.

**Q:** When should TB be suspected and what is the diagnostic workup?

**A:** Suspect if: cough >2-3 weeks, night sweats, weight loss, hemoptysis, fever, especially in high-risk groups (immigrants from endemic areas, Indigenous communities, homeless, HIV+, immunosuppressed). Workup: CXR, 3 sputum samples for AFB smear + culture + NAAT (GeneXpert MTB/RIF), TST or IGRA.

CXR findings: upper lobe cavities (reactivation), miliary pattern (disseminated), hilar LAD + Ghon complex (primary). GeneXpert MTB/RIF provides rapid diagnosis + rifampin resistance detection in 2 hours. Culture remains gold standard (drug susceptibility testing). Induced sputum or bronchoscopy if unable to produce spontaneous sputum.

**Q:** What is the approach to TB-HIV coinfection?

**A:** Start ART for all TB-HIV patients. Timing: start ART within 2 weeks if CD4 <50; within 8 weeks if CD4 ≥50 (delay reduces IRIS). Preferred ART: dolutegravir (dose-adjusted with rifampin: 50 mg BID) or efavirenz. Avoid PIs with rifampin (use rifabutin instead).

TB-HIV: higher mortality, higher risk of extrapulmonary TB, atypical CXR (lower lobe, miliary, non-cavitary). Sputum may be smear-negative more often. IRIS occurs in 10-40% of TB-HIV patients starting ART. Paradoxical TB-IRIS: worsening symptoms despite good ART/TB treatment response — usually self-limited, steroids for severe cases.

**Q:** What populations in Canada are at highest risk for TB?

**A:** Indigenous peoples (especially Inuit: 300× higher incidence), foreign-born from high-incidence countries, homeless/underhoused, incarcerated, HIV+, immunosuppressed (anti-TNF therapy, transplant), people living in congregate settings.

Canada's overall TB incidence is ~5/100,000 but rates are extremely disparate. Inuit TB rates are among highest in developed world. Determinants include overcrowding, food insecurity, barriers to healthcare access, and historical colonization impacts. TB elimination requires addressing social determinants alongside biomedical interventions.

**Q:** What is multidrug-resistant TB (MDR-TB)?

**A:** MDR-TB: resistant to at least INH + rifampin. XDR-TB: MDR + resistance to any fluoroquinolone + at least one injectable (amikacin, capreomycin, kanamycin) — updated definition: MDR + FQ resistance + bedaquiline/linezolid resistance. Treat with ≥4 effective drugs for 9-20 months including bedaquiline, linezolid, fluoroquinolone.

Suspect MDR-TB if: prior TB treatment, contact with MDR case, origin from high MDR-prevalence region (Eastern Europe, Central Asia). GeneXpert detects rifampin resistance rapidly. MDR-TB requires specialized centres. New drugs (bedaquiline, pretomanid, delamanid) have improved outcomes. BPaL regimen (bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid) for XDR-TB.

**Q:** What are the forms of extrapulmonary TB?

**A:** Lymph node (most common extrapulmonary in Canada), pleural, skeletal (Pott disease = vertebral), genitourinary, meningeal, peritoneal, miliary (disseminated), pericardial. Treatment: same RIPE regimen but extend to 9-12 months for CNS TB and bone/joint TB.

Extrapulmonary TB more common in immunocompromised (HIV) and children. TB meningitis: add dexamethasone (reduces mortality and neurological complications). CSF: lymphocytic pleocytosis, high protein, low glucose (similar to fungal). Tissue biopsy showing caseating granulomas + AFB is diagnostic. Pott disease: vertebral body destruction with paraspinal abscess.

**Q:** When should LTBI treatment be offered vs deferred?

**A:** Treat LTBI if: HIV+, close contact of active case, immunosuppressed (starting anti-TNF, transplant), fibronodular changes on CXR (old untreated TB), recent TST conversion (<2 years). Defer/observe if: low reactivation risk, prior complete treatment, concerns about hepatotoxicity in elderly.

Decision: weigh reactivation risk vs treatment toxicity. High reactivation risk (HIV, recent conversion, immunosuppression): treat. Low risk (remote infection, elderly, liver disease): shared decision. All anti-TNF candidates must be screened and treated for LTBI before starting biologic (2 months of treatment before anti-TNF). Canadian TB Standards guide risk stratification.

**Q:** A patient on rifampin for tuberculosis treatment has a subtherapeutic INR despite being on warfarin. Why?

**A:** Rifampin is a potent inducer of CYP enzymes (especially CYP2C9), which accelerates warfarin metabolism, reducing plasma warfarin levels and anticoagulant effect.

CYP enzyme induction is a pharmacokinetic interaction that increases drug clearance. Rifampin is one of the most potent known enzyme inducers and significantly reduces efficacy of warfarin and oral contraceptive pills.

**Q:** What is the PHE recommendation for TB screening in high-risk groups?

**A:** Mantoux skin testing (Grade A) for TB high-risk groups. INH prophylaxis for household contacts or skin test converters (Grade B), and INH prophylaxis for other high-risk subgroups (Grade B).

TB screening with TST (Mantoux) targets high-risk groups: immigrants from endemic countries, homeless individuals, immunocompromised patients, close contacts of active TB cases, and healthcare workers. Positive TST in these groups warrants IGRA testing and/or treatment for latent TB infection to prevent reactivation.

## Meningitis & Encephalitis

**Q:** A 22-year-old college student has sudden onset of severe headache, high fever, nuchal rigidity, and photophobia. Kernig and Brudzinski signs are positive. What is the diagnosis and immediate management?

**A:** Bacterial meningitis; give IV dexamethasone 0.15 mg/kg q6h + IV ceftriaxone 2g q12h immediately — do NOT delay for CT or LP if clinical diagnosis clear

AMMI Canada guidelines recommend immediate empiric antibiotics in suspected bacterial meningitis without awaiting LP if CT or LP would be delayed >30-60 minutes. Add ampicillin if age >50 or immunocompromised (Listeria coverage). Dexamethasone 20 min before first antibiotic dose reduces neurological sequelae.

**Q:** What CSF findings differentiate bacterial from viral meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial: WBC >1000 (PMN predominance), protein >1 g/L, glucose <2.2 mmol/L (CSF:serum ratio <0.4), positive Gram stain 60-90%; Viral: WBC 10-500 (lymphocytes), normal/mildly elevated protein, normal glucose

Additional CSF tests for bacterial meningitis: procalcitonin, lactate (>3.5 mmol/L suggests bacterial). PCR panels can rapidly identify pathogens (N. meningitidis, S. pneumoniae, L. monocytogenes, HSV). Opening pressure elevated in bacterial meningitis (>25 cm H2O).

**Q:** What organisms cause bacterial meningitis by age group?

**A:** Neonates: Group B Strep, E. coli, Listeria; Adults <50: S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis; Adults >50: S. pneumoniae, Listeria, Gram-negatives; HIV: Cryptococcus, Listeria; Post-neurosurgery: Staphylococcus, Gram-negatives

Empiric treatment must cover likely pathogens for age group. Ampicillin added for Listeria coverage in elderly, pregnant, immunocompromised. Meningococcal meningitis: droplet precautions, prophylactic rifampin/ciprofloxacin for close contacts.

**Q:** When is CT head required before lumbar puncture in suspected meningitis?

**A:** CT required before LP if: new focal neurological deficit, papilledema, new onset seizures, altered consciousness, or immunocompromised — risk of herniation

AMMI Canada guidelines recommend CT only in patients with risk factors for herniation. Normal CT does not completely exclude elevated ICP or prevent herniation. If CT required, do not delay antibiotics — blood cultures + antibiotics before CT, then LP.

**Q:** What is meningococcal prophylaxis and who requires it?

**A:** Rifampin 600mg PO BID x2 days OR ciprofloxacin 500mg PO single dose — for household contacts, childcare contacts, anyone with direct exposure to oral secretions within 7 days of illness onset

PHAC guidelines: close contacts of meningococcal meningitis require prophylaxis within 24 hours (no benefit after 14 days). Chemoprophylaxis eliminates nasopharyngeal carriage. Ceftriaxone is alternative in pregnancy (rifampin and cipro relatively contraindicated).

**Q:** A 65-year-old is HIV-positive with CD4 count 40 and has subacute meningitis with headache and confusion. India ink stain of CSF shows encapsulated yeast. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Cryptococcal meningitis; treat with amphotericin B + flucytosine (induction 2 weeks) then fluconazole consolidation x8 weeks, then maintenance fluconazole until CD4 >200 on ART

Cryptococcal meningitis: opening pressure often elevated (>30 cm H2O) — therapeutic LP to reduce ICP (remove 20-30 mL CSF). Serum and CSF cryptococcal antigen has high sensitivity. Delay ART 4-6 weeks after starting antifungal therapy (COAT trial: early ART increases mortality).

**Q:** What is viral (aseptic) meningitis and how is it managed?

**A:** Aseptic meningitis: lymphocytic CSF pleocytosis with negative bacterial cultures; most common causes: enteroviruses, HSV-2; treatment is supportive — start IV acyclovir empirically for HSV if encephalitis features

Enteroviral meningitis is most common in summer/fall. CSF PCR for enterovirus is diagnostic. HSV meningitis (HSV-2): may recur (Mollaret meningitis). IV acyclovir started empirically when HSV cannot be excluded, especially if encephalitis features (altered consciousness, focal deficits).

**Q:** What are the clinical features of herpes simplex encephalitis (HSE)?

**A:** Fever, headache, altered mental status, focal neurological deficits, seizures; MRI shows temporal lobe involvement (T2/FLAIR hyperintensity); CSF PCR for HSV-1 is diagnostic

HSE is the most common cause of fatal sporadic encephalitis. HSV-1 causes temporal lobe encephalitis via retrograde neuronal spread from olfactory nerves. Empiric IV acyclovir 10 mg/kg q8h must be started immediately — do not wait for PCR results. Treatment duration: 14-21 days.

**Q:** What are the meningococcal vaccine indications in Canada?

**A:** Routine immunization: meningococcal C conjugate at 12 months, Men ACWY at 12 years; high-risk groups: asplenia, complement deficiency, travel to endemic areas, lab workers, close contacts of cases

NACI (National Advisory Committee on Immunization) Canada recommends meningococcal ACWY vaccine for adolescents and at-risk groups. Meningococcal B vaccine (Bexsero or Trumenba) recommended for high-risk groups; not in routine schedule.

**Q:** How is tuberculous meningitis (TBM) distinguished from other meningitides?

**A:** TBM: subacute onset (weeks), lymphocytic pleocytosis, low glucose, very high protein, CSF AFB smear low sensitivity, CSF Xpert MTB/RIF; CT/MRI: basilar meningeal enhancement, hydrocephalus, tuberculomas

TBM requires 4-drug anti-TB therapy (RIPE) for minimum 12 months. Dexamethasone reduces mortality and neurological complications in TBM. Hydrocephalus may require ventriculoperitoneal shunt. Prognosis depends on clinical grade at presentation.

**Q:** What is empiric meningitis treatment in adults?

**A:** Ceftriaxone + vancomycin + dexamethasone. Add ampicillin if >50y (Listeria)

Dex reduces mortality for S. pneumoniae. Don't delay antibiotics for LP.

**Q:** What CSF findings distinguish bacterial vs viral vs TB meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial: PMN>1000, low glucose, high protein. Viral: lymph 10-500, normal glucose. TB: lymph, very low glucose, very high protein

Opening pressure high in bacterial. Gram stain positive 60-90%. PCR for HSV/enterovirus.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic regimen for bacterial meningitis by age group?

**A:** Neonates (<1 month): ampicillin + cefotaxime (or gentamicin). 1 month-50 years: ceftriaxone + vancomycin. >50 years or immunocompromised: ceftriaxone + vancomycin + ampicillin (Listeria coverage). ADD dexamethasone 0.15 mg/kg q6h × 4 days (give BEFORE or WITH first antibiotic dose).

Dexamethasone reduces mortality and neurological sequelae in pneumococcal meningitis (de Gans trial). Give before/with antibiotics — no benefit if given after. Vancomycin ensures coverage for penicillin-resistant S. pneumoniae. Do NOT delay antibiotics for LP — if LP will be delayed, draw blood cultures + give antibiotics immediately.

**Q:** What are the CSF findings in bacterial vs viral vs TB/fungal meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial: WBC >1000 (neutrophils), protein >1g/L, glucose <2.2 (CSF:serum ratio <0.4), cloudy. Viral: WBC 10-500 (lymphocytes), protein mildly elevated, glucose normal, clear. TB/fungal: WBC 50-500 (lymphocytes), protein very high, glucose very low, clear/fibrinous.

Partially treated bacterial meningitis can resemble viral (lymphocytic shift). Opening pressure: bacterial >30 cmH2O, viral usually normal, cryptococcal often very elevated. Gram stain positive in 60-90% of bacterial meningitis. Bacterial antigen testing and multiplex PCR panels increase diagnostic yield. CSF lactate >3.5 suggests bacterial.

**Q:** When should CT be done before LP in suspected meningitis?

**A:** CT BEFORE LP if: immunocompromised, history of CNS disease, new-onset seizures, papilledema, altered consciousness, focal neurological deficit. If CT needed: draw blood cultures + give antibiotics BEFORE CT → then LP when safe.

Most patients do NOT need CT before LP. Delaying LP (and thus antibiotics) for CT worsens outcomes. Contraindications to LP: signs of raised ICP with mass effect (not from meningitis alone), coagulopathy (INR >1.5, platelets <50,000), infection at LP site. Antibiotics should NEVER be delayed for imaging.

**Q:** What are the most common organisms causing bacterial meningitis by age?

**A:** Neonates: Group B Strep, E. coli, Listeria. Children: S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis. Adults (18-50): S. pneumoniae (#1), N. meningitidis. Elderly/immunocompromised: S. pneumoniae, Listeria, gram-negatives.

S. pneumoniae is the #1 cause of bacterial meningitis in adults in Canada with the highest mortality (~20-30%). H. influenzae type b meningitis has become rare due to vaccination. N. meningitidis: outbreaks in close quarters (dormitories, military). Listeria: unpasteurized dairy, deli meats in elderly/pregnant/immunocompromised.

**Q:** What chemoprophylaxis is given to close contacts of meningococcal meningitis?

**A:** Ciprofloxacin 500 mg PO × 1 dose (adults, preferred in Canada) OR rifampin 600 mg BID × 2 days OR ceftriaxone 250 mg IM × 1 (pregnancy). Give within 24h of diagnosis. Close contacts: household, daycare, kissing contacts, HCWs performing mouth-to-mouth/intubation.

Chemoprophylaxis reduces secondary attack rate by 89-95%. The index patient should also receive prophylaxis before discharge if treated with penicillin (doesn't eradicate nasopharyngeal carriage — ceftriaxone does). Public health must be notified immediately. Meningococcal vaccine offered to contacts during outbreaks.

**Q:** What is the approach to viral (aseptic) meningitis?

**A:** Most common cause: enteroviruses (summer/fall). HSV-2 (Mollaret recurrent meningitis). Presentation: headache, fever, neck stiffness, photophobia but relatively well-appearing. CSF: lymphocytic, normal glucose, mildly elevated protein. Treatment: supportive for enteroviral. Consider IV acyclovir if HSV suspected.

Enteroviral meningitis is self-limited (5-14 days). Distinguish from HSV ENCEPHALITIS (temporal lobe, altered consciousness, seizures — requires IV acyclovir 10 mg/kg q8h × 14-21 days). Multiplex CSF PCR panels can rapidly identify viral pathogens. Mumps, HIV, VZV, and arboviruses (West Nile) also cause viral meningitis.

**Q:** What are the complications of bacterial meningitis?

**A:** Acute: cerebral edema, seizures (20-30%), hydrocephalus, cranial nerve palsies (III, VI, VII, VIII), cerebral venous thrombosis, subdural empyema, DIC (meningococcal). Long-term: sensorineural hearing loss (most common long-term sequel), cognitive deficits, focal neurological deficits.

Hearing loss occurs in 10-30% — audiometry recommended for all survivors. Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome: adrenal hemorrhagic necrosis in fulminant meningococcemia → adrenal crisis. Repeat LP if: no clinical improvement at 48-72h, gram-negative organisms, or concern for antibiotic failure.

**Q:** What meningococcal vaccines are available in Canada?

**A:** MenC conjugate: routine at 12 months. MenACWY conjugate: grade 7 booster and high-risk. MenB (Bexsero or Trumenba): recommended for high-risk (asplenia, complement deficiency, outbreaks), optional for 14-25. NACI recommends MenACWY and MenB for certain high-risk groups.

Meningococcal disease in Canada: ~150-300 cases/year. Serogroup B accounts for ~50% of cases in young adults — not covered by MenACWY. Provincial programs vary. High-risk: functional/anatomic asplenia, complement deficiency, eculizumab use, HIV, lab workers, travelers to meningitis belt. Outbreak response includes mass vaccination + chemoprophylaxis.

**Q:** What is the approach to cryptococcal meningitis in non-HIV patients?

**A:** Organ transplant/immunosuppressed: same treatment as HIV (amphotericin B liposomal + flucytosine × 2 weeks → fluconazole consolidation/maintenance). Opening pressure management with serial LPs. Reduce immunosuppression if possible. Duration of maintenance: ≥12 months.

Cryptococcal meningitis in non-HIV immunosuppressed patients has worse outcomes than in HIV (higher mortality). India ink and CSF CrAg diagnostic. CT may show hydrocephalus — requires VP shunt if persistent. Non-immunosuppressed patients with cryptococcal meningitis: 6 weeks induction (2 wk amph B then 6 wk fluconazole) with serial LP.

**Q:** What is the role of dexamethasone in meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial meningitis (adults): dexamethasone 0.15 mg/kg IV q6h × 4 days, given BEFORE or WITH first antibiotic dose. Proven benefit for S. pneumoniae (reduces mortality from ~34% to ~14% and hearing loss). Less clear benefit for other organisms. NOT recommended for neonatal meningitis.

de Gans (2002) and Cochrane meta-analyses support dexamethasone in suspected bacterial meningitis in high-income settings. Mechanism: reduces subarachnoid inflammation and BBB disruption. May reduce vancomycin CSF penetration — some experts recommend higher vancomycin doses. If antibiotics already given, starting steroids later has no benefit.

**Q:** What are the clinical features that suggest meningitis as a cause of headache?

**A:** At least 2 of: fever, neck stiffness, altered mental status. Other possible findings: nausea, focal neurologic signs, seizure, papilledema, petechial rash, high CSF WBC, positive blood cultures.

The classic triad of meningitis is fever + neck stiffness + altered mental status. Not all features need to be present. Petechial/purpuric rash suggests meningococcal disease. Kernig's and Brudzinski's signs are classic but have low sensitivity. CT head should be done before LP if papilledema or neurologic deficits are present.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic regimen for bacterial meningitis in the ED, and when should steroids be given?

**A:** Empiric antibiotics: high-dose ceftriaxone + vancomycin + ampicillin (if age >50 yr or immunocompromised) + acyclovir (for HSV encephalitis coverage). Steroids (dexamethasone): administer based on clinical suspicion — give WITH or BEFORE the first dose of antibiotics. DO NOT delay antibiotics for LP. DO NOT wait for LP results before giving dexamethasone.

Early empiric antibiotics save lives. Dexamethasone reduces inflammatory cascade, decreasing hearing loss and neurological sequelae, particularly in pneumococcal meningitis. In practice, the order is: dexamethasone → antibiotics → CT head (if indicated) → LP. Ceftriaxone covers N. meningitidis and S. pneumoniae; ampicillin covers Listeria in the elderly and immunocompromised.

**Q:** What antibiotics are used for pediatric bacterial meningitis, organized by age group?

**A:** Neonatal: ampicillin + cefotaxime (covers GBS, E. coli, Listeria, Gram-negatives). 1–3 months: ceftriaxone/cefotaxime + vancomycin + ampicillin (if immunocompromised). >3 months: ceftriaxone + vancomycin (covers S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae type b [>5yr], meningococcus).

Listeria monocytogenes is resistant to cephalosporins; ampicillin is essential for neonates and immunocompromised patients. Vancomycin is added for >3 months to cover penicillin-resistant S. pneumoniae. Dexamethasone as adjunctive therapy reduces hearing loss in H. influenzae and S. pneumoniae meningitis.

**Q:** What is Meningococcemia and what is the characteristic rash?

**A:** Caused by Gram-negative Neisseria meningitidis. Presents with: flu-like symptoms (headache, myalgia, N/V) → petechial, macular, or maculopapular lesions with grey vesicular centres, usually a few millimeters. Lesions can become confluent and hemorrhagic (purpura fulminans). Signs of meningeal irritation: Brudzinski, Kernig, jolt accentuation test.

Meningococcemia is a rapidly fatal emergency. The petechial/purpuric rash reflects endothelial damage and DIC. Non-blanching petechiae in a febrile patient is meningococcemia until proven otherwise. Immediate IV penicillin G or ceftriaxone and supportive care are required. Close contacts need chemoprophylaxis (rifampin, ciprofloxacin, or ceftriaxone).

## Id Bacterial

**Q:** What is the Baggish scoring algorithm for acute HF diagnosis and what are its performance characteristics?

**A:** Score <6 suggests HF unlikely; score >6 had sensitivity of 96% and specificity of 84% (P<0.001) for acute HF diagnosis

The Baggish algorithm combines clinical and biochemical parameters to risk-stratify patients presenting with dyspnea, with high sensitivity for ruling out acute heart failure.

**Q:** What is the leading cause of morbidity and mortality after cardiac transplantation?

**A:** Infection - risk peaks early (first few months) then declines to a low persistent rate

Immunosuppressive medications required to prevent rejection simultaneously increase susceptibility to opportunistic and non-opportunistic infections.

**Q:** When does infection risk peak after cardiac transplantation?

**A:** Early during the first few months after transplantation, then declines to a low persistent rate

The period of highest immunosuppression coincides with peak infectious risk, requiring prophylactic and vigilant monitoring strategies.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of pemphigus vulgaris?

**A:** Flaccid, non-pruritic intraepidermal bullae/vesicles on mucous membranes and/or skin; erosions and secondary infection common; sites: mouth (90%), scalp, face, chest, axillae, groin, umbilicus; positive Nikolsky's sign and Asboe-Hansen sign

Pemphigus vulgaris typically begins in the oral mucosa and spreads to skin. The flaccid nature of bullae and positive Nikolsky's sign reflect the intraepidermal location of the split.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features of Pemphigus Vulgaris?

**A:** Flaccid, non-pruritic intraepidermal bullae/vesicles on mucous membranes and/or skin; may present with erosions and secondary infection; sites: mouth (90%), scalp, face, chest, axillae, groin, umbilicus; positive Nikolsky sign; positive Asboe-Hansen sign

Pemphigus vulgaris classically starts in the mouth and is intensely uncomfortable but not very itchy. The flaccid nature of bullae reflects their superficial intraepidermal location.

**Q:** What investigations can confirm a diagnosis of herpes simplex virus infection?

**A:** Tzanck smear with Giemsa stain showing multinucleated giant epithelial cells (not type-specific). Viral culture, electron microscopy, PCR, and direct fluorescence antibody test of specimen from unroofed base of a new lesion. Serologic testing can detect antibodies for current or past infection.

The Tzanck smear is a rapid, inexpensive test but cannot distinguish HSV-1 from HSV-2. PCR is the most sensitive test for definitive diagnosis.

**Q:** What are the complications of herpes simplex virus infection?

**A:** Dendritic corneal ulcer (ocular HSV), erythema multiforme (EM), herpes simplex encephalitis (infants at particular risk), and Kaposi's varicelliform eruption (eczema herpeticum) - HSV infection on atopic dermatitis.

HSV can cause both mucocutaneous disease and serious systemic complications, particularly in immunocompromised patients and infants.

**Q:** What is the incubation period for scabies after initial exposure?

**A:** Incubation period of approximately 1 month after initial exposure, after which pruritus begins. Re-infection is followed by hypersensitivity reaction within 24 hours.

The long incubation period reflects the time required for the host immune response to develop against scabies antigens, unlike re-infection which triggers rapid hypersensitivity.

**Q:** What investigations are used to diagnose HSV?

**A:** Tzanck smear (multinucleated giant cells), viral culture, PCR, direct fluorescence antibody test, serology for past/current infection

Multiple diagnostic modalities exist because each has different sensitivity/specificity depending on lesion age and type. Tzanck smear is rapid but non-specific.

**Q:** What is the complication of eczema herpeticum?

**A:** HSV infection spreading on skin affected by atopic dermatitis (AD), causing Kaposi's varicelliform eruption

Patients with AD have impaired skin barrier and immune dysfunction, making them susceptible to widespread HSV infection that can be life-threatening.

**Q:** What is erythema multiforme, and what are its typical triggers?

**A:** Target lesions (macules/papules with central vesicles, bull's-eye pattern); most commonly triggered by HSV infection or Mycoplasma

EM is a hypersensitivity reaction manifesting as characteristic target lesions. HSV is the most common identifiable trigger, making antiviral prophylaxis sometimes indicated.

**Q:** What symptoms may persist for weeks after scabies treatment despite effective therapy?

**A:** Pruritus (itching) may persist for 2-3 weeks due to prolonged hypersensitivity reaction

This persistent itching is NOT a sign of treatment failure. Mid-potency topical corticosteroids and antihistamines can help manage these symptoms.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of scabies?

**A:** Superficial linear burrows, intense nocturnal pruritus, and secondary infection; commonly affects axillae, groin, buttocks, and hand/foot web spaces with sparing of head and neck (except in infants).

Scabies mites burrow into the stratum corneum to lay eggs, causing intense itching that worsens at night due to increased mite activity. The distribution pattern helps differentiate from other pruritic conditions.

**Q:** What is the prodrome for recurrent herpes simplex infection?

**A:** Tingling, pruritus, and pain preceding the appearance of vesicular lesions by several hours to 1-2 days.

The prodromal phase reflects viral migration along sensory neurons to the skin surface. Recognizing this phase allows early initiation of antiviral therapy for maximum benefit.

**Q:** What are serious complications of HSV infection?

**A:** Dendritic corneal ulcer (ocular HSV), erythema multiforme, herpes simplex encephalitis (especially in infants), and eczema herpeticum (Kaposi's varicelliform eruption on atopic dermatitis patients).

HSV can cause vision-threatening keratitis, life-threatening CNS infection, and widespread cutaneous dissemination in immunocompromised or eczematous patients.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of scabies and where does it typically spare?

**A:** Superficial burrows, intense nocturnal pruritus, and secondary infection. Common sites: axillae, groin, buttocks, hands/feet (especially web spaces). Spares head and neck (except in infants).

Scabies presents with characteristic burrows and intense itching that worsens at night. The distribution pattern helps differentiate it from other pruritic conditions.

**Q:** What complications can occur with HSV infection?

**A:** Dendritic corneal ulcer (ocular), erythema multiforme, HSV encephalitis (especially in infants), and eczema herpeticum (Kaposi's varicelliform eruption) on atopic dermatitis patients.

Eczema herpeticum is a medical emergency requiring urgent antiviral treatment. HSV encephalitis has high mortality without prompt treatment.

**Q:** How is HSV diagnosed?

**A:** Tzanck smear (Giemsa stain) shows multinucleated giant epithelial cells. Gold standard: viral culture, PCR, direct fluorescent antibody test, or electron microscopy from unroofed vesicle base. Serologic testing for past/current infection.

Tzanck smear is rapid but cannot differentiate HSV-1 from HSV-2. PCR is now the preferred diagnostic test due to high sensitivity.

**Q:** What investigations are used for leishmaniasis?

**A:** Histology, culture, and PCR. Most cases resolve spontaneously; treatment with oral antifungals (fluconazole, ketoconazole) or parenteral therapy may be considered in severe/complicated cases.

Leishmaniasis is transmitted by sandflies infected with Leishmania. Diagnosis requires tissue confirmation.

**Q:** What is Kaposi sarcoma and what virus is required for its development?

**A:** An angioproliferative neoplasm that requires infection with Human Herpesvirus 8 (HHV-8)

HHV-8 is the necessary etiologic agent for all forms of Kaposi sarcoma, distinguishing it from other vascular tumors.

**Q:** What are the contraindications for doxycycline in acne and rosacea?

**A:** Pregnancy, hepatic impairment, drug hypersensitivity, concurrent acitretin/isotretinoin, penicillin antibiotics, and oral typhoid vaccine

Doxycycline 100mg bid is used for acne vulgaris and rosacea. Photosensitivity is a common side effect requiring sun avoidance counseling.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to cyclosporine?

**A:** Abnormal renal function, uncontrolled hypertension, malignancy (except NMSC), uncontrolled infection, immunodeficiency (excluding autoimmune disease), hypersensitivity

Cyclosporine is nephrotoxic and can worsen hypertension. Baseline and ongoing monitoring of renal function and blood pressure is mandatory during therapy.

**Q:** What are absolute contraindications for doxycycline?

**A:** Pregnancy, hepatic impairment, drug hypersensitivity, concurrent use with acitretin/isotretinoin/penicillin antibiotics, and oral typhoid vaccine

Doxycycline is contraindicated in pregnancy due to fetal tooth discoloration and bone growth effects, and should not be combined with other tetracyclines or retinoids.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to doxycycline in dermatology?

**A:** Pregnancy, hepatic impairment, drug hypersensitivity, concurrent use of acitretin or isotretinoin, concurrent use of penicillin antibiotics, concurrent oral typhoid vaccine

Doxycycline is a tetracycline antibiotic used for acne vulgaris, rosacea, and bullous pemphigoid. It chelates calcium and is contraindicated in pregnancy and with certain drugs.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of herpes simplex virus (HSV) infections?

**A:** Herpetiform (grouped) vesicles on an erythematous base on skin or mucous membranes

HSV is transmitted via contact with erupted vesicles or asymptomatic viral shedding.

**Q:** What is eczema herpeticum (Kaposi's varicelliform eruption)?

**A:** HSV infection on atopic dermatitis (AD) patients causing widespread vesicular eruption

This is a serious complication where HSV spreads extensively on skin compromised by atopic dermatitis.

**Q:** What investigations confirm HSV infection?

**A:** Tzanck smear with Giemsa stain (shows multinucleated giant epithelial cells), viral culture, PCR, direct fluorescence antibody test

Serologic testing can also determine current or past infection.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of head lice?

**A:** Nits (louse eggs) on hairs; red, excoriated skin with secondary bacterial infection; lymphadenopathy

Intense pruritus leads to excoriations, which can become secondarily infected.

**Q:** What are the contraindications for Doxycycline in acne vulgaris?

**A:** Pregnancy, hepatic impairment, drug hypersensitivity. Also contraindicated with acitretin, isotretinoin, or penicillin antibiotics. Avoid with oral typhoid vaccine.

Doxycycline is a tetracycline antibiotic used for acne vulgaris, rosacea, and bullous pemphigoid. Works via anti-inflammatory and anti-bacterial effects.

**Q:** What is the management of anterior urethral injury?

**A:** Conservative; if cannot void, Foley or suprapubic cystostomy and antibiotics

Most anterior urethral injuries can be managed non-operatively.

**Q:** What is the management of urethral injuries?

**A:** Anterior urethral injuries: conservative, or Foley/suprapubic cystostomy + antibiotics if cannot void. Posterior: suprapubic cystostomy (avoid catheterization) + surgical repair

Posterior urethral injuries are often associated with pelvic fractures. Catheterization should be avoided as it may worsen injury.

**Q:** What is the management for suspected spinal infection?

**A:** Early IV antibiotics and infectious disease/spine surgery consultation

Spinal infections require prompt antimicrobial therapy and surgical evaluation

**Q:** What is the management of an anterior urethral injury?

**A:** Conservative management; if unable to void, Foley or suprapubic cystostomy with antibiotics

Anterior urethral injuries are typically managed conservatively; catheterization or suprapubic cystostomy is needed if urinary retention occurs.

**Q:** What is the management for spinal infection?

**A:** Early IV antibiotics and infectious disease/spine surgery consultation

Spinal infections require prompt antimicrobial therapy and surgical assessment for potential debridement.

**Q:** List common precipitating factors for HHS

**A:** Infection (pneumonia, UTI, sepsis), missed insulin doses, cardiac surgery, and drugs (diuretics, glucocorticoids, lithium, atypical antipsychotics)

Identifying and treating the precipitating cause is essential in HHS management, similar to DKA, as it prevents ongoing hyperglycemic stress.

**Q:** What is the management of anterior urethra injury?

**A:** Conservative; if cannot void, Foley or suprapubic cystostomy and antibiotics

Anterior urethral injuries are often managed conservatively unless obstruction occurs.

**Q:** What is the management of spinal infection?

**A:** Early IV antibiotics and infectious disease/spine surgery consultation

Delayed treatment of spinal infection can lead to neurological compromise and sepsis.

**Q:** What is the management for spinal infection causing back pain?

**A:** Early IV antibiotics and infectious disease/spine surgery consultation

Spinal infections (osteomyelitis, discitis, epidural abscess) require prompt antimicrobial therapy and surgical drainage if indicated.

**Q:** What causes secondary low HDL?

**A:** Obesity/metabolic syndrome, DM, acute infections, inflammatory conditions

Low HDL is often associated with metabolic syndrome and is a target for lifestyle intervention.

**Q:** What are causes of low HDL?

**A:** Obesity/metabolic syndrome, DM, smoking, drugs (beta-blockers without ISA, anabolic steroids), acute infections, inflammatory conditions

Low HDL is an independent risk factor for CAD, especially in patients with DM and post-menopausal women.

**Q:** What are the causes of low HDL cholesterol?

**A:** Obesity/metabolic syndrome, diabetes, drugs (beta-blockers, anabolic steroids), acute infections, inflammatory conditions, smoking

Low HDL is an important independent cardiovascular risk factor that requires management of underlying causes.

**Q:** What is complete androgen insensitivity syndrome (CAIS)?

**A:** A condition where androgen receptors are completely non-functional, leading to female external genitalia despite XY karyotype

Mutations in the androgen receptor gene prevent androgen action, resulting in phenotypic female with intra-abdominal testes.

**Q:** What is the foot exam recommendation for diabetes patients?

**A:** Foot exam for sensation using 10g monofilament, ulcers or infection

Regular foot exams are essential to prevent diabetic foot complications.

**Q:** A patient with fever presents with dysuria, foul-smelling urine, and flank pain. What investigations are indicated?

**A:** Urinalysis, urine culture, and consider CBC

These symptoms suggest UTI/pyelonephritis; urine culture is gold standard for diagnosis.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for suspected meningitis?

**A:** CBC, blood culture, and lumbar puncture for CSF analysis

LP is essential for definitive diagnosis unless contraindicated by increased ICP signs.

**Q:** What are the three most common infections associated with vaginal discharge in adult women?

**A:** Bacterial vaginosis (BV), vulvovaginal candidiasis (VVC), and trichomoniasis

These three conditions account for the majority of vaginal discharge complaints in adult women.

**Q:** What are the three most common infections causing vaginal discharge in adult women?

**A:** Vulvovaginal candidiasis (VVC), bacterial vaginosis (BV), trichomoniasis

These are the three most common causes of vaginal discharge in adult women.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for primary herpes simplex virus infection?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 5-10 days, OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID x 5 days, OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID x 10 days

Antiviral therapy reduces symptom duration and viral shedding. Treatment is most effective when started within 72 hours of lesion onset.

**Q:** What is the foot exam recommendation for diabetic patients?

**A:** Foot exam for sensation using 10g monofilament, ulcers or infection assessment

Regular foot examinations using monofilament testing are essential to detect peripheral neuropathy and prevent diabetic foot ulcers.

**Q:** What are second-line antibiotics for cellulitis in adults?

**A:** Cloxacillin 500 mg PO QID OR clindamycin 300 mg PO for 10-14 days

Clindamycin is useful for penicillin-allergic patients or suspected MRSA.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for suspected secondary headache?

**A:** CBC (for infection), ESR/CRP (for temporal arteritis), neuroimaging (CT/MRI), and CSF analysis

Investigations are only indicated when red flags suggest secondary etiology; neuroimaging rules out intracranial pathology.

**Q:** What antibiotic is first-line for severe Campylobacter jejuni infection?

**A:** Macrolides (e.g., azithromycin) or fluoroquinolones

Macrolides are preferred; fluoroquinolones are alternatives, with treatment duration typically about 1 week for diarrhea >1 week.

**Q:** What is the treatment for C. difficile infection?

**A:** Stop the culprit antibiotic if possible; oral vancomycin is first-line, with IV metronidazole if ileus present; fidaxomicin is an alternative

For fulminant C. difficile, oral vancomycin plus IV metronidazole is recommended; fidaxomicin has lower recurrence rates.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Yersinia infection?

**A:** Fluoroquinolones only for severe illness

Most Yersinia cases are self-limiting; fluoroquinolones are reserved for severe illness only.

**Q:** What is the typical duration of EIEC infection?

**A:** 1-10 days

Enteroinvasive E. coli (EIEC) causes dysentery-like illness with bloody stool, abdominal pain, and fever; relatively uncommon.

**Q:** What is the association between Campylobacter infection and Guillain-Barré syndrome?

**A:** Campylobacter infection is associated with Guillain-Barré syndrome as a post-infectious complication

Campylobacter jejuni infection can trigger Guillain-Barré syndrome through molecular mimicry.

**Q:** What is the first step in managing C. difficile infection?

**A:** Stop the culprit antibiotic if possible

Discontinuing the inciting antibiotic is the first step in C. difficile treatment, along with supportive therapy.

**Q:** What are first-line antibiotics for C. difficile infection?

**A:** Oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin; metronidazole if access is limited

Vancomycin and fidaxomicin are first-line for C. difficile; metronidazole is reserved for mild cases when other options are unavailable.

**Q:** What is the characteristic complication of C. difficile infection?

**A:** Pseudomembranous colitis

C. difficile toxins cause pseudomembranous colitis, characterized by adherent yellow-white plaques on colonoscopy.

**Q:** What antibiotics are used for ETEC (traveller's diarrhea)?

**A:** Fluoroquinolone or azithromycin

Fluoroquinolones and azithromycin are effective against ETEC, though fluoroquinolone resistance is increasing in some regions.

**Q:** What antibiotics are used for Salmonella Typhi?

**A:** Ceftriaxone, ciprofloxacin, or azithromycin

Empiric treatment for typhoid uses ceftriaxone, ciprofloxacin, or azithromycin; fluoroquinolone resistance is increasing.

**Q:** What is unique about Shigella infection?

**A:** Very small inoculum needed for infection

Shigella is highly transmissible with as few as 10-100 organisms needed to cause infection.

**Q:** What complications can occur with Shigella infection?

**A:** Toxic megacolon and HUS

Shigella dysenteriae can cause toxic megacolon; antidiarrheals may increase this risk.

**Q:** What is the characteristic stool output in Vibrio cholerae infection?

**A:** Massive watery diarrhea (1-3 liters per day)

Cholera causes profuse watery diarrhea that can rapidly lead to severe dehydration and death without treatment.

**Q:** What antibiotics are used for Vibrio cholerae?

**A:** Tetracycline or fluoroquinolones (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Tetracycline and fluoroquinolones are effective against V. cholerae and reduce bacterial shedding.

**Q:** What is the treatment for severe Yersinia infection?

**A:** Fluoroquinolones for severe illness

Fluoroquinolones are used for severe Yersinia infections; most cases in children are self-limited.

**Q:** What is the main concern with rotavirus infection?

**A:** Severe dehydration

Rotavirus can cause profuse watery diarrhea leading to severe dehydration, particularly in young children.

**Q:** Why is antibiotic prophylaxis not recommended for traveller's diarrhea?

**A:** Increases risk of infection with resistant organisms

Prophylactic antibiotics promote resistance and may lead to infection with resistant organisms; not recommended by CDC.

**Q:** What stool investigations are indicated for chronic diarrhea?

**A:** C. difficile toxin and culture/sensitivity

Stool analysis for C. difficile toxin and bacterial culture are initial investigations guided by history in chronic diarrhea.

**Q:** What antibiotic is used for fulminant C. difficile infection?

**A:** Oral vancomycin

For fulminant C. difficile infection (previously called severe), oral vancomycin is used, with IV metronidazole added if ileus is present.

**Q:** What investigation is recommended for chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Stool analysis for C. difficile toxin and culture/sensitivity

Investigations for chronic diarrhea are guided by history, with stool analysis for C. difficile toxin and C&S being key initial tests.

**Q:** Why are antidiarrheals contraindicated in Shigella infection?

**A:** They increase the risk of toxic megacolon

Antidiarrheals may increase the risk of toxic megacolon in Shigella infection and should be avoided despite the pathogen requiring only a very small inoculum for transmission.

**Q:** What antibiotic is typically used for severe non-typhoidal salmonellosis?

**A:** Ciprofloxacin only in severe illness

Ciprofloxacin is reserved for severe non-typhoidal salmonellosis in extreme age, joint prostheses, valvular heart disease, severe atherosclerosis, cancer, or uremia.

**Q:** What are the three most common infections associated with vaginal discharge?

**A:** Bacterial vaginosis, vulvovaginal candidiasis, and trichomoniasis

These cause the majority of vaginal discharge presentations in adult women.

**Q:** What is the characteristic stool pattern in C. difficile infection?

**A:** Pseudomembranous colitis

C. difficile can cause pseudomembranous colitis, characterized by pseudomembranes visible on colonoscopy.

**Q:** What antibiotic is first-line for C. difficile infection?

**A:** Oral vancomycin

For C. difficile infection, oral vancomycin is the first-line treatment; fidaxomicin is an alternative.

**Q:** What is the infective dose of Shigella (how many organisms needed for infection)?

**A:** Very small inoculum (as few as 10-100 organisms)

Shigella has a very low infective dose, making it highly transmissible via fecal-oral route.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Giardia lamblia infection?

**A:** Metronidazole or nitazoxanide

Giardia is treated with metronidazole or nitazoxanide; treatment of asymptomatic carriers is NOT recommended.

**Q:** What is the complication of untreated Entamoeba histolytica infection?

**A:** Liver abscess

Entamoeba histolytica can spread hematogenously to cause amoebic liver abscess if untreated.

**Q:** What pathogen is normally present in colon in small numbers and causes disease after antibiotic exposure?

**A:** Clostridioides difficile (C. difficile)

C. difficile is normally present in low numbers; disease occurs after antibiotic exposure disrupts flora.

**Q:** Which antibiotics are highest risk for C. difficile infection?

**A:** Clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, cephalosporins

Broad-spectrum antibiotics especially clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, and cephalosporins disrupt colonic flora.

**Q:** What is the treatment for first-episode herpes simplex virus (HSV) infection?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 5-10 days OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID x 5 days OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID x 10 days

Antiviral therapy reduces symptom duration, viral shedding, and speeds healing of lesions.

**Q:** A patient with recurrent C. difficile infection is being treated. Which antibiotic has the lowest recurrence rate?

**A:** Fidaxomicin

Fidaxomicin has a lower recurrence rate compared to vancomycin for C. difficile infection due to its narrow spectrum and inhibition of spore production.

**Q:** What are the 'rose spots' associated with Salmonella typhi infection and when do they appear?

**A:** Pink blanching maculopapular rash on the anterior thorax and upper abdomen; appears in the second week of illness

Salmonella typhi (typhoid fever) causes rose spots - transient, blanching erythematous papules that appear on the trunk during the second week of fever.

**Q:** Which bacterial pathogen requires the smallest inoculum to cause infection?

**A:** Shigella

Shigella requires a very small inoculum (as few as 10-100 organisms) to cause infection due to its ability to invade colonic epithelium.

**Q:** What antibiotics are associated with C. difficile infection?

**A:** Clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, and cephalosporins

These antibiotics disrupt normal colonic flora, allowing C. difficile to overgrow and produce toxins. Recent hospitalization within 6 weeks is also a risk factor.

**Q:** A traveler to Mexico develops moderate diarrhea. What is the first-line empiric antibiotic?

**A:** Azithromycin (given increasing fluoroquinolone resistance)

In areas with high fluoroquinolone resistance (e.g., South and Southeast Asia), azithromycin is preferred for empiric treatment of traveler's diarrhea.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Shigella infection?

**A:** Fluoroquinolones (e.g., ciprofloxacin)

Fluoroquinolones are first-line for Shigella infection. Supportive therapy is important, but antidiarrheals should be avoided due to risk of toxic megacolon.

**Q:** What is the prevention strategy for traveler's diarrhea (non-antibiotic)?

**A:** Avoid high-risk foods/beverages, proper hygiene, bismuth subsalicylate (Pepto-Bismol) 60% effective

Prevention includes avoiding street vendor food, unbottled water, raw fruits without peels, and taking bismuth subsalicylate 2 tablets QID.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Campylobacter jejuni infection?

**A:** Macrolides (e.g., azithromycin) or fluoroquinolones if susceptible

Macrolides are first-line for Campylobacter. Fluoroquinolone resistance is increasing, so empiric treatment should consider local resistance patterns.

**Q:** What neurological complication is associated with Campylobacter jejuni infection?

**A:** Guillain-Barré syndrome

Campylobacter jejuni infection is a trigger for Guillain-Barré syndrome due to molecular mimicry between bacterial and neural antigens.

**Q:** What complication can develop from Clostridioides difficile infection?

**A:** Pseudomembranous colitis

C. difficile infection can lead to pseudomembranous colitis, characterized by inflammatory plaques on the colonic mucosa.

**Q:** Should antibiotics be used to treat EHEC (STEC) diarrhea?

**A:** No - antibiotics increase risk of HUS

Antibiotics are NOT recommended for EHEC/STEC infection as they increase the risk of HUS by promoting shiga toxin release.

**Q:** What is the characteristic rash seen in Salmonella Typhi (typhoid fever) infection?

**A:** Rose spots - blanching erythematous macules on the anterior thorax and upper abdomen

Rose spots are a characteristic finding in typhoid fever, typically appearing on the trunk as small, blanching erythematous papules.

**Q:** What is notable about the infectious dose of Shigella?

**A:** Very small inoculum needed for infection (as few as 10-100 organisms)

Shigella requires a very small inoculum to cause infection, making it highly transmissible via person-to-person spread.

**Q:** What is the typical daily stool volume in severe Vibrio cholerae infection?

**A:** 1-3 liters per day (massive watery diarrhea)

Vibrio cholerae causes profuse watery diarrhea with stool volumes up to 1-3 liters daily, which can rapidly cause life-threatening dehydration.

**Q:** What should be included in stool analysis for chronic diarrhea?

**A:** C. difficile toxin and culture & sensitivity (C&S)

Stool analysis for chronic diarrhea should include C. difficile toxin testing and culture & sensitivity to identify bacterial pathogens.

**Q:** What antibiotic is first-line for fulminant C. difficile infection?

**A:** Oral vancomycin (with IV metronidazole if ileus present)

For fulminant C. difficile infection, oral vancomycin is used; IV metronidazole is added to the regimen if ileus is present.

**Q:** What is unique about Shigella infection requiring treatment?

**A:** Very small inoculum needed for infection

Shigella requires a very small inoculum to cause infection, making it highly contagious. Complications include toxic megacolon and HUS.

**Q:** Which antibiotic is typically used for severe Campylobacter jejuni infection?

**A:** Macrolide (e.g., azithromycin) or fluoroquinolone if diarrhea >1 week, bloody, or immunocompromised

Campylobacter jejuni is treated with macrolides or fluoroquinolones when diarrhea is prolonged (>1 week), bloody, or in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** Why are antibiotics contraindicated in EHEC/STEC infection?

**A:** Antibiotics increase the risk of HUS

Antibiotics increase the risk of hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) in EHEC/STEC infection, so treatment is supportive and antidiarrheals should be avoided.

**Q:** Which antibiotics are typically avoided in patients developing C. difficile?

**A:** Clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, and cephalosporins

C. difficile infection typically follows treatment with clindamycin, fluoroquinolones, penicillins, or cephalosporins; these should be stopped if possible when C. difficile develops.

**Q:** What is Ascending Cholangitis?

**A:** Infection of the biliary tree due to stasis from obstruction or stricture

Most commonly caused by E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Enterococcus, with possible coinfection by Bacteroides and Clostridia.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of treated ascending cholangitis (without Reynolds' pentad)?

**A:** Good with effective drainage and antibiotics

Mild to moderate cases have good outcomes when promptly treated with drainage (preferably ERCP) and appropriate antibiotics.

**Q:** What bacteria cause ascending cholangitis?

**A:** E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Enterococcus; coinfection with Bacteroides and Clostridia can occur

Broad-spectrum antibiotics covering Gram-negatives, Enterococcus, and anaerobes are required

**Q:** What complication is associated with Shigella dysenteriae infection?

**A:** Toxic megacolon and HUS

Shigella can cause toxic megacolon, and antidiarrheals may increase this risk. HUS is also a complication.

**Q:** Which pathogen has the smallest inoculum required for infection?

**A:** Shigella

Shigella requires a very small inoculum for infection, making it highly transmissible through fecal-oral route.

**Q:** What is the typical duration of norovirus infection?

**A:** 24 hours

Norovirus causes self-limited gastroenteritis with 24-hour incubation period and 24-hour duration, with no specific treatment.

**Q:** What stool studies should be ordered for chronic diarrhea workup?

**A:** C. difficile toxin and culture & sensitivity (C&S)

Stool analysis for C. difficile toxin and C&S are part of the initial workup for chronic diarrhea, guided by history.

**Q:** What is the recommended antibiotic prophylaxis for cirrhotic patients with upper gastrointestinal bleeding?

**A:** Antibiotic prophylaxis is recommended for cirrhotic patients with upper GI bleeding to prevent bacterial infections and reduce rebleeding

A Cochrane updated review (Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2011) demonstrated that antibiotic prophylaxis improves outcomes in cirrhotics with variceal or non-variceal bleeding

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and treatment of cholera?

**A:** Cholera is an enteric infection caused by Vibrio cholerae, presenting with voluminous watery diarrhea, and treated with rehydration (oral and IV) and antibiotics

A review (Lancet 2012) covers cholera pathophysiology, transmission, clinical presentation, and treatment approaches including antibiotic therapy

**Q:** What antibiotics should be used for Ascending Cholangitis?

**A:** Broad-spectrum antibiotics covering Gram-negatives, Enterococcus, and anaerobes (e.g., ampicillin-sulbactam, piperacillin-tazobactam, or metronidazole + 3rd gen cephalosporin)

No clear consensus exists; consider coverage especially when CBD manipulation is anticipated.

**Q:** What is the infection rate for dirty/infected surgical wounds?

**A:** Approximately 30-40%.

Dirty/infected wounds (established infection before surgery or traumatic wounds with devitalized tissue) have the highest infection rate at 30-40%.

**Q:** What are the procedure-related risk factors for surgical site infection?

**A:** Prolonged preoperative hospitalization, inadequate skin preparation, multiple antibiotics, reduced blood flow, breaks in sterile technique, foreign bodies (drains, sutures), excessive tension, hematoma, seroma, hypoxemia, and hypothermia.

These factors create an environment conducive to bacterial growth or compromise host defenses at the surgical site.

**Q:** What are the non-antibiotic measures to prevent surgical site infection?

**A:** Normothermia (maintain 36-38°C), hyperoxygenation (FiO2 80%), chlorhexidine-alcohol skin wash, hair removal only if necessary (clipping preferred over shaving), and consider delayed primary closure for contaminated wounds.

These measures optimize host defenses and reduce bacterial load through physical and physiological optimization.

**Q:** When do surgical site infections typically present postoperatively?

**A:** Postoperative day 5-8, though Streptococcus and Clostridium can present within 24 hours.

Most SSIs present in the first week after surgery, but early-onset infections within 24-48 hours suggest highly virulent organisms like Streptococcus or Clostridium.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of surgical site infection?

**A:** Fever, localized pain, blanchable erythema, induration, purulent discharge, and warmth.

These classic signs of infection result from the inflammatory response to bacterial contamination of the surgical wound.

**Q:** What are the complications of surgical site infection?

**A:** Fistula, sinus tracts, sepsis, abscess, suppressed wound healing, superinfection, necrotizing fasciitis, wound dehiscence, evisceration, and hernia.

SSI can progress to deep tissue involvement and systemic complications if not adequately treated, potentially becoming life-threatening.

**Q:** What is the treatment for established surgical site infection?

**A:** Examine the wound (inspect, compress, swab drainage for C&S), reopen affected portion, drain and pack to heal by secondary intention; debride necrotic tissue for deeper infections; antibiotics only if cellulitis or immunodeficiency.

Treatment focuses on source control—drainage and removal of infected tissue—plus antibiotics for selected cases. Most SSIs heal by secondary intention.

**Q:** What organisms commonly cause surgical site infections from skin flora?

**A:** Gram-positive cocci including Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus species.

These are the most common pathogens in clean surgeries where endogenous flora from skin is the source of contamination.

**Q:** When should postoperative antibiotics be reserved for in SSI management?

**A:** Postoperative antibiotics should be reserved for treatment of suspected or documented intra-abdominal infection, not routine prophylaxis.

Unnecessary postoperative antibiotics contribute to resistance without benefit; they should only be used when treating established infection.

**Q:** What is the sensitivity and specificity of Anti-CCP for Rheumatoid Arthritis?

**A:** Sensitivity 80%, Specificity 94-98%

Anti-CCP is highly specific for RA and may be useful in early disease to predict persistent and erosive disease.

**Q:** What is the sensitivity and specificity of ANCA (anti-PR3 by ELISA) for Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis?

**A:** Sensitivity 90%, Specificity 80-95%

ANCA testing using ELISA method (anti-PR3) is more specific than immunofluorescence technique.

**Q:** Why is Rheumatoid Arthritis considered an independent risk factor for cardiovascular disease?

**A:** RA is associated with increased overall mortality and morbidity from all causes, particularly cardiovascular disease, neoplasms (especially lymphoma), and infection

The chronic inflammatory state in RA contributes to accelerated atherosclerosis.

**Q:** What is the clinical significance of a negative ANA test in a patient suspected of having SLE?

**A:** A negative ANA can help rule out SLE due to its high sensitivity (98%)

Given the high false positive rate, ANA should only be measured when there is high pre-test probability.

**Q:** What is the first-line imaging study for hydronephrosis and its sensitivity/specificity?

**A:** Ultrasound - greater than 90% sensitive and specific

Ultrasound is the initial imaging modality of choice due to its high sensitivity and specificity for detecting hydronephrosis without radiation exposure.

**Q:** What is the first-line antibiotic for uncomplicated UTI?

**A:** TMP-SMX (160 mg/800 mg PO BID) for 3 days, or nitrofurantoin (100 mg PO BID) for 5 days, or fosfomycin (3g PO single dose)

Choice should be based on local resistance patterns and adjusted according to culture and sensitivity results.

**Q:** What is the definition of asymptomatic bacteriuria?

**A:** Asymptomatic with 100,000 CFU/mL of a single organism on urine culture

Asymptomatic bacteriuria only requires treatment in specific patients (pregnancy, immunosuppressed, prior to urologic surgery).

**Q:** When should cystoscopy be considered in overactive bladder evaluation?

**A:** To rule out recurrent infections, carcinoma in situ, and other intravesical abnormalities

Cystoscopy is indicated when there is suspicion of structural or malignant pathology.

**Q:** What laboratory investigations are needed for hydronephrosis?

**A:** CBC, electrolytes, creatinine, BUN, urinalysis, and urine culture and sensitivity

These tests evaluate for infection, renal function impairment, and guide antibiotic therapy if needed.

## General

**Q:** What infectious contacts and exposures should be asked about in fever history?

**A:** Travel history, camping, daycare, TB contact, foodborne illness, animal exposure

Exposure history helps identify potential infectious etiologies.

**Q:** How is recurrent herpes simplex virus episode treated?

**A:** Acyclovir 800 mg PO BID x 5 days, OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID x 3 days, OR famciclovir 1000 mg PO single dose

Recurrent episodes are treated with shorter courses or single doses compared to primary infection, as viral burden is lower.

**Q:** What is the treatment for gonorrhea?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 250 mg IM single dose + azithromycin 1 g PO single dose

Dual therapy is recommended due to increasing antibiotic resistance. Patients should abstain from intercourse for 1 week after treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for uncomplicated chlamydia?

**A:** Doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 7 days

Azithromycin 1 g PO single dose is an alternative but doxycycline has better efficacy. Patients should abstain from intercourse for 1 week after treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for mastitis?

**A:** Cloxacillin 500mg PO QID x 7 days OR Cephalexin 500mg PO QID x 7 days

S. aureus is the most common cause of mastitis. Antibiotics covering penicillinase-producing staphylococci are first-line.

**Q:** What is the treatment for first-episode genital herpes (HSV)?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID for 5-10 days, OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID for 5 days, OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID for 10 days

Antiviral therapy reduces symptoms duration and viral shedding. Treatment is most effective when started early.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chlamydia?

**A:** Doxycycline 100 mg PO BID for 7 days

Azithromycin 1 g PO single dose is an alternative. Patients should abstain from intercourse for 1 week after treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for mastitis caused by Staphylococcus aureus?

**A:** Cloxacillin 500 mg PO QID for 7 days

S. aureus is the most common cause of mastitis. Oral antibiotics are usually sufficient for uncomplicated cases.

**Q:** What is the treatment for mastitis caused by Streptococcus pyogenes?

**A:** Cephalexin 500 mg PO QID for 7 days

Streptococcus pyogenes is an alternative cause of mastitis. Cephalexin covers this organism effectively.

**Q:** What is the treatment for primary episode herpes genitalis?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x5-10 days OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID x5 days OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID x10 days

First-line antiviral therapy for primary HSV infection reduces symptoms and healing time.

**Q:** What is the treatment for recurrent herpes genitalis episode?

**A:** Acyclovir 800 mg PO TID x2 days OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID x3 days OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO daily x5 days

Shorter courses of antivirals are effective for recurrent episodes.

**Q:** What is the treatment for uncomplicated gonorrhea?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 250 mg IM x1 dose + azithromycin 1g PO x1 dose

Dual therapy with ceftriaxone and azithromycin is recommended for gonorrhea.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for mastitis in adults?

**A:** Cloxacillin 500 mg PO QID x7 days (for S. aureus) OR cephalexin 500 mg PO QID x7 days (for S. pyogenes)

Mastitis is typically caused by S. aureus; antibiotics should cover penicillinase-producing staphylococci.

**Q:** What are common causes of mastitis?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes

These are the primary bacterial causes of mastitis in breastfeeding and non-breastfeeding patients.

**Q:** What pathogens cause gonorrhea and chlamydia?

**A:** Neisseria gonorrhoeae causes gonorrhea; Chlamydia trachomatis causes chlamydia

These are the causative organisms for the two most common bacterial STDs.

**Q:** What virus causes genital herpes?

**A:** Herpes simplex virus (HSV) - primarily HSV-2, but HSV-1 can also cause genital herpes

HSV-2 is the most common cause of recurrent genital herpes, though HSV-1 increasingly causes primary genital infections.

**Q:** What counseling should be given after treatment for gonorrhea or chlamydia?

**A:** No intercourse for 1 week after treatment completion

Patients should abstain from sexual activity until treatment is completed and symptoms resolve to prevent reinfection.

**Q:** What advice should be given after chlamydia or gonorrhea treatment?

**A:** No intercourse for 1 week after treatment

This prevents reinfection and allows healing of mucosal surfaces.

**Q:** What is the treatment for a pregnant patient with recurrent genital herpes near delivery?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID starting at 36 weeks gestation

Suppressive therapy at 36 weeks reduces viral shedding and cesarean section rates for women with recurrent herpes.

**Q:** What organisms cause mastitis?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus (most common) and Streptococcus pyogenes

S. aureus is the predominant pathogen in mastitis. Culture may be needed for recurrent or treatment-resistant cases.

**Q:** What is the treatment for recurrent genital herpes with famciclovir?

**A:** Famciclovir 1000 mg PO BID x 1 day OR 125 mg PO BID x 5 days OR 500 mg once followed by 250 mg BID x 2 days

Multiple famciclovir regimens are effective for recurrent episodes.

**Q:** What is the treatment for primary episode herpes simplex virus?

**A:** Acyclovir 400mg PO TID x 5-10 days OR famciclovir 250mg PO TID x 5 days OR valacyclovir 1000mg PO BID x 10 days

Antiviral treatment reduces symptoms and viral shedding duration. Start within 72 hours of lesion onset for maximum benefit.

**Q:** What is the treatment for recurrent herpes simplex virus episodes?

**A:** Acyclovir 800mg PO TID x 2 days OR valacyclovir 500mg PO BID x 3 days

Valacyclovir 500mg BID x 3 days or 1000mg daily x 5 days are also effective. Earlier treatment leads to faster resolution.

**Q:** What organisms cause mastitis and what is first-line treatment?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus (most common) and Streptococcus pyogenes. First-line: cloxacillin 500mg PO QID x 7 days

Cloxacillin is first-line for S. aureus. For suspected S. pyogenes, use cephalexin 500mg PO QID x 7 days.

**Q:** What is the treatment for first-episode herpes simplex virus (HSV)?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 5-10 days, OR famciclovir 250 mg PO TID x 5 days, OR valacyclovir 1000 mg PO BID x 10 days

Antiviral therapy reduces symptoms duration and viral shedding. Earlier treatment provides better outcomes.

**Q:** What are the causes of mastitis?

**A:** Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes

These are the two most common bacterial causes of mastitis, typically resulting from breastfeeding-related nipple cracking or milk stasis.

## Infectious Disease

**Q:** What are the three major endemic mycoses in North America and their geographic distributions?

**A:** 1. Histoplasma capsulatum: Ohio/Mississippi River valleys, Ontario, Quebec (associated with chicken coops, bird roosts, bat caves). 2. Blastomyces dermatitidis: East of Mississippi, Northern Ontario, Great Lakes. 3. Coccidioides immitis: Southwestern US deserts, northwestern Mexico ('Valley fever').

All are thermally dimorphic (mould in cold/soil, yeast at body temperature), acquired by inhalation of spores, can cause pneumonia and disseminate hematogenously, and may reactivate during immunocompromised states. Previously defined regions of endemicity are expanding.

**Q:** What is the classic presentation and treatment of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP)?

**A:** Presentation: fever, non-productive cough, progressive dyspnea/hypoxia. CXR: bilateral symmetric interstitial infiltrates. Labs: elevated LDH (>220 IU/L), reduced pO2, elevated A-a gradient. Treatment: TMP/SMX (first-line), add corticosteroids if pO2 <70 mmHg. Prophylaxis: TMP/SMX when CD4 <200 in HIV patients.

PJP occurs in HIV patients with CD4 <200 who lack prophylaxis, and in non-HIV immunocompromised patients. Most clinical disease is due to reactivation. Without prophylaxis, HIV+ patients with CD4 <200 have an 80% lifetime risk. Adjunctive steroids improve survival in severe hypoxia.

**Q:** What is the leading cause of meningitis in HIV patients, and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Cryptococcus neoformans (most commonly). Diagnosis: serum cryptococcal antigen, CSF India-ink stain (50% sensitivity, higher in HIV), CSF cryptococcal antigen test (high sensitivity), LP with opening pressure measurement. Treatment: amphotericin B + flucytosine (2-week induction) → fluconazole (consolidation ≥8 weeks → prolonged maintenance).

C. neoformans is acquired by inhaling encapsulated yeast from soil contaminated with pigeon droppings. It tends to affect immunocompromised hosts while C. gattii affects immunocompetent hosts. Serial lumbar puncture for ICP management is an important adjunct to therapy.

**Q:** What are the clinical forms of aspergillosis and their presentations?
