**A:** 1. ABPA: IgE-mediated asthma-like reaction, transient pulmonary infiltrates (patients with asthma/allergies). 2. Aspergilloma: fungus ball in pre-existing cavity, 'air crescent' sign on CXR, asymptomatic to massive hemoptysis. 3. Invasive aspergillosis: prolonged neutropenia, pneumonia with 'halo' sign on CT, may disseminate to brain/skin, high mortality. 4. Mycotoxicosis: hepatic effects from aflatoxin → hepatocellular carcinoma.

Treatment differs by form: ABPA = corticosteroids + itraconazole; aspergilloma = surgical resection ± bronchial artery embolization; invasive = voriconazole (first-line) or amphotericin B. Invasive aspergillosis is associated with persistent neutropenia or transplantation.

**Q:** What is the life cycle of Plasmodium malaria and which species can relapse from liver hypnozoites?

**A:** Cycle: infected Anopheles mosquito injects sporozoites → liver infection → merozoites released → RBC infection → RBC lysis (fever paroxysms). P. vivax and P. ovale can relapse from dormant liver hypnozoites months later. Treatment of hypnozoites requires primaquine or tafenoquine.

P. falciparum is the most lethal species causing cerebral malaria, severe anemia, and ARDS. Malaria is the most common fatal infectious disease imported into Canada. In returned travellers from endemic areas, fever is malaria until proven otherwise. Diagnosis requires thick and thin blood smears ×3 (q12-24h).

**Q:** What is the treatment for P. falciparum malaria?

**A:** Most areas show chloroquine resistance. Options: 1) Artemisinin combination therapy (artesunate + doxycycline, or artemether-lumefantrine). 2) Atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone). 3) Quinine + doxycycline or clindamycin. For severe disease: parenteral artesunate. Check local resistance patterns.

Markers of severity include clinical features plus degree of parasitemia. P. falciparum causes >90% of malaria deaths. Artemisinin and mefloquine resistance is increasing in Southeast Asia. Prevention includes antimalarial prophylaxis, insecticide-treated bed nets, DEET repellent, and covering exposed skin.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of congenital toxoplasmosis?

**A:** Chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus, and intracranial calcifications. Transmitted transplacentally during acute primary maternal infection in pregnancy.

Toxoplasma gondii is acquired from cat feces (oocysts), undercooked meat (tissue cysts), or transplacentally. In immunocompetent patients it is usually asymptomatic. In immunocompromised (AIDS with CD4 <200), it causes encephalitis with ring-enhancing lesions on CT. Prevention in pregnancy: avoid undercooked meat and cat litter.

**Q:** What is the presentation and treatment of Giardia lamblia infection?

**A:** Presentation ('beaver fever'): watery diarrhea, steatorrhea, bloating, flatulence, abdominal cramps, weight loss. No blood in stool (no tissue invasion). Chronic infection causes malabsorption. Diagnosis: multiple stool samples for microscopy, stool antigen, multiplex PCR. Treatment: single dose tinidazole or 3-day nitazoxanide; metronidazole for children <12 months.

Giardia is transmitted via the fecal-oral route through contaminated water/food. Risk factors include travel, camping, daycare centres, institutions, and MSM. Key distinguishing feature from amoebic dysentery: no hematochezia because Giardia does not invade the intestinal wall.

**Q:** What parasitic infection is associated with Löffler syndrome, and what is the mechanism?

**A:** Ascaris lumbricoides and Strongyloides stercoralis. Löffler syndrome (eosinophilic pneumonitis): cough, dyspnea, transient pulmonary infiltrates, and peripheral eosinophilia caused by larval migration through the lungs.

The larvae of intestinal nematodes migrate through the lungs as part of their life cycle, provoking an eosinophilic inflammatory response. Strongyloides is unique among nematodes in its ability to autoinfect the host, potentially causing hyperinfection syndrome in immunocompromised patients (especially with corticosteroids).

**Q:** What tapeworm causes cysticercosis and what is the clinical presentation?

**A:** Taenia solium (pork tapeworm). Cysticercosis occurs from ingesting eggs (not larvae), which form mass lesions in CNS, eyes, and skin. Neurocysticercosis causes seizures (most common presentation), headache, and focal neurological deficits. Treatment: corticosteroids + albendazole + praziquantel; antiepileptics for seizures.

Intestinal taeniasis (from eating undercooked pork containing larvae) causes mild GI symptoms. Cysticercosis (from fecal-oral transmission of eggs) is the serious form. Diphyllobothrium latum (fish tapeworm) causes vitamin B12 deficiency and macrocytic anemia.

**Q:** What are the chronic complications of Schistosoma infection by species?

**A:** S. mansoni/S. japonicum: worms in mesenteric veins → intestinal polyps, portal HTN, hepatosplenomegaly, pulmonary HTN. S. haematobium: worms in vesical plexus → hematuria, obstructive uropathy, squamous cell carcinoma of the bladder. All species: neurologic complications (transverse myelitis, seizures).

Schistosomiasis is acquired through fresh water exposure in endemic areas (Africa, Southeast Asia). Acute infection (Katayama fever) presents with fever, hives, eosinophilia. Diagnosis: serology, eggs in stool/urine, eosinophilia. Treatment: praziquantel; add prednisone for acute schistosomiasis.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis for fever in a returned traveller with incubation period <21 days?

**A:** Malaria (most important to exclude), typhoid fever, dengue fever, chikungunya, Zika, rickettsioses, traveller's diarrhea, respiratory tract infections (including COVID-19). Must also exclude HBV and TB.

Fever in a returned traveller from a malaria-endemic area is malaria until proven otherwise. All travellers with fever need CBC, thick and thin blood smears ×3, blood cultures. Incubation >21 days makes dengue, chikungunya, and traveller's diarrhea less likely but does not exclude malaria or typhoid.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of fever of unknown origin (FUO)?

**A:** Infections (~30%): TB (especially extrapulmonary), abscess, osteomyelitis, endocarditis. Neoplasms (~20%): lymphomas (especially NHL), leukemias, renal cell carcinoma. Collagen vascular diseases (~30%): SLE, RA, temporal arteritis, vasculitis. Miscellaneous (~20%): drug fever, sarcoidosis, IBD, PE/DVT. Unknown in 30-50% despite workup.

FUO is defined as temperature ≥38.3°C on several occasions, duration >3 weeks, and diagnosis uncertain after 3 outpatient visits or 3 days in hospital. TB is the most common infectious cause. Patients who remain undiagnosed despite extensive workup generally have a good prognosis.

**Q:** What types of infections are patients with cell-mediated vs. humoral immune deficiency susceptible to?

**A:** Cell-mediated (HIV, Hodgkin, cytotoxic drugs): latent viruses (CMV, HSV, VZV), fungi (Candida, Aspergillus, PJP, Cryptococcus), parasites (Toxoplasma), NTM. Humoral (CLL, myeloma, asplenia, sickle cell): encapsulated organisms (S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, N. meningitidis, Salmonella, GBS).

Neutrophil dysfunction (chemotherapy, chronic granulomatous disease) predisposes to catalase-producing organisms: Staphylococcus, Serratia, Nocardia, Aspergillus. Understanding immune deficiency patterns is critical for predicting likely pathogens and selecting empiric therapy.

**Q:** What defines febrile neutropenia and what is the initial management?

**A:** Definition: fever ≥38.3°C (or ≥38.0°C for ≥1h) + ANC <1.0 × 10⁹/L (severe if <0.5). Workup: blood cultures ×2, urine C&S, culture all catheter ports, CBC, electrolytes, Cr, liver enzymes, chest imaging. Do NOT perform DRE. Treatment: empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics per local protocol. Prophylaxis with G-CSF if risk of FN ≥20%.

Febrile neutropenia is the most common life-threatening complication of cancer chemotherapy. A causative organism is identified only 1/3 of the time. Gram-positive organisms are now more common than gram-negatives. Fungal superinfection (Candida, Aspergillus) occurs with prolonged neutropenia.

**Q:** What is immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome (IRIS)?

**A:** A harmful inflammatory response against a previously acquired infection following immune recovery (e.g., after starting ART in HIV or discontinuing immunosuppressive therapy). Can present as paradoxical worsening of treated infection or new response to previously subclinical infection. Worse with rapid CD4 increase and lower pre-treatment CD4.

IRIS affects ~10% of HIV patients starting ART. Common pathogens include TB, Cryptococcus, PJP, Toxoplasma, and hepatitis viruses. Diagnosis is by exclusion (rule out drug reaction, non-adherence, drug resistance). Management: continue ART (unless life-threatening), treat underlying infection, consider corticosteroids.

**Q:** What infections are common >1 month post solid organ transplant?

**A:** Viral: CMV, EBV, VZV. Fungal: Aspergillus, Cryptococcus, P. jirovecii. Protozoan: Toxoplasma. Bacterial/mycobacterial: TB, Nocardia, Listeria. Pre-transplant vaccinations: DTaP, pneumococcal, influenza, hepatitis A/B, COVID-19 for all; MMR, varicella, HPV in select patients.

Infection is a leading cause of early morbidity/mortality in transplant recipients. Infections <1 month are typically donor-derived or surgical site/line infections. The degree of immunosuppression determines susceptibility. Live vaccines (MMR, varicella) must be given pre-transplant as they are contraindicated post-transplant.

**Q:** What is Chagas disease (American trypanosomiasis) and what are its chronic complications?

**A:** Caused by Trypanosoma cruzi, transmitted by reduviid bug ('kissing bug') that defecates on skin. Acute: usually asymptomatic; Romaña sign (unilateral periorbital swelling). Chronic (30-40% of infected, 10-25 years later): dilated cardiomyopathy, megaesophagus, megacolon. 60-70% remain in asymptomatic indeterminate phase.

Found in Mexico, Central and South America. Congenital acquisition is increasingly recognized in non-endemic areas. Treatment with benznidazole for acute and indeterminate phases. Chronic determinate phase: symptomatic therapy, may need heart transplant or surgery. Screen seropositive patients before planned immunosuppression.

**Q:** What is the most common reportable STI in Canada, and what is the recommended treatment for concurrent gonococcal and chlamydial urethritis/cervicitis?

**A:** Chlamydia is the most common reportable STI. Treatment for concurrent infection: ceftriaxone 250 mg IM single dose + azithromycin 1 g PO single dose (always treat for both if either is suspected).

When urethritis or cervicitis is suspected, always treat for both gonococcal and non-gonococcal types simultaneously. Mandatory reporting applies to chlamydia, gonorrhea, hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, and chancroid.

**Q:** A 10-year-old presents with sore throat, fever 39C, tonsillar exudate, and tender anterior cervical nodes without cough. Using the Modified Centor Score, what is the score and recommended action?

**A:** Score: 5 (fever +1, tonsillar exudate +1, tender anterior nodes +1, cough absent +1, age 5-14 +1). Score >=3: perform rapid antigen detection test (RADT) and treat with antibiotics if positive.

The Modified Centor Score stratifies the risk of GABHS pharyngitis. Score 0-2 has low risk (1-8%) and needs no testing. Score >=3 has higher risk and warrants RADT. If RADT negative in patients <20, perform throat culture. Treatment is penicillin V for 10 days.

**Q:** A 22-year-old presents with infectious mononucleosis confirmed by positive monospot. What activity restriction is essential, and why?

**A:** Avoid heavy physical activity and contact sports for at least 1 month or until splenomegaly resolves, due to risk of splenic rupture.

EBV-related infectious mononucleosis causes splenomegaly which increases the risk of traumatic splenic rupture. Treatment is supportive with acetaminophen/NSAIDs. Antibiotics are not indicated. Ampicillin/amoxicillin should be avoided as it causes a maculopapular rash in EBV infection.

**Q:** What is the Modified Centor Score for strep pharyngitis, and how is it used?

**A:** Criteria (1 point each): cough absent, fever >38°C, tonsillar exudate, swollen/tender anterior nodes, age 5-14 (age 15-44=0, >45=-1). Score 0-2: no testing/antibiotics. Score ≥3: rapid antigen test and treat if positive.

GABHS is the most common bacterial cause of pharyngitis (5-15% adults, 30-40% pediatric). Gold standard is throat culture. Antibiotic treatment reduces severity, transmission (after 24h), and rheumatic fever risk.

**Q:** What is the recommended treatment for Group A beta-hemolytic Streptococcal pharyngitis in adults?

**A:** Penicillin V 300 mg PO TID or 600 mg BID x 10 days. Mild penicillin allergy: cefuroxime 500 mg BID x 10 days. Severe allergy: azithromycin.

Ten days of treatment is required. Antibiotic treatment does not decrease glomerulonephritis incidence. A 48-hour delay in antibiotics does not increase rheumatic fever risk, so culture confirmation is preferred when possible.

**Q:** What precaution must be observed in infectious mononucleosis (EBV)?

**A:** Avoid heavy physical activity and contact sports for at least 1 month or until splenomegaly resolves, due to the risk of splenic rupture.

EBV presents with pharyngitis, tonsillar exudate, fever, lymphadenopathy, and fatigue. Diagnosis uses peripheral blood smear and monospot test. Treatment is supportive; antibiotics are not indicated.

**Q:** What are the mandatory reportable STIs in Canada?

**A:** Chlamydia, gonorrhea, hepatitis B, HIV, syphilis, and chancroid.

Chlamydia is the most common reportable STI in Canada. When urethritis/cervicitis is suspected, always treat for both gonococcal and non-gonococcal types (ceftriaxone 250 mg IM + azithromycin 1g PO).

**Q:** What is the basic structure distinguishing Gram-positive from Gram-negative bacteria, and why does this matter clinically?

**A:** Gram-positive (GP): thick, rigid peptidoglycan cell wall that retains crystal violet stain (purple). Gram-negative (GN): thin peptidoglycan layer + outer membrane of lipoproteins and lipopolysaccharides (LPS), stains pink. Clinical significance: Gram stain results guide empiric antibiotic selection before culture results are available.

LPS (endotoxin) in GN bacteria triggers immune responses causing sepsis. GP bacteria can also release exotoxins. This structural difference also determines which antibiotics are effective — beta-lactams must penetrate the outer GN membrane.

**Q:** What are acid-fast bacilli (AFB), and which organisms are AFB-positive vs. modified AFB-positive?

**A:** AFB: bacteria with high mycolic acid content in their cell wall that resist decolourization with acid-alcohol (e.g., Mycobacterium species — positive on both AFB and modified AFB stain). Partially AFB (modified AFB positive only): moderate mycolic acid content, decolourize with acid-alcohol but resist weaker acid (e.g., Nocardia — negative on standard AFB, positive on modified AFB).

This distinction is clinically important: Mycobacterium tuberculosis and M. avium are AFB-positive. Nocardia causes pulmonary, CNS, and disseminated infections, particularly in immunocompromised hosts, and requires modified staining for identification.

**Q:** What are the 6 major mechanisms of bacterial virulence/pathogenesis?

**A:** 1. Adherence to skin/mucous membranes (fimbriae/pili attach to epithelial cells, e.g., E. coli UTI). 2. Invasion/crossing epithelial barriers. 3. Evasion of host defenses (capsule inhibits phagocytosis — S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis, H. influenzae). 4. Toxin production (exotoxins secreted by living bacteria; endotoxins = GN cell wall LPS released on lysis). 5. Intracellular growth (obligate: Rickettsia, Chlamydia; facultative: Salmonella, Listeria, Mycobacteria, Legionella). 6. Biofilm formation (S. epidermidis on prosthetic devices).

Understanding virulence mechanisms helps explain clinical presentations and guides treatment. Encapsulated organisms require opsonization for phagocytosis — asplenic patients are particularly susceptible. Biofilm-forming bacteria (S. epidermidis) are major causes of prosthetic device infections.

**Q:** Which encapsulated bacteria inhibit phagocytic uptake via their polysaccharide capsule, and why are asplenic patients particularly vulnerable?

**A:** Key encapsulated bacteria inhibiting phagocytosis: Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus influenzae. The spleen filters and opsonizes encapsulated organisms. Asplenic patients cannot effectively clear these bacteria, putting them at risk for overwhelming post-splenectomy infection (OPSI). Vaccination against these organisms is mandatory post-splenectomy.

The capsule prevents direct phagocytic recognition. Opsonization with IgG or complement (C3b) is required, and the spleen is a major site for this. Asplenic patients must receive pneumococcal, meningococcal, and Hib vaccines.

**Q:** What is the difference between exotoxins and endotoxins? Give clinical examples of each.

**A:** Exotoxins: proteins secreted by living pathogenic bacteria; cause disease even without bacterial presence. Examples: Clostridium tetani (tetanospasmin causing tetanus), Clostridium botulinum (botulinum toxin). Endotoxins: structural LPS components of Gram-negative bacterial cell walls; released during cell lysis. Trigger inflammatory cascade causing sepsis.

Exotoxins are heat-labile proteins that can often be neutralized by antitoxins. Endotoxin (LPS) activates toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4) on macrophages, triggering TNF, IL-1, and IL-6 release — the pathogenesis of Gram-negative sepsis and septic shock.

**Q:** What categories of 'atypical bacteria' are important to recognize, and how do they differ from typical bacteria?

**A:** Atypical bacteria are not seen on Gram stain and are difficult to culture. Categories: obligate intracellular bacteria (Chlamydia — cannot live outside host cell), bacteria lacking cell wall (Mycoplasma — intrinsically resistant to beta-lactam antibiotics), and spirochetes (Treponema pallidum — syphilis). Require special diagnostic methods and specific antibiotics (e.g., macrolides, doxycycline).

Mycoplasma pneumoniae is the classic atypical pneumonia pathogen, which does not respond to beta-lactams because it lacks a cell wall. Spirochetes are visualized by darkfield microscopy or silver staining. Chlamydia is diagnosed by PCR or cell culture.

## Microbiology

**Q:** What bacteria cause most UTIs? Use the mnemonic KEEPS.

**A:** Klebsiella, E. coli (most common), Enterobacter spp., Proteus mirabilis, Pseudomonas spp., S. saprophyticus.

E. coli is responsible for approximately 80% of uncomplicated UTIs. S. saprophyticus is the second most common cause in young sexually active women.

## Infectious Diseases

**Q:** Which bacteria are Gram-positive cocci (aerobes) in the common bacteria classification?

**A:** Staphylococcus (S. aureus, S. saprophyticus, S. epidermidis, S. lugdunensis) and Streptococcus (S. pneumoniae [diplococci], S. pyogenes [GAS], S. agalactiae [GBS], S. anginosus group) and Enterococcus (E. faecalis)

Gram-positive cocci aerobes are clinically important pathogens causing a wide range of infections from skin/soft tissue to endocarditis. Distinguishing staphylococci (catalase-positive) from streptococci (catalase-negative) is a key lab step.

**Q:** Which Gram-positive bacilli are NOT spore-forming?

**A:** Listeria monocytogenes, Corynebacterium diphtheriae, Actinomyces, Cutibacterium (formerly Propionibacterium) acnes, and Lactobacillus

Non-spore-forming GP bacilli are distinguished from spore-formers like Bacillus and Clostridium. Listeria is important in food-borne illness, particularly in immunocompromised and pregnant patients.

**Q:** Which bacteria are NOT seen on Gram stain and why?

**A:** Acid-fast bacteria (Mycobacterium tuberculosis, M. leprae, M. avium complex), obligate intracellular bacteria (Rickettsia, Chlamydia), bacteria with no cell wall (Mycoplasma), and spirochetes (Treponema pallidum) are not visible on standard Gram stain.

These organisms require special staining techniques: acid-fast stain (Ziehl-Neelsen) for mycobacteria, dark-field microscopy or silver stain for spirochetes, and cell culture or PCR for obligate intracellular organisms. Mycoplasma lacks a cell wall entirely.

**Q:** What is the normal commensal flora of the vagina?

**A:** Lactobacillus acidophilus, viridans group streptococci, coagulase-negative staphylococci, and facultative anaerobes

Lactobacillus is the dominant organism and maintains vaginal pH via lactic acid production, protecting against pathogens. Disruption of this flora (e.g., antibiotics) can predispose to bacterial vaginosis or candidiasis.

**Q:** What is the key laboratory difference between Staphylococcus aureus and coagulase-negative staphylococci?

**A:** S. aureus is coagulase-positive; coagulase-negative staphylococci (e.g., S. epidermidis, S. saprophyticus, S. lugdunensis) are coagulase-negative.

The coagulase test is essential for identifying S. aureus, the more virulent pathogen associated with abscesses, bacteremia, endocarditis, and toxic shock syndrome. Coagulase-negative species are less virulent but important in prosthetic device infections.

**Q:** How are Streptococci classified in the laboratory?

**A:** By hemolysis pattern on sheep blood agar (alpha, beta, or gamma hemolysis) and by Lancefield grouping (A, B, C, D, G). S. pneumoniae is alpha-hemolytic, S. pyogenes (GAS) is Group A beta-hemolytic, S. agalactiae (GBS) is Group B beta-hemolytic.

Beta-hemolytic streptococci lyse red blood cells completely, alpha-hemolytic cause partial lysis (greenish discoloration), and gamma-hemolytic cause no lysis. This classification guides empiric antibiotic therapy.

**Q:** How are Gram-negative bacilli (Enterobacterales) further classified in the laboratory?

**A:** By oxidase test (negative for Enterobacterales), then glucose fermentation, and then lactose fermentation. Lactose fermenters: E. coli, Klebsiella. Non-lactose fermenters: Salmonella typhi, Shigella, Proteus. Oxidase-positive GNB: Pseudomonas, Vibrio, Campylobacter.

These biochemical tests guide identification and narrow antibiotic selection. For example, non-lactose fermenting colonies on MacConkey agar require further characterization to differentiate enteric pathogens.

**Q:** What are the three patterns of viral disease?

**A:** 1. Acute infections: host cells lysed during virion release (e.g., adenovirus); some may have late sequelae (e.g., measles causing subacute sclerosing panencephalitis). 2. Chronic infections (>6 months): host cell machinery chronically releases virions (e.g., HBV, HIV). 3. Latent infections: viral genome remains latent and can reactivate (e.g., HSV, VZV).

Understanding viral disease patterns informs prognosis and treatment. Latent viruses (herpesviruses) can reactivate during immunosuppression; chronic infections (HBV, HCV) lead to long-term complications like cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma.

**Q:** Which human herpesviruses (HHV) exist, and what disease does each cause?

**A:** HHV1 (HSV-1): oral/ocular/genital herpes, encephalitis. HHV2 (HSV-2): genital/oral/ocular herpes, encephalitis. HHV3 (VZV): chickenpox, shingles. HHV4 (EBV): mononucleosis, viral hepatitis. HHV5 (CMV): retinitis, pneumonitis, hepatitis, encephalitis. HHV6: roseola. HHV7: roseola. HHV8 (KSHV): Kaposi's sarcoma, multicentric Castleman's disease, body cavity lymphoma.

All herpesviruses are enveloped dsDNA viruses (Herpesviridae family) and establish lifelong latency. Reactivation is especially clinically significant in immunocompromised patients (HIV, transplant).

**Q:** Which viruses are positive-sense single-stranded RNA with NO envelope (non-enveloped)?

**A:** Picornaviridae (poliovirus, echovirus, rhinovirus, coxsackievirus, hepatitis A), Caliciviridae (noroviruses, hepatitis E), Reoviridae (rotavirus — dsRNA).

Non-enveloped viruses are more resistant to desiccation, detergents, and harsh environments, making them effective in fecal-oral transmission. Alcohol-based hand sanitizers are less effective; soap and water are preferred for norovirus.

**Q:** What is the key difference between Influenza A and Influenza B viruses?

**A:** Influenza A infects humans, birds, and mammals and can undergo both antigenic drift AND shift, leading to epidemics and pandemics. Influenza B infects humans only, undergoes antigenic drift but NOT shift, and causes epidemics but NOT pandemics.

Antigenic shift (abrupt, major changes via reassortment) occurs only in Influenza A because it infects multiple species, allowing mixing of viral segments. This is why pandemic influenza is always Influenza A (e.g., H1N1 2009).

**Q:** What is the mechanism of fungal cell membrane and cell wall that differs from human cells and bacteria?

**A:** Fungi use ergosterol in their cell membrane (humans use cholesterol; bacteria have no sterol) and chitin (complex glycopolysaccharide) in their cell wall (bacteria use peptidoglycan; humans have no cell wall).

These differences are the basis of antifungal drug targets: azoles and polyenes target ergosterol (analogous to statins targeting cholesterol synthesis in humans). Echinocandins target glucan synthesis in the fungal cell wall.

**Q:** What are the 3 morphologies of fungi?

**A:** 1. Yeast (unicellular). 2. Moulds/filamentous fungi (multicellular with hyphae — septate or non-septate). 3. Dimorphic fungi (mould at room temperature, yeast at body temperature — e.g., Histoplasma, Blastomyces, Coccidioides).

The mnemonic 'mould in the cold, yeast in the heat' applies to dimorphic fungi. Aspergillus (septate hyphae) and Mucor/Rhizopus (non-septate hyphae) are important pathogenic moulds.

**Q:** What are the key differences between protozoa and helminths?

**A:** Protozoa: unicellular, motile trophozoite/inactive cyst stage, multiply in human host, eosinophilia unusual, indefinite lifespan. Helminths: multicellular, adult-egg-larva life cycle, no multiplication in human host, eosinophilia proportional to tissue invasion, definite lifespan.

Peripheral eosinophilia is a key diagnostic clue pointing toward helminthic (parasitic worm) infection. Note: adult Ascaris does NOT cause eosinophilia, but its migratory larval phase causes high-grade eosinophilia.

**Q:** What are the 5 mechanisms of parasitic disease?

**A:** 1. Mechanical obstruction (e.g., ascariasis, clonorchiasis). 2. Competition with host for resources (e.g., anemia in hookworm, vitamin B12 deficiency in diphyllobothriasis). 3. Cytotoxicity causing abscesses/ulcers (e.g., amoebiasis, leishmaniasis). 4. Inflammatory response (acute hypersensitivity in Loeffler's, egg granulomas in schistosomiasis). 5. Immune-mediated injury (autoimmune myocarditis in Chagas, immune complex nephritis in malaria).

Understanding the mechanism helps explain clinical presentations: vitamin B12 deficiency from fish tapeworm (Diphyllobothrium latum) consuming B12; Loeffler's syndrome (transient pulmonary eosinophilia) from larval migration.

**Q:** What are the infection control precautions for airborne vs. droplet vs. contact transmission?

**A:** Airborne (<5 μm droplet nuclei, remain infectious over time and distance): N95 mask, negative pressure room — examples: M. tuberculosis, disseminated VZV, measles. Droplet (>5 μm, <2 m range): surgical mask, private room — examples: influenza, mumps, N. meningitidis, B. pertussis. Contact: gloves, gown — examples: MRSA, skin-to-skin infections.

COVID-19 experience showed a continuum between droplet and airborne transmission. Hand hygiene remains the most essential precaution across all modes of transmission.

**Q:** What is the definition of a nosocomial (healthcare-associated) infection?

**A:** An infection acquired >48 hours after admission to a healthcare facility OR within 30 days from discharge.

The 48-hour threshold distinguishes hospital-acquired from community-acquired infection. Risk factors include prolonged hospital stay, antibiotic use, surgery, ICU care, hemodialysis, and immunodeficiency.

**Q:** What are the treatment and decolonization strategies for MRSA?

**A:** Treatment of MRSA infection: vancomycin, daptomycin, or linezolid. Decolonization: chlorhexidine 2% wash once daily + rifampin + (doxycycline or TMP/SMX) + mupirocin cream BID to nares × 7 days. Infection control: contact precautions.

MRSA screening from nares and peri-anal region identifies colonization. Decolonization is important in preventing transmission and recurrent infection in carriers. Vancomycin is the first-line IV agent for serious MRSA infection.

**Q:** What is the treatment algorithm for Clostridioides difficile infection (CDI)?

**A:** 1. Stop culprit antibiotic (especially fluoroquinolones and clindamycin). 2. Supportive therapy (IV fluids). 3. Empiric treatment: vancomycin PO or fidaxomicin PO. If access limited, metronidazole may be used. 4. Fulminant CDI: oral vancomycin + IV metronidazole (if ileus). Diagnosis: stool PCR for toxin A and B genes (most sensitive); stool immunoassay less sensitive. Infection control: contact precautions.

The hypervirulent NAP1/B1/027 strain produces more toxin. Metronidazole is now second-line as fidaxomicin and vancomycin are preferred for efficacy. Fecal microbiota transplantation is used for recurrent CDI.

**Q:** What is the treatment for ESBL-producing Enterobacterales infections?

**A:** Carbapenems (e.g., meropenem, ertapenem) or non-β-lactam antibiotics. ESBL producers are resistant to most β-lactam antibiotics except carbapenems (e.g., resistant to penicillins, cephalosporins, aztreonam). Contact precautions.

ESBLs hydrolyze extended-spectrum cephalosporins and penicillins. Common ESBL producers include E. coli and Klebsiella pneumoniae. Carbapenems are the treatment of choice; inappropriate use promotes carbapenem resistance.

**Q:** What treatment options exist for Carbapenemase-Producing Enterobacterales (CPE)?

**A:** Colistin, tigecycline (based on susceptibility); newer options available through Canada's Special Access Program: cefiderocol, ceftazidime-avibactam, meropenem-vaborbactam, imipenem-relebactam, plazomicin. Contact precautions required.

CPE represents the most resistant gram-negative organisms. These bacteria are resistant to virtually all β-lactam antibiotics including carbapenems. Treatment options are severely limited and require specialist input.

**Q:** What are the common organisms causing community-acquired pneumonia (CAP)?

**A:** Typical bacteria: S. pneumoniae (most common), Moraxella catarrhalis, Haemophilus influenzae, S. aureus/GAS. Atypical bacteria: Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydia pneumoniae, Legionella pneumophila. Viral: Influenza, Adenovirus, SARS-CoV-2.

S. pneumoniae remains the most common bacterial cause of CAP. Atypical pathogens do not respond to β-lactams and require macrolides, doxycycline, or fluoroquinolones. Legionella is associated with contaminated water systems and presents with GI symptoms.

**Q:** What organisms cause pneumonia in immunocompromised patients?

**A:** Pneumocystis jirovecii (PJP), fungi (e.g., Cryptococcus), Nocardia, enteric GNB, S. aureus, oral anaerobes (aspiration), HSV.

PJP is the classic AIDS-defining pneumonia (CD4 <200). Cryptococcus is also common in HIV. Nocardia occurs in T-cell immunodeficiency. Recognizing the immunocompromised state broadens the differential significantly.

**Q:** What is the CURB-65 score and how is it interpreted?

**A:** CURB-65 predicts mortality in community-acquired pneumonia. 1 point each for: Confusion (altered mental status), Urea >7 mmol/L or BUN >20 mg/dL, Respiratory Rate >30 breaths/min, Blood Pressure <90 mmHg systolic or <60 mmHg diastolic, Age ≥65. Score 0-1: <5% mortality, consider outpatient. Score 2-3: ~15% mortality, consider hospitalization. Score 4-5: 15-30% mortality, consider ICU.

CURB-65 is a validated clinical prediction tool for CAP. It guides disposition decisions. The PSI (Pneumonia Severity Index) is also used and is preferred by some guidelines but is more complex to calculate.

**Q:** What are the components of the Pneumonia Severity Index (PSI) and its risk classes?

**A:** PSI uses demographics (age, sex, nursing home residence), comorbidities (neoplasm +30, liver disease +20, CHF +10, CVD +10, renal disease +10), exam findings (AMS +20, RR ≥30 +20, sBP <90 +20, T<35 or ≥40 +15, HR >125 +10), and labs/imaging (pH <7.35 +30, BUN >30 +20, Na <130 +20, glucose ≥250 +10, Hct <30 +10, PaO2 <60 +10, effusion +10). Class I (<51 pts): 0.7%; II (51-70): 0.6%; III (71-90): 0.9-2.8%; IV (91-130): 8.2-9.3%; V (>130): 21-29.2%. Classes I-III: consider outpatient; IV-V: hospitalize.

PSI is more validated and precise than CURB-65 but more complex. The PSI is preferred over CURB-65 in recent IDSA/ATS guidelines for guiding hospitalization decisions in CAP.

**Q:** What is the outpatient treatment for CAP with no comorbidities per IDSA/ATS 2019 guidelines?

**A:** Amoxicillin OR Doxycycline OR Macrolide (azithromycin or clarithromycin — if local pneumococcal resistance <25%). For patients with comorbidities: amoxicillin-clavulanate or cephalosporin (cefpodoxime or cefuroxime) PLUS macrolide or doxycycline; OR respiratory fluoroquinolone (moxifloxacin or levofloxacin).

Monotherapy with amoxicillin covers S. pneumoniae. Dual therapy with a β-lactam plus macrolide or doxycycline adds atypical coverage. Respiratory fluoroquinolones cover both typical and atypical pathogens in a single agent.

**Q:** What defines severe CAP requiring ICU admission per IDSA/ATS criteria?

**A:** One MAJOR criterion: septic shock requiring vasopressors, or respiratory failure requiring mechanical ventilation. OR Three or more MINOR criteria: RR ≥30, PaO2/FiO2 <250, multilobar infiltrates, confusion/disorientation, BUN ≥20 mg/dL, WBC <4000/μL (from infection), platelets <100,000/μL, temperature <36°C, hypotension requiring aggressive fluid resuscitation.

Severity criteria direct triage to ICU and guide empiric antibiotic breadth. Severe CAP requires broader coverage including consideration for Legionella and more coverage of S. pneumoniae.

**Q:** What antibiotics are recommended for hospital-acquired pneumonia (HAP) without risk factors for MRSA or high mortality risk?

**A:** Monotherapy with ONE of: piperacillin-tazobactam, cefepime, levofloxacin, imipenem, or meropenem (per IDSA/ATS HAP/VAP guidelines 2016).

HAP (non-VAP) without MRSA risk factors can be treated with antipseudomonal monotherapy. Adding MRSA coverage (vancomycin or linezolid) is indicated when there is increasing likelihood of MRSA or prior IV antibiotic use within 90 days.

**Q:** What vaccines does the Public Health Agency of Canada recommend for pneumonia/influenza prevention?

**A:** 1. Influenza vaccine annually for all ages ≥6 months. 2. mRNA COVID-19 vaccine (Moderna Spikevax or Pfizer-BioNTech Comirnaty) for all ages ≥5 years, with boosters per public health guidance; and for ages ≥6 months to 5 years at high risk. 3. Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine Pneu-C-15 (VAXNEUVANCE) or Pneu-C-20 (PREVNAR 20) for children 2 months to 18 years; Pneu-C-20 for immunocompromised and all adults ≥65 years.

Canadian pneumococcal vaccine guidelines were updated May 2024 (NACI). Pneu-C-20 covers all serotypes in PNEUMOVAX 23 plus 7 additional. However, Pneu-C-20 and Pneu-C-15 are not yet covered by all provincial/territorial programs.

**Q:** What are the subtypes of influenza A and how are they classified?

**A:** Influenza A is classified by two envelope glycoproteins: hemagglutinin (H, 18 types) and neuraminidase (N, 11 types). Currently circulating seasonal strains: influenza A (H1N1) and influenza A (H3N2). Antigenic drift creates new strains causing seasonal epidemics; antigenic shift creates entirely new subtypes causing pandemics (influenza A only).

H1N1 caused the 1918 Spanish flu pandemic and the 2009 swine flu pandemic. H5N1 is a highly pathogenic avian influenza strain of pandemic concern. Antigenic shift requires reassortment between two different influenza A strains from different animal hosts.

**Q:** What is the treatment for influenza and who is at high risk for complications?

**A:** Treatment: neuraminidase inhibitors — oseltamivir (Tamiflu) or zanamivir (Relenza) or peramivir (Rapivab) for types A and B. Started within 48 hours; decreases duration by ~1 day. High-risk groups: hospitalized patients, elderly ≥65, children <2 years, pregnant women/≤2 weeks postpartum, immunocompromised, and those with severe/chronic medical conditions (asthma, COPD, CVD, DM, obesity).

Treatment beyond 48 hours may be warranted in immunosuppressed and critically ill patients. Influenza complications include viral pneumonia, secondary bacterial pneumonia, rhabdomyolysis, encephalitis, and Guillain-Barré syndrome. Research also shows influenza increases acute MI risk within 7 days.

**Q:** What are the key features of SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) pathophysiology and clinical presentation?

**A:** SARS-CoV-2 is an enveloped positive-sense ssRNA virus. Entry via spike protein binding to ACE2 receptors on lung surface epithelium. Causes cytotoxic damage to alveolar epithelium; dysregulated immune response can lead to cytokine storm and organ failure. Most common symptoms: fever, fatigue, dry cough. Common: dyspnea, anosmia/ageusia, anorexia, myalgia. 1 in 3 infected are asymptomatic.

ACE2 is highly expressed in lung, GI tract, heart, kidney, and blood vessels, explaining multisystem involvement. Post-COVID condition (long COVID) causes persistent symptoms across organ systems, often exacerbated by mental and physical overexertion.

**Q:** What is the treatment for critically ill hospitalized COVID-19 adults requiring ventilatory or circulatory support?

**A:** 1. Dexamethasone PO or IV (for duration of hospitalization until discharge). 2. Tocilizumab (if within 14 days of new COVID-19 diagnosis). 3. Prophylactic low molecular-weight or unfractionated heparin (VTE prophylaxis).

Dexamethasone reduces mortality in patients requiring oxygen (RECOVERY trial). Tocilizumab (IL-6 receptor antagonist) reduces inflammation and improves survival in severe disease. Anticoagulation prevents COVID-associated coagulopathy and thromboembolic events.

**Q:** What is the treatment for mild-to-moderate COVID-19 in high-risk patients who do not require supplemental oxygen?

**A:** Nirmatrelvir/ritonavir (Paxlovid) if presenting within 5 days of symptom onset, OR remdesivir if presenting within 7 days of symptom onset. Low-risk patients receive supportive care only.

Paxlovid (nirmatrelvir/ritonavir) is an oral protease inhibitor combination with significant drug-drug interactions due to ritonavir's CYP3A4 inhibition. Remdesivir is an RNA polymerase inhibitor given IV. Stratifying by hospitalization risk guides antiviral therapy decisions.

**Q:** Which groups are RECOMMENDED for COVID-19 antiviral therapy regardless of vaccination status?

**A:** 1. Age ≥65 with no other risk factors. 2. Immunocompromised patients (any age): advanced untreated HIV or HIV with CD4 ≤200/mm3, bone marrow/stem cell transplant, solid organ transplant, active hematological malignancy or recent anti-CD20 agents/B-cell depleting agents in last 2 years, CAR T-cell therapy in last 6 months, systemic cancer therapy in last 6 months or radiation in last 3 months, prednisone ≥20 mg/day for ≥14 days or other severely immunosuppressive therapies, primary immunodeficiencies.

Antiviral therapy for COVID-19 is most beneficial in immunocompromised patients who may not mount adequate immune responses to infection. Early treatment (within 5-7 days of symptom onset) is essential for efficacy.

**Q:** What is cellulitis and what are its most common causative organisms?

**A:** Cellulitis is an acute infection of the skin involving principally the dermis and subcutaneous tissue. Most common causative agents: β-hemolytic streptococci (most common in non-purulent cellulitis), S. aureus, and occasionally S. lugdunensis. Immunocompromised or water-exposed patients: also GN rods and fungi. Bite wounds: skin flora of 'bitee' and mouth flora of 'biter'.

Non-purulent cellulitis is predominantly streptococcal; purulent cellulitis (with abscess or drainage) is more likely S. aureus/MRSA. Risk factors include trauma, PVD, lymphedema, DM, and tinea pedis (portal of entry).

**Q:** What are the clinical features, investigations, and treatment of cellulitis?

**A:** Clinical: pain, edema, erythema with indistinct borders ± regional lymphadenopathy, systemic symptoms (fever, chills, malaise); can lead to ascending lymphangitis (red streaking along lymphatics). Investigations: CBC and blood C&S (if malignancy, severe systemic features, unusual predisposing factors); skin swab ONLY if open wound with pus. Treatment: cephalexin PO (broader coverage if GN rod risk); cefazolin IV if extensive erythema or systemic symptoms; empiric MRSA coverage if MRSA suspected.

The indistinct border of cellulitis helps distinguish it from erysipelas (which has a distinct raised border). Elevating the affected limb reduces edema and speeds recovery. Blood cultures are low yield in uncomplicated cellulitis.

**Q:** What is necrotizing fasciitis and what are its two types?

**A:** Necrotizing fasciitis is a life- and limb-threatening infection of the deep fascia characterized by rapid spread. Type I: polymicrobial (aerobes + anaerobes: e.g., S. aureus, Bacteroides, Enterobacterales). Type II: monomicrobial with GAS (Group A Streptococcus), less commonly S. aureus.

Type I is more common in diabetics and post-surgical patients. Type II (GAS) is classic 'flesh-eating disease' with streptococcal toxic shock syndrome. Both require aggressive surgical debridement — delay is fatal.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of necrotizing fasciitis that distinguish it from cellulitis?

**A:** 1. Pain out of proportion to clinical findings and beyond border of erythema. 2. Edema ± crepitus (subcutaneous gas from anaerobes). 3. Rapid spread. 4. Rapid systemic toxicity (tachycardia, hypotension, disorientation, fever). 5. Late findings: skin turns dusky blue/black (thrombosis and necrosis), induration, hemorrhagic bullae, loss of sensation (paresthesias).

The hallmark is severe pain disproportionate to the visible skin findings. Crepitus indicates gas-producing anaerobes (Type I). Skin necrosis and bullae are late, ominous signs. Elevated serum CK indicates myonecrosis.

**Q:** What is the treatment of necrotizing fasciitis?

**A:** 1. IV fluid resuscitation. 2. Emergency surgical debridement (may require amputation) — do NOT wait for investigation results. 3. IV antibiotics: unknown organism: meropenem or pip-tazo + clindamycin IV ± vancomycin. Type I (polymicrobial): piperacillin-tazobactam + clindamycin IV. Type II with GAS: penicillin G + clindamycin IV. Type II with S. aureus: cefazolin (or cloxacillin) + clindamycin IV. 4. Evaluate for streptococcal toxic shock syndrome and consider IVIG (Type I).

Clindamycin is added in all regimens to suppress toxin production (blocks ribosome protein synthesis). IVIG may neutralize streptococcal toxins in streptococcal toxic shock syndrome. Surgical debridement is the definitive treatment — antibiotics alone are insufficient.

**Q:** What investigations are used to diagnose necrotizing fasciitis?

**A:** Clinical/surgical diagnosis — do NOT wait for investigations before treatment. Blood and tissue C&S. Serum creatine kinase (CK) — elevated CK indicates myonecrosis (late sign). Plain film x-ray or CT (preferred) — to visualize soft tissue gas. Surgical exploration for definitive diagnosis and debridement.

CT is the best imaging for detecting soft tissue gas and fascial involvement. However, imaging should never delay surgical intervention when clinical suspicion is high. The LRINEC (Laboratory Risk Indicator for Necrotizing Fasciitis) score can aid diagnosis but is not definitive.

**Q:** A 58-year-old diabetic man presents with severe leg pain far exceeding what is visible on examination, with a small area of erythema. His leg feels tense. What is the most likely diagnosis and what should be done immediately?

**A:** Most likely diagnosis: necrotizing fasciitis. Immediate action: emergency surgical consultation for debridement; do NOT wait for investigations to confirm before proceeding to surgery. Begin IV fluids and broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (meropenem or pip-tazo + clindamycin IV ± vancomycin).

Pain out of proportion to visible findings in a diabetic patient is the classic presentation of necrotizing fasciitis. Diabetes is a major risk factor for Type I (polymicrobial) necrotizing fasciitis. Delay in surgical debridement dramatically increases mortality.

**Q:** A 72-year-old woman is admitted with cough, fever, and a right lower lobe infiltrate. Her CURB-65 score is 3. What does this indicate and what treatment setting is recommended?

**A:** CURB-65 score of 3 indicates approximately 15% mortality and warrants hospitalization. Inpatient treatment with a β-lactam (e.g., ampicillin-sulbactam, cefotaxime, or ceftriaxone) PLUS a macrolide (azithromycin or clarithromycin), OR a respiratory fluoroquinolone (moxifloxacin or levofloxacin) for non-severe CAP without MRSA/Pseudomonas risk factors.

Each CURB-65 component (Confusion, Urea >7 mmol/L, RR >30, BP <90/60, Age ≥65) contributes one point. Hospitalization is generally recommended for scores ≥2-3. The treatment regimen should cover typical and atypical organisms.

**Q:** What is the importance of the 3 As of Klebsiella pneumonia?

**A:** Klebsiella pneumonia is associated with: Aspiration pneumonia (in alcohol use disorder and diabetic patients), Alcohol use disorder and diabetes as risk factors, and Abscess in lungs. Characteristic sputum: red currant jelly sputum (bloody mucoid).

Klebsiella causes a particularly severe pneumonia often with abscess formation and 'red currant jelly' sputum due to necrosis. It preferentially affects the right upper lobe in alcohol use disorder. Enteric GN rods are more common nosocomial and alcohol use disorder-associated pneumonia pathogens.

**Q:** What is the diagnosis and gold standard test for influenza?

**A:** Diagnosis is primarily clinical based on symptoms during influenza season. Gold standard: nasopharyngeal swabs for RT-PCR. Rapid antigen detection (DFA, direct fluorescent antibody) has lower sensitivity. Lower respiratory specimens for RT-PCR can also be used. Serology is rarely used for clinical management.

RT-PCR distinguishes influenza A from B and can identify subtypes. Rapid antigen tests are faster but miss more cases, particularly in immunocompromised patients with lower viral loads. Clinical diagnosis is reasonable during established outbreaks.

**Q:** What are the etiology categories for acquired oral lesions?

**A:** 1. Infection (candidiasis, gonococcal infection, HSV). 2. Malignancy (adenocarcinoma, leukoplakia). 3. Poor oral hygiene (caries, periodontal disease). 4. Trauma (e.g., abuse). 5. Toxic ingestion. 6. Xerostomia (dry mouth — age, medications). 7. Systemic diseases (lichen planus, Behçet disease).

Oral candidiasis (thrush) is common in immunocompromised patients, those on inhaled corticosteroids, and antibiotic users. Leukoplakia is a white patch that cannot be scraped off and has malignant potential. Behçet disease causes recurrent oral and genital ulcers.

**Q:** What is the commensal flora of the colon?

**A:** E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Enterococcus, and anaerobes (Bacteroides, Peptostreptococcus, Clostridium).

The colon contains the highest bacterial density of any body site (~10^11/mL). Anaerobes outnumber aerobes 100:1 in the colon. Disruption of this flora by antibiotics predisposes to C. difficile infection. Bacteroides fragilis is the most common anaerobic pathogen from colonic perforation.

**Q:** How does vertical transmission of infectious diseases occur and what are examples?

**A:** Vertical transmission: spread of disease from parent to offspring. Examples: congenital syndromes (TORCH infections — Toxoplasma, Other [syphilis, varicella, parvovirus B19], Rubella, CMV, Herpes simplex), and perinatal transmission (HIV, HBV, GBS). Prevention: prenatal screening and prophylactic treatment.

TORCH infections can cause serious fetal harm. CMV is the most common congenital infection. GBS (Group B Streptococcus) is screened at 35-37 weeks gestation; intrapartum prophylaxis with penicillin reduces neonatal GBS disease. HIV transmission can be reduced to <1% with antiretroviral therapy.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of neuraminidase inhibitors for influenza treatment?

**A:** Neuraminidase inhibitors (oseltamivir/Tamiflu, zanamivir/Relenza, peramivir/Rapivab) inhibit viral neuraminidase, which normally cleaves sialic acid from cell surfaces to allow newly formed virions to be released from infected cells. Inhibition traps virions on the cell surface, preventing spread to new cells.

Neuraminidase (N) is one of the two surface glycoproteins of influenza A (H and N). Oseltamivir is oral; zanamivir is inhaled (avoid in COPD/asthma due to bronchospasm risk); peramivir is IV. All are active against influenza A and B.

**Q:** What risk factors associated with moderate-to-high risk of severe COVID-19 outcomes may prompt consideration of antiviral therapy (per Ontario Health guidelines)?

**A:** Risk factors: never vaccinated, active TB, cerebrovascular disease, CKD (especially stage 4-5 or dialysis), chronic lung diseases (asthma, COPD, bronchiectasis, ILD, pulmonary HTN, PE), chronic liver diseases (cirrhosis, NAFLD, alcoholic liver disease, autoimmune hepatitis), cystic fibrosis, DM (type 1 or 2), Down syndrome, heart conditions (HF, CAD, cardiomyopathy), mental health conditions (mood disorders, schizophrenia), neurologic conditions affecting respiratory secretions (e.g., dementia), obesity (BMI >30), pregnancy or recent pregnancy (≤42 days postpartum).

Therapy is RECOMMENDED for ≥3 risk factors (higher risk), MAY BE CONSIDERED for 2 (moderate risk), and generally not indicated for 0-1 (lower risk). These guidelines from Ontario Health reflect Canadian-specific recommendations for antiviral allocation.

**Q:** What are the key distinguishing features between oral candidiasis and oral squamous cell carcinoma?

**A:** Oral candidiasis: white patches that can be wiped off (pseudomembranous) or red patches (atrophic); risk factors include antibiotics, immunocompromise, inhaled corticosteroids, infants/elderly with dentures. Oral SCC: red, red-and-white, or ulcerated lesion present ≥3 weeks, indurated on palpation; risk factors include tobacco, betel, alcohol, HPV-16.

The ability to wipe off white patches is a key bedside test: pseudomembranous candidiasis plaques wipe off revealing an erythematous base, while SCC lesions are fixed and indurated. Any oral lesion lasting ≥3 weeks warrants biopsy and ENT referral per Canadian guidelines to exclude malignancy.

**Q:** What is the most common route of infection in septic arthritis, and why is it a medical emergency?

**A:** Hematogenous spread is the most common route. Septic arthritis is a medical emergency because untreated infection causes rapid, irreversible joint destruction.

Bacteria seed the synovial space most often from distant bacteremia. The synovial membrane lacks a limiting basement membrane, allowing rapid bacterial proliferation and enzyme-mediated cartilage destruction. Prompt diagnosis via arthrocentesis and empiric antibiotics are critical to preserve joint function.

**Q:** What are the most common organisms causing septic arthritis in different patient populations?

**A:** N. gonorrhoeae: young sexually active adults (previously 75%). S. aureus: most non-gonococcal cases in adults (especially RA patients), rapidly destructive. Streptococcus spp. (Group A/B). GN organisms: neonates, elderly, IDU, immunocompromised. S. pneumoniae and Kingella kingae: children <4 yr. Salmonella spp.: sickle cell disease. Coagulase-negative Staphylococcus: prosthetic joints.

Organism selection guides empiric therapy. S. aureus is the dominant non-gonococcal pathogen and is rapidly destructive; MRSA must be considered. Kingella kingae is increasingly recognized in children <4 years. In sickle cell patients, Salmonella causes osteomyelitis and septic arthritis due to functional asplenia.

**Q:** What is the Disseminated Gonococcal Infection (DGI) triad?

**A:** 1. Migratory arthralgias, 2. Tenosynovitis, 3. Pustular skin lesions.

DGI from N. gonorrhoeae can manifest in two overlapping forms: a bacteremic form (triad of migratory arthralgias, tenosynovitis, pustular skin lesions) and a septic arthritis form with local joint findings. DGI is the most common form of disseminated gonococcal disease in young, sexually active adults and is treated with ceftriaxone plus azithromycin for concurrent C. trachomatis.

**Q:** What are the mandatory investigations for suspected septic arthritis, and what synovial fluid findings suggest infection?

**A:** Arthrocentesis is mandatory. Synovial fluid analysis includes: CBC with differential, Gram stain, culture and sensitivity, crystal assessment. Infectious findings: opaque fluid, WBC >15,000/mm³ (higher WBC increases likelihood), PMNs >90%, culture positive. N. gonorrhoeae grows from synovial fluid in <50% of cases.

Arthrocentesis is both diagnostic and therapeutic. The combination of elevated WBC with PMN predominance and positive culture is confirmatory, but culture negativity does not exclude infection (especially gonococcal). Crystal analysis also helps rule out gout and pseudogout, which can mimic septic arthritis.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic treatment for septic arthritis in adults vs. fully vaccinated children?

**A:** Adults: cefazolin + vancomycin (empiric). Fully vaccinated children: cefazolin or cloxacillin IV (unless MRSA is a consideration). Gonococcal: ceftriaxone + azithromycin for 7 days. Non-gonococcal durations: Streptococcus 2–3 weeks IV then PO; S. aureus minimum 4 weeks IV; GNB 4 weeks (early switch to PO is safe and effective per newer evidence).

Empiric coverage must target the most likely organisms. Vancomycin is included in adults to cover MRSA. In fully vaccinated children, Hib and S. pneumoniae are less likely, so cefazolin/cloxacillin is appropriate. Early switch from IV to oral antibiotics for GNB infections is supported by the OVIVA trial.

**Q:** What are the indications for surgical intervention in septic arthritis?

**A:** 1. Consider surgery in all cases if possible. 2. Persistent positive joint cultures on repeat arthrocentesis. 3. Hip joint involvement (especially pediatric). 4. Prosthetic joint involvement.

The hip is an anatomically confined joint where pus under pressure rapidly compromises blood supply, risking avascular necrosis—surgical drainage is particularly urgent in children. Prosthetic joints generally require hardware removal. Daily joint aspirations until cultures are sterile is an alternative in accessible joints.

**Q:** What are the three key pathophysiologic contributors to diabetic foot infections, and how is infection severity classified?

**A:** Contributors: neuropathy, peripheral vascular disease (PVD), and hyperglycemia — all impair healing and predispose to infection. Severity: Mild = superficial (no bone/joint). Moderate = deep (beneath superficial fascia, bone/joint involvement, or erythema >2 cm). Severe = systemic toxicity (fever, tachypnea, leukocytosis, tachycardia, hypotension).

Neuropathy removes protective sensation enabling unnoticed trauma; PVD impairs perfusion and immune cell delivery; hyperglycemia impairs leukocyte function. The probe-to-bone test (positive if bone palpated) has high specificity for osteomyelitis. MRI is the gold standard for diagnosing osteomyelitis when clinical doubt remains.

**Q:** What organisms cause mild vs. moderate/severe diabetic foot infections, and what are the corresponding antibiotic treatments?

**A:** Mild: Streptococcus spp., S. aureus → cloxacillin or cephalexin. Moderate/severe: polymicrobial (S. aureus, Streptococcus, Enterococcus, GNB + anaerobes including Peptostreptococcus, Bacteroides, Clostridium) → amoxicillin/clavulanate or ceftriaxone.

Mild infections are monomicrobial and can be managed with oral narrow-spectrum agents targeting skin flora. Moderate/severe infections require broad-spectrum coverage including anaerobes due to polymicrobial etiology. Glycemic optimization, pressure offloading, wound care, and consideration of surgical debridement are essential adjuncts.

**Q:** What are the high-risk vs. low/no-risk cardiac conditions for infective endocarditis prophylaxis?

**A:** High-risk (prophylaxis indicated): prosthetic cardiac valve, previous IE, unrepaired/recently repaired congenital heart disease (within 6 mo) or with residual defects, cardiac transplant with valve disease, surgically constructed systemic-to-pulmonary shunts/conduits. Low/no risk (no prophylaxis needed): secundum ASD, surgically repaired VSD/PDA, mitral valve prolapse, ischemic heart disease, previous CABG.

IE prophylaxis is targeted only at highest-risk individuals undergoing specific procedures (dental procedures causing bleeding, respiratory tract incision/biopsy, infected skin/soft tissue procedures). Blanket prophylaxis for all cardiac patients is no longer recommended per Canadian/AHA guidelines to reduce antibiotic overuse.

**Q:** What is the order of valve involvement frequency in infective endocarditis, and which valve is most commonly affected in IVDU?

**A:** Overall: MV >> AV > TV > PV. In IVDU: tricuspid valve is involved in 50% of cases.

The mitral valve is most commonly affected overall due to its high pressure and flow dynamics. In IVDU, the tricuspid valve predominates because injected organisms enter the venous system and directly seed the right heart. Right-sided IE in IVDU typically presents with septic pulmonary emboli rather than systemic emboli.

**Q:** What organisms most commonly cause infective endocarditis on native valves vs. early prosthetic valve (< 2 months) vs. late prosthetic valve (> 2 months)?

**A:** Native valve: Streptococcus (36%), S. aureus (28%), Enterococcus (11%), S. epidermidis, GNB. Early prosthetic (<2 mo): S. aureus (68%), Streptococcus (13%), S. epidermidis (17%), GNB, Candida. Late prosthetic (>2 mo): Streptococcus (20%), S. aureus (20%), S. epidermidis (20%), Enterococcus (13%).

Early prosthetic valve IE is predominantly S. aureus from perioperative inoculation, requiring specific empiric coverage. Late prosthetic valve IE has an etiology similar to native valve IE (Streptococcus, S. aureus). Viridans streptococci are the classic cause of subacute IE from dental bacteremia. S. bovis (now Streptococcus gallolyticus) IE is associated with colon cancer.

**Q:** What are the peripheral stigmata of infective endocarditis and their characteristics?

**A:** Splinter hemorrhages: linear, reddish-brown lesions in nail bed. Janeway lesions: painless, 5 mm erythematous/hemorrhagic macules or papules on soles/palms (vascular phenomenon). Osler's nodes: painful, raised, red/brown, 3–15 mm on digits (immune complex). Roth's spots: retinal hemorrhages with pale centre (immune complex). Petechiae over legs.

Janeway lesions (painless) vs. Osler's nodes (painful) is a classic MCCQE1 distinction. Janeway = embolic/vascular; Osler's = immune complex. The mnemonic FROM JANE: Fever, Roth spots, Osler nodes, Murmur, Janeway lesions, Anemia, Nail-bed hemorrhages, Emboli. These findings combined with bacteremia and echocardiographic abnormalities form the Modified Duke Criteria.

**Q:** How is infective endocarditis diagnosed using the Modified Duke Criteria?

**A:** Definitive: 2 major criteria, OR 1 major + 3 minor, OR 5 minor. Possible: 1 major + 1 minor, OR 3 minor. Major criteria: (1) Microbiologic (typical organisms in ≥2 blood cultures, or atypical in ≥3, or PCR for Coxiella/Bartonella/Whipplei, or single culture/serology for Coxiella); (2) Imaging (echo/CT showing vegetation, abscess, perforation, fistula, new regurgitation, PET/CT abnormality). (3) Surgical evidence. Minor criteria include: predisposing conditions, fever ≥38°C, vascular phenomena, immunologic phenomena, positive blood cultures not meeting major criteria.

The Modified Duke Criteria provide a standardized diagnostic framework. Blood cultures are essential — 3 sets from different sites >1 hour apart. TTE is first-line but TEE has ~90% sensitivity and is indicated for non-diagnostic TTE or suspected prosthetic valve/complicated IE. An ECG showing prolonged PR interval suggests perivalvular abscess.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic treatment for native valve vs. prosthetic valve infective endocarditis?

**A:** Native valve (empiric): vancomycin + ceftriaxone OR gentamicin. Prosthetic valve (empiric): vancomycin + gentamicin + rifampin. Targeted therapy is 4–6 weeks, adjusted based on valve, organism, and susceptibilities.

Empiric treatment must cover S. aureus, coagulase-negative Staphylococcus, and gram-negative organisms. Rifampin is added for prosthetic valves because biofilm formation on foreign material is better penetrated with rifampin. Blood cultures should always be drawn before starting antibiotics. The POET trial showed that carefully selected IE patients can be transitioned to oral antibiotics.

**Q:** What are the surgical indications for infective endocarditis?

**A:** Most common: refractory CHF (most frequent indication). Other indications: valve ring abscess, valve perforation, unstable prosthesis, large vegetation >1 cm, recurrent emboli despite adequate antimicrobial treatment, antimicrobial failure (persistently positive blood cultures), fungal etiology, S. aureus on a prosthetic valve.

Heart failure from acute valvular regurgitation is the leading cause of IE-related death and the primary driver for surgical intervention. S. aureus on a prosthetic valve has high mortality with medical therapy alone, necessitating early surgical consideration. IE with Candida requires surgery given poor response to antifungal therapy alone.

**Q:** What is the antibiotic prophylaxis regimen for IE-high-risk patients undergoing dental procedures?

**A:** Amoxicillin single dose 30–60 minutes prior to procedure. If truly penicillin-allergic: azithromycin or clarithromycin. For infected skin/soft tissue procedures: cephalexin single dose (modify based on etiology).

IE prophylaxis is reserved for high-risk patients (prosthetic valves, previous IE, certain congenital heart diseases, cardiac transplant with valve disease) undergoing procedures that risk bacteremia: dental procedures causing bleeding, respiratory tract incisions/biopsies, or procedures on infected skin/musculoskeletal tissue. GI/GU procedures no longer require routine prophylaxis.

**Q:** What are the common organisms causing bacterial meningitis grouped by age?

**A:** Age <3 months: GBS, E. coli, L. monocytogenes, Klebsiella. Age >3 months (childhood): S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis, H. influenzae, L. monocytogenes. Adults: S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis, L. monocytogenes (especially if age >50 or comorbidities). Viral: HSV-1/2, enteroviruses, VZV, parechoviruses, West Nile. Fungal: Cryptococcus, Coccidioides.

Neonates are especially vulnerable to GBS (from maternal colonization) and gram-negative enteric organisms. L. monocytogenes is classically added to coverage for patients >50 years, pregnant women, and immunocompromised patients. S. pneumoniae remains the most common and most lethal cause of bacterial meningitis in adults.

**Q:** What are Kernig's sign and Brudzinski's sign, and how sensitive are they for meningitis?

**A:** Brudzinski's sign: passive neck flexion causes involuntary flexion of hips and knees. Kernig's sign: resistance to knee extension when hip is flexed. Jolt accentuation: headache worsens when head turned horizontally 2–3 times per second — more sensitive than Brudzinski's and Kernig's. These signs have limited sensitivity; absence of fever, neck stiffness, and altered mental status effectively rules out meningitis.

Classical meningeal signs have poor sensitivity individually. A JAMA 1999 systematic review showed that clinical history items have low accuracy for meningitis diagnosis in adults. However, the triad of fever + neck stiffness + altered mental status is highly specific. In high-risk patients, clinicians should proceed directly to LP given the serious implications of missing bacterial meningitis.

**Q:** What are the typical CSF profiles for bacterial vs. viral meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial: glucose decreased, protein markedly increased, WBC 500–10,000/μL, predominant cell type = neutrophils. Viral: glucose normal, protein increased (mildly), WBC 10–500/μL, predominant cell type = lymphocytes.

In bacterial meningitis, glucose is decreased because bacteria consume glucose and impair transport (CSF:serum glucose ratio <0.6 is abnormal). Protein is markedly elevated due to blood-brain barrier breakdown and cellular exudate. High WBC with neutrophil predominance indicates purulent meningitis. Early viral meningitis can occasionally show a neutrophil predominance, making clinical correlation essential.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic treatment for bacterial meningitis by age group, and what key adjunct therapy should be given?

**A:** Age <28 days: ampicillin + cefotaxime. Age 29 days–3 months: ceftriaxone/cefotaxime + vancomycin + ampicillin. Age >3 months: ceftriaxone + vancomycin. Add ampicillin IV if L. monocytogenes risk (age >50, alcohol use disorder, immunocompromised). Adjunct: dexamethasone IV within 20 minutes prior to or with first antibiotic dose (continue only if pneumococcal meningitis confirmed; discontinue for non-pneumococcal).

Bacterial meningitis is a medical emergency — antibiotics must NOT be delayed for CT or LP. Vancomycin is added to ceftriaxone to cover penicillin-resistant S. pneumoniae. Dexamethasone reduces inflammation and decreases mortality/morbidity (particularly deafness) in pneumococcal meningitis by attenuating the inflammatory cascade triggered by bacterial lysis.

**Q:** What are the complications and mortality rates of bacterial meningitis by organism?

**A:** Complications: death, headache, seizures, cerebral edema, hydrocephalus, SIADH, residual neurological deficits (especially CN VIII damage causing deafness). Mortality: S. pneumoniae ~25%; N. meningitidis 5–10%; H. influenzae ~5%. Worse prognosis: extremes of age, delayed treatment, stupor/coma, seizures, focal neurological signs, septic shock at presentation.

Pneumococcal meningitis carries the highest mortality among common organisms. Sensorineural hearing loss (CN VIII) is the most common neurological sequela of bacterial meningitis overall. Early dexamethasone reduces the incidence of hearing loss. N. meningitidis causes fulminant purpura fulminans via DIC and carries epidemic potential.

**Q:** What are the prophylaxis recommendations for close contacts of meningitis cases?

**A:** H. influenzae type B: rifampin prophylaxis for household contacts if they live with an inadequately immunized (<4 yr) or immunocompromised (<18 yr) child. N. meningitidis: ciprofloxacin, rifampin, or ceftriaxone for close/household contacts; meningococcal vaccines recommended for post-exposure prophylaxis and outbreak control.

Close contacts of meningococcal meningitis are at significantly elevated risk due to shared respiratory secretions. Single-dose ciprofloxacin is the preferred post-exposure prophylaxis in adults. Meningococcal vaccines (quadrivalent and serogroup B) should be offered in outbreak settings and to immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** What is encephalitis, how does it differ from meningitis, and what is the most important empiric treatment to initiate?

**A:** Encephalitis = inflammation of brain parenchyma, with abnormal cerebral function (altered LOC, seizures, focal neurological signs, abnormal speech/movement). Meningitis = inflammation of meninges, with preserved cerebral function. Overlap ('meningoencephalitis') is common. Most important empiric treatment: IV acyclovir empirically until HSV encephalitis is ruled out (due to high mortality).

HSV encephalitis is the most important cause to identify and treat empirically because it is treatable and has very high mortality if untreated. It causes acute necrotizing encephalitis predominantly in the medial temporal and inferior frontal lobes. CT/MRI shows temporal lobe changes; EEG shows focal slowing and periodic discharges. PCR of CSF for HSV is the diagnostic test of choice.

**Q:** What are the characteristic features of herpes simplex encephalitis (HSE)?

**A:** Acute onset (<1 week) of focal neurological signs: hemiparesis, ataxia, aphasia, focal/generalized seizures. Temporal lobe involvement: behavioural disturbance. Rapidly progressive over days, may result in coma or death. CT/MRI: medial temporal lobe necrosis. EEG: early focal slowing, periodic discharges. Common sequelae: memory and behavioural disturbances. Rare complication 1 month post-treatment: Kluver-Bucy syndrome features.

HSE is caused predominantly by HSV-1 (reactivation of latent virus in trigeminal ganglia) and less often by HSV-2. The temporal lobe predilection causes personality and memory changes resembling psychiatric illness initially. Kluver-Bucy syndrome (hypersexuality, hyperorality, visual agnosia) occurring weeks post-treatment is a recognized rare sequela. PCR of CSF for HSV has >95% sensitivity.

**Q:** Describe the pathophysiology, clinical features, and treatment of generalized tetanus.

**A:** Pathophysiology: Clostridium tetani (anaerobic GP bacillus) spores contaminate wounds → produce tetanus toxin → retrograde axonal transport to CNS → blocks inhibitory neurotransmitter (GABA) release → disinhibition of spinal motor reflexes → tetany and autonomic hyperactivity. Clinical features: trismus ('lockjaw'), sustained skeletal muscle contraction with painful spasms (triggered by stimuli), respiratory failure from pharyngeal/respiratory muscle contraction, autonomic hyperactivity (diaphoresis, tachycardia, HTN, fever). Treatment: wound debridement + IV metronidazole (or penicillin G) + tetanus immune globulin (TIG) + benzodiazepines + early tracheostomy + autonomic control (labetalol, magnesium sulfate). Vaccination post-diagnosis (infection does not confer immunity).

Tetanus toxin (tetanospasmin) irreversibly binds presynaptic inhibitory neurons (Renshaw cells) preventing glycine and GABA release. Antimicrobial therapy alone is insufficient without wound debridement to eliminate spore burden. TIG neutralizes circulating unbound toxin but cannot reverse bound toxin — early treatment is critical.

**Q:** What are the 5 stages of rabies infection and what are the two forms of the acute neurologic syndrome?

**A:** 1. Incubation: 1–3 months average (days to years). 2. Prodrome (<1 week): low-grade fever, malaise, pain/paresthesia at wound site — once prodrome begins, rapid irreversible progression. 3. Acute neurologic syndrome: (a) Encephalitic (most common): hyperactivity, fluctuating LOC, hydrophobia, aerophobia, hypersalivation, seizures; (b) Paralytic: quadriplegia, loss of anal sphincter tone, fever. 4. Coma: complete flaccid paralysis, respiratory/cardiovascular failure. 5. Death (within days to weeks).

Hydrophobia and aerophobia are pathognomonic of encephalitic rabies — painful pharyngeal spasms are triggered by water and air stimuli respectively. The incubation period length is inversely related to distance of bite from the CNS (facial bites have shorter incubation). Once clinical symptoms appear, the disease is nearly universally fatal — prevention via post-exposure prophylaxis is critical.

**Q:** What is the post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) protocol for rabies in a non-previously-immunized individual?

**A:** 1. Wound care: clean immediately with soap and running water for 15 minutes. 2. Passive immunization: rabies immunoglobulin (RIG) infiltrated into wound, with remaining volume IM at distant anatomical site. 3. Active immunization: inactivated human diploid cell rabies virus vaccine (HDCV) series — 4 shots on days 0, 3, 7, and 14 into deltoid (NOT gluteal, due to variable response). For previously immunized: wound care + 2 doses HDCV on days 0 and 3 only (no RIG needed).

RIG provides immediate passive immunity while vaccine-induced active immunity develops. RIG should NOT be given into the same site as vaccine. Deltoid administration is required; gluteal injection produces variable, often inadequate antibody responses. Animal bites/contacts potentially causing rabies must be reported to Public Health authorities in Canada.

**Q:** How is sepsis defined using the Sepsis-3 criteria, and how does it differ from septic shock?

**A:** Sepsis: life-threatening organ dysfunction from dysregulated host response to infection, identified by acute SOFA score increase ≥2 points. Quick-SOFA (qSOFA) screening: ≥2 of (1) respiratory rate ≥22/min, (2) sBP <100 mmHg, (3) altered mentation (GCS <15) — SOFA score >2 = ~10% mortality. Septic shock: subset of sepsis with circulatory and cellular/metabolic dysfunction; clinically: despite adequate fluid resuscitation, (1) vasopressors needed to maintain MAP ≥65 mmHg AND (2) serum lactate >2 mmol/L → hospital mortality >40%.

Sepsis-3 (2016) eliminated the terms 'severe sepsis' and 'SIRS' from clinical use. The SOFA score assesses organ dysfunction across respiratory, coagulation, hepatic, cardiovascular, CNS, and renal domains. Serum lactate >2 mmol/L despite adequate resuscitation defines septic shock metabolically. Early identification is critical as mortality rises sharply with each hour of delayed antibiotics.

**Q:** What are the key treatment principles for sepsis and septic shock?

**A:** 1. Respiratory support: O₂ + intubation as needed. 2. Cardiovascular support: IV fluids + blood transfusion + vasopressors + ICU. 3. IV antibiotics (empiric, guided by suspected source) — broad-spectrum (e.g. piperacillin/tazobactam or meropenem); add aminoglycosides for drug-resistant GNB or vancomycin for MRSA; narrow once sensitivities known. 4. Source control: remove catheter, drain abscess. 5. Hydrocortisone IV for septic shock unresponsive to fluids and vasopressors.

The breadth of empiric coverage should consider estimated adequacy of spectrum vs. degree of instability. Causative organisms are identified in only 50–70% of cases; when identified, GP and GN organisms account for 90%. Hydrocortisone (200 mg/day) in refractory septic shock is supported by Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines to correct relative adrenal insufficiency.

**Q:** What is leprosy (Hansen's disease), and how does it classify into tuberculoid vs. lepromatous forms?

**A:** Caused by Mycobacterium leprae (obligate intracellular, slow-growing, survives in macrophages). Transmitted via nasal secretions or skin lesions; invades skin and peripheral nerves → chronic granulomatous disease affecting cooler body tissues. Paucibacillary (tuberculoid): intact cell-mediated immunity, 1–5 skin lesions, bacilli absent on smear. Multibacillary (lepromatous): weak cell-mediated immunity, ≥6 lesions OR nerve involvement OR bacilli on slit-skin smear. Borderline: intermediate between the two.

Leprosy is a spectral disease where the host immune response determines clinical form. Strong Th1 response (tuberculoid) limits bacterial spread but causes nerve destruction via granulomatous inflammation. Weak Th1 response (lepromatous) allows unchecked bacillary proliferation with widespread skin and nerve involvement. The cooler extremities (hands, feet, earlobes) are preferentially affected due to optimal temperature for M. leprae replication.

**Q:** What is the WHO-recommended treatment for paucibacillary vs. multibacillary leprosy?

**A:** Paucibacillary: dapsone daily + rifampin monthly + clofazimine monthly AND low-dose clofazimine daily × 6 months. Multibacillary: dapsone daily + rifampin monthly + clofazimine monthly × 12 months AND low-dose clofazimine daily × 12 months.

Multi-drug therapy (MDT) prevents resistance to any single agent. Rifampin is the most bactericidal agent against M. leprae. Clofazimine has anti-inflammatory properties in addition to bacteriostatic activity. Treatment can trigger immune reactions: erythema nodosum leprosum (treated with thalidomide or prednisone) and reversal reaction (treated with prednisone). Clofazimine causes hyperpigmentation, a major cause of social stigma.

**Q:** What is Lyme disease, what tick transmits it, and what are the three stages of clinical disease?

**A:** Caused by Borrelia burgdorferi (North America) spirochete, transmitted by Ixodes tick (requires >36 hours attachment). In Canada: southern Ontario/Quebec, Manitoba, New Brunswick, Nova Scotia, southern BC. Stage 1 (early localized, 7–14 days): malaise, fatigue, headache + erythema migrans (expanding non-pruritic bull's-eye target lesion at bite site). Stage 2 (early disseminated, weeks): CNS (aseptic meningitis, CN VII palsy, peripheral neuritis), cardiac (heart block, myocarditis). Stage 3 (late persistent, months–years): MSK (chronic mono/oligoarticular arthritis), neurologic (encephalopathy, neuropathy), acrodermatitis chronicum atrophicans (B. afzelii).

Erythema migrans is pathognomonic of Stage 1 Lyme disease and is a clinical diagnosis — serology may be negative this early. The mnemonic BAKE summarizes key Lyme manifestations: Bell's palsy (CN VII), Arthritis, heart blocK, Erythema migrans. Climate change is expanding the geographic range of Ixodes ticks in Canada, increasing Lyme disease incidence.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Lyme disease by stage, and what is the post-exposure prophylaxis regimen?

**A:** Stage 1: doxycycline, amoxicillin, or cefuroxime (oral). Stage 2–3: ceftriaxone IV or doxycycline. Post-exposure prophylaxis: single dose doxycycline within 72 hours of removal of an engorged Ixodes scapularis tick in a hyperendemic area (local tick infection rate ≥20%), for patients >8 years who are not pregnant or lactating.

Oral doxycycline is the preferred treatment for most stages in non-pregnant adults. Ceftriaxone IV is used for neurologic and cardiac involvement. Post-exposure prophylaxis with single-dose doxycycline is only indicated under specific conditions (engorged tick, hyperendemic area, adequate time since attachment) — routine prophylaxis for all tick bites is not recommended. Serology in Stage 1 may be negative (primarily clinical diagnosis).

**Q:** What is Toxic Shock Syndrome (TSS), what organisms cause it, and what is the mechanism?

**A:** TSS is caused by superantigens produced by S. aureus (TSST-1) or Group A Streptococcus (SPEA, SPEB, SPEC). Superantigens cause non-specific, widespread T-cell activation and massive pro-inflammatory cytokine release (IL-1, IL-6, TNF), leading to acute fever, shock, and multi-organ failure. The course is precipitous.

Unlike conventional antigens that activate 0.01% of T cells, superantigens activate up to 20–30% of all T cells simultaneously by binding outside the normal antigen-binding groove. This cytokine storm drives the clinical syndrome. Staphylococcal TSS is associated with tampon use, wound infections, and nasal packing. Streptococcal TSS often complicates invasive GAS infections (necrotizing fasciitis).

**Q:** Which CSF Gram stain findings correspond to N. meningitidis, H. influenzae, L. monocytogenes, and S. pneumoniae?
