**A:** N. meningitidis: Gram-negative diplococci. H. influenzae: pleomorphic Gram-negative coccobacilli. L. monocytogenes: Gram-positive rods. S. pneumoniae: Gram-positive diplococci (lancet-shaped).

Rapid identification of organisms on Gram stain allows early directed therapy before culture results. Gram stain sensitivity in bacterial meningitis is approximately 60–90%, highest for S. pneumoniae and N. meningitidis. The morphology and staining directly guide initial antibiotic selection while culture results are pending.

**Q:** What are the indications for adding ampicillin to meningitis empiric therapy, and which organism does it cover?

**A:** Ampicillin is added to cover Listeria monocytogenes in patients with risk factors: age >50 years, alcohol use disorder, or immunocompromised status. L. monocytogenes is intrinsically resistant to all cephalosporins, so ceftriaxone + vancomycin alone is insufficient coverage.

L. monocytogenes has natural resistance to cephalosporins due to low affinity penicillin-binding proteins. It enters macrophages and spreads cell-to-cell, evading humoral immunity. Ampicillin (with or without gentamicin for synergy) is the drug of choice. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole is used in true penicillin allergy. Risk factors reflect impaired cell-mediated immunity needed to control Listeria.

**Q:** What ECG finding in infective endocarditis suggests perivalvular abscess, and what are the indications for TTE vs. TEE?

**A:** ECG finding: prolonged PR interval (suggests aortic root/perivalvular abscess extending to AV node). TTE: indicated for all suspected IE (poor sensitivity, inadequate in 20% due to obesity/COPD/chest wall deformities). TEE (~90% sensitivity): indicated when TTE is non-diagnostic in patients with at least possible endocarditis, or when prosthetic valve endocarditis or complicated endocarditis (e.g. paravalvular abscess/perforation) is suspected.

A perivalvular abscess (most common at aortic root) can track to the fibrous skeleton of the heart, interrupting the AV node and causing progressive PR prolongation or complete heart block. TEE provides superior visualization of the posterior structures and prosthetic valves due to proximity of the transducer to the heart. TEE is also essential for surgical planning.

**Q:** What are the pre-exposure vaccination recommendations for rabies in Canada?

**A:** Recommended for high-risk persons: laboratory staff working with rabies virus, veterinarians, animal and wildlife control workers, long-term travellers to endemic areas. Use standard precautions when caring for rabies patients.

Pre-exposure prophylaxis simplifies post-exposure management (only 2 doses of vaccine needed, no RIG) and provides a safety buffer. Canada's Public Health Agency requires mandatory reporting of potential rabies exposures. In Canada, bats are the most important reservoir for human rabies transmission, and any bat contact (including sleeping in the same room) may warrant PEP consideration.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic test of choice for post-mortem rabies confirmation, and what inclusion bodies are found?

**A:** Post-mortem: direct immunofluorescence in nerve tissue + identification of Negri bodies (intracytoplasmic inclusion bodies in neurons, particularly in hippocampal Purkinje cells). Antemortem: direct immunofluorescence or PCR on saliva, skin biopsy (nuchal skin), serum, and CSF.

Negri bodies represent aggregates of viral nucleocapsid (ribonucleoprotein) and are pathognomonic of rabies on histopathology. They are most reliably found in Purkinje cells of the cerebellum and pyramidal cells of the hippocampus. Antemortem diagnosis allows public health containment and identification of exposed contacts requiring PEP.

**Q:** What tick-borne diseases other than Lyme disease are expected to increase in Canada due to climate change?

**A:** Anaplasmosis, Babesiosis, Powassan virus, and Borrelia miyamotoi disease — all transmitted by Ixodes ticks and expected to increase in prevalence as tick ranges expand northward into Canada due to warmer winters.

Climate change is driving northward expansion of the Ixodes scapularis (blacklegged tick) range in Canada. A negative Lyme disease test does not rule out other tick-borne illnesses — further testing may be needed if symptoms persist. Babesiosis presents like malaria (hemolytic anemia); Anaplasmosis causes leukopenia/thrombocytopenia; Powassan virus causes severe encephalitis.

**Q:** What are the three major endemic mycoses in North America, and how are they acquired?

**A:** Histoplasmosis (Histoplasma capsulatum), blastomycosis (Blastomyces dermatitidis), and coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides immitis). All are acquired through inhalation of spores from the environment (soil, bird/bat droppings, vegetation) or rarely inoculation injury. They are thermally dimorphic: mould at cold temperatures, yeast at body temperature.

Endemic mycoses are a key MCCQE1 topic. Previously defined geographic regions of endemicity are expanding and can no longer be used to definitively rule out infections. All three can cause pneumonia and may disseminate hematogenously, especially in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** A patient from Ohio presents with fever, cough, chest pain, headache, and myalgia. CXR shows pulmonary infiltrates with hilar lymphadenopathy. What is the most likely diagnosis and how is it confirmed?

**A:** Histoplasmosis (Histoplasma capsulatum). Confirmed by fungal culture, fungal stain, antigen detection in urine and serum, or serology. Histoplasmosis is commonly associated with exposure to chicken coops, bird roosts, and bat caves.

Histoplasma capsulatum is endemic to the Ohio and Mississippi River valleys in central USA, and in Ontario and Quebec. CXR in acute disease shows pulmonary infiltrates and hilar lymphadenopathy; chronic disease may show cavitary lesions. Dissemination (to bone marrow, GI tract, lymph nodes, skin, liver, adrenals, CNS) occurs primarily in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** What is the characteristic skin finding of disseminated blastomycosis, and how does it differ from the typical pulmonary presentation?

**A:** Disseminated blastomycosis causes verrucous (wart-like) skin lesions that mimic skin cancer, ulcers, and subcutaneous nodules. Other disseminated sites include bones (osteomyelitis, osteolytic lesions) and genitourinary tract (prostatitis, epididymitis). The pulmonary form presents as acute or chronic pneumonia with fever, cough, chest pain, chills, night sweats, and weight loss.

Blastomyces dermatitidis is endemic to states east of the Mississippi River, Northern Ontario, and along the Great Lakes. Diagnosis is made by sputum smear and culture, or direct examination of clinical specimens showing characteristic broad-based budding yeast. The verrucous skin lesions are a classic exam finding.

**Q:** What is 'Valley Fever' and what are its characteristic features?

**A:** Valley Fever is the primary pulmonary form of coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides immitis infection). It presents as subacute fever, chills, cough, chest pain, sore throat, and fatigue lasting weeks to months. A hypersensitivity reaction with arthralgias and erythema nodosum may develop. Rare dissemination occurs to skin (ulcers), joints (synovitis), bones (lytic lesions), and meninges (meningitis).

Coccidioides immitis is endemic to deserts in the southwestern USA and northwestern Mexico. It is a common opportunistic infection in HIV patients. Diagnosis is by sputum culture or direct examination for characteristic yeast forms. Coccidioidal meningitis is the most feared complication.

**Q:** What is the CD4 threshold for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) prophylaxis in HIV-positive patients, and what is the preferred prophylactic agent?

**A:** HIV-positive patients with CD4 count <200 cells/mm³ should receive prophylaxis with TMP/SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole). Without prophylaxis, these patients have an 80% lifetime risk of PJP.

PJP (formerly PCP - Pneumocystis carinii pneumonia) is the most common AIDS-defining opportunistic infection. Most cases occur in patients unaware of HIV status or not using prophylaxis. In HIV-negative patients, PJP occurs almost exclusively in immunocompromised patients (transplant, inflammatory conditions, hematologic malignancies).

**Q:** Describe the classic CXR finding in PJP and the laboratory hallmarks used in diagnosis.

**A:** Classic CXR: bilateral symmetrical interstitial infiltrates (bilateral diffuse opacities). Laboratory hallmarks: reduced pO₂ and elevated A-a gradient on ABG, elevated serum LDH (>220 IU/L). Diagnosis confirmed by induced sputum or lower airway sampling (BAL) positive for Pneumocystis.

The hallmark triad of PJP diagnosis is bilateral interstitial infiltrates on CXR, elevated LDH, and hypoxemia with elevated A-a gradient. Pleural effusions are uncommon. Most clinical disease results from reactivation of latent infection or reinfection in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for PJP and when are corticosteroids added?

**A:** First-line: TMP/SMX (PO or IV), even for those already taking it for prophylaxis. Alternatives: dapsone + TMP, clindamycin + primaquine, atovaquone, or pentamidine IV (second-line for severe disease). Corticosteroids are added as adjuvant therapy in severe hypoxia: pO₂ <70 mmHg OR A-a gradient >35 mmHg.

Adjuvant corticosteroids in severe PJP reduce mortality by dampening the inflammatory response to dying organisms. The threshold for corticosteroid use (pO₂ <70 mmHg or A-a O₂ gradient >35 mmHg) is a classic MCCQE1 fact.

**Q:** What are the two main Cryptococcus species, how do they differ in host predilection, and what is their geographic distribution?

**A:** C. neoformans: inhaled from soil contaminated with pigeon droppings; tends to affect immunocompromised hosts. C. gattii: found in certain tree species (Eucalyptus, Douglas fir); tends to affect immunocompetent hosts. C. gattii geographic distribution includes Vancouver Island, Northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea.

Cryptococcosis is a key HIV-related opportunistic infection. C. neoformans is the leading cause of meningitis in patients with HIV. The distinction between C. neoformans (immunocompromised) and C. gattii (immunocompetent) is clinically important and frequently tested.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic test of choice for cryptococcal meningitis and what is the sensitivity of India ink stain?

**A:** CSF cryptococcal antigen test (or lateral flow cryptococcal antigen assay from serum and CSF) is now preferred. India ink stain has only ~50% sensitivity (higher in HIV patients) and has largely been replaced by the cryptococcal antigen test. Culture confirms the diagnosis. Opening pressure must be measured on lumbar puncture.

In HIV patients with cryptococcal meningitis, elevated ICP is common and requires management with serial lumbar punctures or other ICP-lowering measures. Serum cryptococcal antigen is also useful for screening.

**Q:** Describe the treatment regimen for cryptococcal meningitis in HIV-positive patients.

**A:** Induction (2 weeks): Amphotericin B + flucytosine. Consolidation (≥8 weeks): fluconazole. Maintenance: lower-dose fluconazole for prolonged period. Serial lumbar puncture or other ICP management is an important adjunct.

The three-phase approach (induction, consolidation, maintenance) for cryptococcal meningitis in HIV is a classic MCCQE1 treatment protocol. Amphotericin B has significant nephrotoxicity, hence the time-limited induction phase followed by oral azole therapy.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for invasive Candida infection, and how does invasive candidiasis differ from mucocutaneous candidiasis?

**A:** Risk factors for invasive candidiasis: critically ill patients, broad-spectrum antibiotics, central venous catheters, total parenteral nutrition, immunocompromised state (DM, corticosteroids). Invasive disease includes candidemia, endophthalmitis, endocarditis, upper UTI, and hepatosplenic disease — systemic and life-threatening. Mucocutaneous disease (thrush, esophagitis, vulvovaginitis, diaper rash) is superficial and treated topically or with oral fluconazole.

Candida albicans is a normal part of skin, mouth, vaginal, and GI microbiome. Overgrowth leads to disease. A classic finding of cutaneous candidiasis is satellite lesions beyond the margin of the main rash. Treatment escalates from topical (mild mucocutaneous) to fluconazole to echinocandins for systemic disease.

**Q:** What are the three clinical syndromes of Aspergillus infection and their key distinguishing features?

**A:** 1. ABPA (Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis): IgE-mediated asthma-type reaction; dyspnea, high fever, transient pulmonary infiltrates; occurs in asthmatics/atopics. 2. Aspergilloma (fungus ball): ball of hyphae in pre-existing lung cavity; range from asymptomatic to massive hemoptysis; CXR shows 'air crescent' sign (round opacity with thin lucent rim, upper lobes). 3. Invasive Aspergillosis: severe neutropenia or transplant; fever, cough, dyspnea, cavitation; CXR shows 'halo' sign (nodule with surrounding ground glass); fatal if untreated.

The 'air crescent' sign (aspergilloma) and 'halo' sign (invasive aspergillosis) are classic radiographic findings. Aflatoxin from A. flavus is associated with hepatocellular carcinoma and is responsible for mycotoxicosis.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for invasive aspergillosis and for ABPA?

**A:** Invasive aspergillosis: voriconazole (first-line) or amphotericin B. ABPA: corticosteroids + itraconazole. Aspergilloma: surgical resection ± bronchial artery embolization for severe hemoptysis.

Voriconazole replaced amphotericin B as first-line for invasive aspergillosis due to improved survival and tolerability. ABPA is primarily an allergic/inflammatory condition requiring immune suppression (steroids) plus antifungal to reduce fungal burden.

**Q:** How is Entamoeba histolytica transmitted and what are its major clinical manifestations?

**A:** Transmission: fecal-oral route via cysts from areas of poor sanitation, or via sexual activity (MSM). Clinical manifestations: (1) Asymptomatic carriage; (2) Amoebic dysentery: abdominal pain, colitis, bloody diarrhea, fever from ulceration of large intestine; (3) Amoebic abscesses: most commonly liver (right upper quadrant pain, weight loss, fever, hepatomegaly), also lungs and brain.

E. histolytica is microscopically indistinguishable from the non-pathogenic E. dispar; distinction requires specific stool antigen detection, multiplex stool PCR, or serology. Treatment: metronidazole for invasive disease, followed by iodoquinol or paromomycin for cyst elimination.

**Q:** What is the treatment for amoebic dysentery and hepatic abscess, and when is aspiration of a hepatic abscess indicated?

**A:** Treatment: Metronidazole (invasive disease), followed by iodoquinol or paromomycin to eliminate intestinal cysts. Aspiration of hepatic abscess is indicated if: risk of rupture, poor response to medical therapy, or diagnostic uncertainty. Asymptomatic cyst shedding: iodoquinol or paromomycin alone.

The two-drug strategy (metronidazole + luminal agent) is key to prevent relapse from residual intestinal cysts. Prevention involves good personal hygiene and water purification by boiling or filtration — importantly, chlorination alone does not reliably kill cysts.

**Q:** What are the clinical features that distinguish Giardia lamblia infection from amoebic dysentery?

**A:** Giardia: watery (not bloody) diarrhea, steatorrhea, bloating, flatulence, no hematochezia, no mucus in stool — no intestinal wall invasion. Chronic giardiasis causes malabsorption with vitamin A/B12/B deficiencies and hypoalbuminemia. Amoebiasis: bloody diarrhea, mucus, ulceration of large intestine, risk of liver abscess.

Giardia ('beaver fever') is transmitted by fecal-oral route, especially in camping/outdoor settings in Canada (e.g., Rocky Mountain streams). Diagnosis: multiple stool samples or multiplex PCR. Treatment: tinidazole (single dose) or nitazoxanide (3 days); metronidazole for children <12 months.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Trichomonas vaginalis infection and how is it treated?

**A:** Often asymptomatic (10–50%), especially in males (occasionally urethritis, prostatitis). In females: profuse, malodorous, yellow-green or grey frothy vaginal discharge, pruritus, dysuria, dyspareunia (trichomonal vaginitis). Diagnosed by wet mount showing motile parasites, antigen detection, culture, or urine PCR (in males). Treatment: metronidazole for patient AND all partners.

T. vaginalis causes ~25% of vaginitis cases. Treating partners simultaneously is essential to prevent reinfection. It is sexually transmitted and classified as an STI.

**Q:** How does Cryptosporidium infection differ in immunocompetent vs. immunocompromised patients, and what is the diagnostic test?

**A:** Immunocompetent: self-limited watery diarrhea. Immunocompromised: chronic, severe, non-bloody diarrhea with nausea/vomiting, abdominal pain, anorexia, weight loss, and potentially death. Diagnosis: modified acid-fast stain of stool for oocysts, direct fluorescent antibody stool antigen detection, or multiplex enteric parasite PCR.

Cryptosporidium is transmitted via fecal-oral route from contaminated water (including from cows). In HIV patients, restoration of immune function (ART to increase CD4 >100) is key to management; nitazoxanide or macrolides may be tried if ART fails.

**Q:** What Plasmodium species causes the most severe malaria, and what are its distinguishing features compared to P. vivax and P. ovale?

**A:** P. falciparum causes the most severe and most lethal malaria. Key features: less predictable fever pattern (vs. 48-h cycles for P. vivax/P. ovale, 72-h for P. malariae), >90% of infected patients ill within 30 days, chloroquine-resistant in most areas. Severe complications: cerebral malaria (seizures, coma), severe anemia, AKI, ARDS. P. vivax and P. ovale produce dormant liver hypnozoites causing relapsing attacks months later.

The periodicity of fever (48h vs 72h vs irregular) and the capacity for relapse (P. vivax/ovale hypnozoites) are classic exam distinctions. P. knowlesi and rarely P. vivax can also be fatal.

**Q:** How is malaria diagnosed and what are the laboratory hallmarks?

**A:** Blood smear q12-24h x3 (Giemsa stain): thick smear for presence of organisms, thin smear for species identification and quantification. CBC triad: thrombocytopenia + elevated LDH + anemia (without leukocytosis). Rapid antigen detection tests and PCR are also available.

The CBC triad of thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, and anemia without leukocytosis in a febrile traveller should prompt malaria workup. Three blood smears q12-24h are needed to rule out infection due to cyclical parasitemia.

**Q:** What is the treatment for P. falciparum malaria and P. vivax/P. ovale malaria?

**A:** P. falciparum: Most areas are chloroquine-resistant. Use artemisinin combination therapy (e.g., artesunate + doxycycline, or artemether-lumefantrine), or atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone), or quinine + doxycycline/clindamycin. Check local resistance patterns. P. vivax/P. ovale: Chloroquine + primaquine or tafenoquine (to eradicate liver hypnozoites and prevent relapse). Chloroquine-resistant P. vivax: atovaquone/proguanil + primaquine/tafenoquine.

Primaquine/tafenoquine are essential for P. vivax and P. ovale to eliminate dormant liver hypnozoites and prevent relapse. G6PD testing is required before using primaquine/tafenoquine (risk of hemolysis). Mefloquine and artemisinin resistance is increasing in Southeast Asia.

**Q:** What is Chagas disease (American trypanosomiasis), how is it transmitted, and what are its chronic complications?

**A:** Chagas disease is caused by Trypanosoma cruzi, transmitted by the reduviid 'kissing bug' (feces rubbed into bite site or mucous membranes). Also transmitted via blood transfusion, organ transplantation, vertical (placental) transfer, and ingestion of contaminated food/drink. Chronic complications (30–40% of infected, 10–25 years later): dilated cardiomyopathy, esophagomegaly, megacolon.

Endemic in Mexico, Central and South America. Acute phase: usually asymptomatic; 'Romana's sign' (unilateral periorbital swelling) if conjunctival inoculation; 'Chagoma' (local swelling at bite site). The chronic determinate phase can cause fatal cardiomyopathy. Treatment: benznidazole or nifurtimox (acute/indeterminate phases).

**Q:** What is 'Romana's sign' and what does it indicate?

**A:** Romana's sign is unilateral periorbital swelling (palpebral edema and conjunctivitis) resulting from Trypanosoma cruzi inoculation through the conjunctiva. It is a classic sign of acute Chagas disease.

Romana's sign indicates conjunctival entry point for the trypomastigotes. Most acute Chagas is asymptomatic. The Chagoma (local swelling at the skin inoculation site) is the equivalent for skin entry. Both are signs of acute infection.

**Q:** What are the major routes of Toxoplasma gondii transmission and who is at highest risk for severe disease?

**A:** Transmission: ingestion of oocysts from cat feces (gardening without gloves, emptying litter boxes), undercooked meat (tissue cysts), transplacental transmission (primary maternal infection during pregnancy), organ transplantation, blood transfusion, contaminated water. Highest risk for severe disease: immunocompromised patients (HIV with CD4 <200) and fetuses/neonates (congenital toxoplasmosis).

1/3 of Ontario's population is infected with Toxoplasma gondii. Most immunocompetent adults are asymptomatic or have a mononucleosis-like syndrome. Infection remains latent for life and reactivates with immunosuppression causing CNS ring-enhancing lesions.

**Q:** What is the classic CNS finding in toxoplasmosis in an HIV patient, and how is it treated?

**A:** Classic finding: single or multiple ring-enhancing masses on CT scan (cortex or deep nuclei), with headache and focal neurological signs — toxoplasmic encephalitis. Treatment for immunocompromised patients: pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine + folinic acid. HIV patients: TMP/SMX (also used for prophylaxis and treatment).

Ring-enhancing CNS lesions in an HIV patient with CD4 <200 have a broad differential (toxoplasmosis, CNS lymphoma, abscess). Empiric anti-toxoplasma treatment is often started — clinical/radiologic improvement at 2 weeks supports the diagnosis. Folinic acid (not folic acid) is added to counteract pyrimethamine's antifolate effects.

**Q:** What is congenital toxoplasmosis and what are its manifestations?

**A:** Congenital toxoplasmosis results from acute primary T. gondii infection during pregnancy (a TORCH infection). Can cause stillbirth (rare), or classic tetrad: chorioretinitis, intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus, and psychomotor delay. Initially asymptomatic infants may develop reactivation chorioretinitis (blurred vision, scotoma, ocular pain, photophobia) as adolescents or adults.

Treatment in pregnancy: spiramycin if fetal infection status unknown; pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine + folinic acid if confirmed fetal infection. Pregnant women should avoid undercooked meat and not empty cat litter boxes.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Ascaris lumbricoides infection and what is Löffler's syndrome?

**A:** Treatment: mebendazole OR albendazole OR pyrantel pamoate OR ivermectin. Löffler's syndrome is a transient pulmonary infiltrate with eosinophilia caused by larval migration through the lungs, presenting with cough and dyspnea. This can occur with both Ascaris lumbricoides and Strongyloides stercoralis.

Ascaris is the most common intestinal roundworm worldwide (tropical/subtropical areas). Heavy infections cause intestinal blockage. The life cycle involves larval migration through the lungs (causing Löffler's syndrome) before larvae mature in the small intestine.

**Q:** What is the diagnosis and treatment of pinworm (Enterobius vermicularis) infection?

**A:** Diagnosis: sticky tape test applied to perianal skin (need 5–7 tests to rule out; eggs adhere to tape). Examination of perianal skin at night may reveal adult worms. Usually no eosinophilia (no tissue invasion). Treatment: mebendazole or albendazole; pyrantel pamoate (in pregnancy). Treat all family members simultaneously. Prevention: change underwear, bathe in morning, wear pajamas to bed, wash hands, trim fingernails.

Pinworm is the most common helminth infection in Canada and other developed countries. Females migrate out of the anus at night to deposit eggs in perianal skin, causing pruritus ani. Reinfection is common due to fecal-oral self-inoculation.

**Q:** What makes Strongyloides stercoralis unique among intestinal nematodes, and who is at risk for hyperinfection?

**A:** Strongyloides is one of the few worms able to complete its life cycle and multiply within the human host (autoinfection) — larvae penetrate GI mucosa or perianal skin. Hyperinfection (massive auto-infection) can be fatal, particularly in immunocompromised patients. The most common risk factor for disseminated infection is immunoablative therapy including high-dose corticosteroids. Treatment: ivermectin and/or albendazole.

Transmission: skin penetration from fecal-contaminated soil (barefoot walking). Presents with pruritic dermatitis at penetration site, transient pulmonary symptoms (eosinophilic pneumonitis/Löffler's syndrome), abdominal pain, diarrhea, larva currens (itchy skin rash). Screening for Strongyloides before initiating immunosuppression is important in those from endemic areas.

**Q:** What are the clinical consequences of Onchocerca volvulus infection and how is it transmitted and treated?

**A:** Onchocerca volvulus causes river blindness (onchocerciasis) and dermatitis. It is transmitted by blackfly bite in sub-Saharan Africa and Latin America. Treatment: ivermectin + doxycycline.

Microfilariae of Onchocerca migrate through the skin and eyes, causing intense inflammation that leads to progressive visual loss ('river blindness'). Mass drug administration of ivermectin is used for community-level control.

**Q:** What are the clinical manifestations of Wuchereria bancrofti infection and how is it treated?

**A:** Wuchereria bancrofti causes lymphatic filariasis (elephantiasis): lymphadenopathy, lymphedema, lymphatic obstruction, hydrocele, and tropical pulmonary eosinophilia. Transmitted by mosquito bite in the tropics. Treatment: diethylcarbamazine + doxycycline.

The massive limb swelling (elephantiasis) is due to chronic lymphatic obstruction from adult worms living in the lymphatics. Tropical pulmonary eosinophilia is a hypersensitivity reaction to microfilariae in the lungs.

**Q:** What distinguishes Taenia solium (pork tapeworm) from Taenia saginata (beef tapeworm) in terms of serious complications?

**A:** T. solium can cause cysticercosis (ingestion of eggs from human feces): larval cysts lodge in CNS, eyes, and skin causing seizures, mass lesions, and potentially fatal neurocysticercosis. T. saginata (beef tapeworm, from larvae in undercooked beef) only causes mild GI symptoms (taeniasis) — no cysticercosis. Treatment for cysticercosis: corticosteroids + albendazole + praziquantel + antiepileptics if seizures.

The key distinction: T. solium eggs (from human feces) can cause the serious tissue infection cysticercosis, whereas T. saginata does not cause tissue cysts. Neurocysticercosis is the most common cause of adult-onset epilepsy in many developing countries.

**Q:** What vitamin deficiency does Diphyllobothrium latum cause and why?

**A:** D. latum (fish tapeworm, from raw fish) causes vitamin B₁₂ deficiency leading to macrocytic anemia and posterior column deficits (subacute combined degeneration). The tapeworm competes with the host for dietary vitamin B₁₂ in the intestinal lumen. Treatment: praziquantel.

D. latum is found in Europe, North America, and Asia, acquired by eating raw fish. It is the largest tapeworm that infects humans and can persist for decades. Posterior column deficits from B₁₂ deficiency include loss of vibration sense, proprioception, and spastic paraparesis.

**Q:** What is the major risk of Echinococcus granulosus infection and how is it managed?

**A:** E. granulosus (dog tapeworm) causes hydatid cysts in the liver and lungs, which enlarge over years and can cause mass effects or rupture. Risk of anaphylaxis if cystic fluid is released during surgical evacuation. Management options: albendazole + praziquantel alone; surgery + perioperative albendazole; or percutaneous aspiration + perioperative albendazole.

Acquired from dog feces (eggs) in rural, sheep-raising areas. The cysts must be handled carefully during aspiration/surgery to prevent spillage of cyst fluid containing protoscolices, which can cause peritoneal seeding (secondary echinococcosis) and anaphylaxis.

**Q:** What malignancy is associated with Schistosoma haematobium and which Schistosoma species is associated with cholangiocarcinoma?

**A:** S. haematobium: squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) of the bladder (due to chronic granulomatous inflammation from eggs in the bladder wall causing fibrosis and hematuria). Cholangiocarcinoma is associated with Clonorchis sinensis (liver fluke), which lives in bile ducts and causes chronic biliary inflammation.

These parasite-cancer associations are high-yield: Schistosoma haematobium → bladder SCC; Clonorchis sinensis (and Opisthorchis) → cholangiocarcinoma; H. pylori → gastric cancer; EBV → Burkitt lymphoma. S. haematobium also causes hematuria and obstructive uropathy.

**Q:** What is Katayama fever and when does it occur in schistosomiasis?

**A:** Katayama fever is acute schistosomiasis: a hypersensitivity reaction to migrating parasites occurring 4–8 weeks after skin penetration by cercariae. Features: fever, hives, headache, weight loss, cough, abdominal pain, chronic diarrhea, high-grade eosinophilia. Most symptomatic disease occurs in non-immune travellers.

Schistosomiasis has three phases: (1) Swimmer's itch (cercarial dermatitis) at the time of skin penetration; (2) Katayama fever (4–8 weeks post-exposure); (3) Chronic disease (portal hypertension, bladder cancer). Treatment: praziquantel; add prednisone for acute schistosomiasis or neurologic complications.

**Q:** What are the chronic hepatic complications of S. mansoni and S. japonicum schistosomiasis?

**A:** Worms live in mesenteric veins; eggs deposited in portal tracts of liver and bowel cause granulomatous inflammation → periportal (pipestem) fibrosis on liver ultrasound. Heavy infections cause intestinal polyps, portal hypertension, splenomegaly (secondary to portal HTN), and hepatomegaly. Can also cause pulmonary HTN and cor pulmonale.

Chronic hepatic schistosomiasis leads to portal hypertension by periportal fibrosis (not cirrhosis of parenchyma). Liver function is often preserved until late. Risk stratification with liver ultrasound (looking for periportal fibrosis) guides management of chronic complications.

**Q:** Which insect vectors transmit Borrelia burgdorferi, Trypanosoma brucei, and Leishmania spp. respectively?

**A:** Borrelia burgdorferi (Lyme disease): tick. Trypanosoma brucei (African sleeping sickness/HAT): tsetse fly. Leishmania spp. (Leishmaniasis): sandfly.

Insect vector associations are high-yield for MCCQE1: mosquito (malaria, dengue, West Nile, yellow fever, filariasis, Zika, chikungunya, Japanese encephalitis); tick (Lyme, RMSF); tsetse fly (African trypanosomiasis); sandfly (Leishmaniasis, bartonellosis); blackfly (Onchocerciasis); deerfly (Loiasis); flea (plague, tungiasis).

**Q:** What pathogens should be considered in a febrile returned traveller and what is the most common cause?

**A:** Most common: malaria (20–30%). Other causes: non-specific viral/mononucleosis-like syndrome (4–25%), dengue (5%), traveller's diarrhea (10–20%), respiratory tract infections (10–15%), typhoid from Salmonella (2–7%), viral hepatitis (3%), rickettsioses (3%), UTI/STI (2–3%). Rule: fever in a returned traveller from a malaria endemic area is considered malaria until proven otherwise.

The workup of fever in a returned traveller requires thick and thin blood smears x3, CBC, LFTs, blood C&S, urinalysis, plus special tests based on symptoms. Empiric treatment after 48–72h of fever with negative malaria smears: azithromycin + doxycycline (India/SE Asia) or ciprofloxacin + doxycycline (elsewhere).

**Q:** How does incubation period help narrow the differential diagnosis in a returned traveller with fever?

**A:** <21 days: consider malaria, typhoid fever, dengue, chikungunya, Zika, rickettsioses, traveller's diarrhea, respiratory infections (ILI, COVID-19); can exclude HBV and TB. >21 days: consider malaria, TB, typhoid fever; can exclude dengue, chikungunya, traveller's diarrhea, rickettsioses.

Using the earliest and latest possible dates of exposure to calculate the incubation period is a key tool for narrowing the differential in travel medicine. This is tested on MCCQE1 as part of the systematic approach to a febrile returned traveller.

**Q:** What water purification method is effective against Giardia and Cryptosporidium cysts, and what method is NOT effective?

**A:** Effective: boiling water, filtration, and iodine tablets. NOT reliably effective: chlorination alone. Both Giardia and Cryptosporidium cysts are resistant to standard chlorination concentrations used in water treatment.

This is a frequently tested public health point. Boiling (most reliable), filtration (0.1–1 micron pore size), and iodine are effective. Chlorine dioxide is more effective than standard chlorine against Cryptosporidium. Travelers should be advised to boil or filter water in endemic areas.

**Q:** What are the standard travel vaccines recommended before international travel in Canada?

**A:** Standard vaccines (ensure up to date): hepatitis B, MMR, tetanus/diphtheria, varicella, pertussis, polio, influenza, meningococcus. Travel-specific vaccines: hepatitis A/B, Japanese encephalitis, typhoid fever, yellow fever, rabies, ETEC, cholera, chikungunya, dengue.

Yellow fever vaccine may be required for entry into certain countries or upon return from endemic areas. Pre-travel consultation should assess the itinerary, duration, activities, and medical history to individualize vaccine and prophylaxis recommendations.

**Q:** What are the malaria chemoprophylaxis options for travellers, and where does resistance to mefloquine and artemisinin occur?

**A:** Prophylaxis options: chloroquine (limited use due to widespread resistance), mefloquine, atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone), doxycycline. Mefloquine and artemisinin resistance is increasing in Southeast Asia — check local resistance patterns before prescribing. Mefloquine has neuropsychiatric side effects (abnormal dreams, insomnia, anxiety, depressed mood).

Choice of prophylaxis depends on destination, duration, individual risk factors, drug tolerability, and cost. Atovaquone/proguanil and doxycycline are common first-line options for most travelers. The MCCQE1 expects familiarity with the major prophylaxis options and the issue of drug resistance.

**Q:** What pathogens are transmitted by fresh water exposure and what diseases do they cause?

**A:** Leptospira spp. (leptospirosis), Schistosoma spp. (schistosomiasis), Acanthamoeba (granulomatous amoebic encephalitis), Naegleria fowleri (primary amoebic meningoencephalitis — rapidly fatal). Soil exposure adds: Hookworms, Toxocara spp. (visceral larva migrans), Leptospira interrogans.

Naegleria fowleri causes primary amoebic meningoencephalitis (PAM) — rapidly fatal, associated with warm freshwater lakes/rivers. Schistosomiasis is a major concern for travellers to sub-Saharan Africa (e.g., Lake Malawi). Leptospirosis occurs via skin/mucosal contact with water contaminated by infected animal urine.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for staphylococcal toxic shock syndrome (TSS)?

**A:** Tampon use, nasal packing, wound infections (e.g., postpartum vaginal, cesarean, or surgical infections).

Staphylococcal TSS is caused by superantigen toxins (TSST-1) produced by S. aureus. Tampon use creates an anaerobic, protein-rich environment ideal for toxin production. Recognition of these risk factors is key to MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for streptococcal toxic shock syndrome (TSS)?

**A:** Minor trauma, surgical procedures, preceding viral illness (e.g., chickenpox), use of NSAIDs.

Group A Streptococcus (GAS) TSS often follows skin/soft tissue infection. NSAIDs may mask early symptoms and impair leukocyte function. Varicella is a classic predisposing condition in children.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for staphylococcal TSS?

**A:** Fever >38.9°C, SBP <90 mmHg, diffuse erythroderma with subsequent desquamation (especially palms/soles), and involvement of ≥3 organ systems: GI, muscular (elevated CK), mucous membranes, renal, hepatic, hematologic (thrombocytopenia), or CNS (disorientation). Isolation of S. aureus is NOT required.

S. aureus is rarely recovered from blood in TSS because the disease is toxin-mediated, not bacteremic. The multi-system involvement distinguishes TSS from simple cellulitis or bacteremia. MCCQE1 commonly tests the diagnostic criteria.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for streptococcal TSS?

**A:** SBP <90 mmHg AND isolation of GAS from a normally sterile site (blood, pleural, tissue biopsy, or surgical wound) PLUS ≥2 of: coagulopathy, liver involvement, ARDS, soft tissue necrosis (necrotizing fasciitis, myositis, gangrene), renal impairment, or erythematous macular rash that may desquamate.

Unlike staphylococcal TSS, streptococcal TSS requires bacteriologic confirmation from a sterile site. Necrotizing fasciitis is a hallmark complication. Early recognition is life-saving.

**Q:** What is the treatment for streptococcal TSS?

**A:** Supportive care, fluid resuscitation, surgical debridement of infected tissue, IV penicillin + clindamycin + IVIG.

Clindamycin is added because it inhibits protein (toxin) synthesis at the ribosomal level, suppressing superantigen production even when organisms are not actively dividing. IVIG neutralizes circulating toxins. These are Canadian/international guidelines.

**Q:** What is the treatment for staphylococcal TSS caused by MRSA versus methicillin-susceptible S. aureus (MSSA)?

**A:** MSSA: clindamycin + cloxacillin IV. MRSA: clindamycin + vancomycin, for 10–14 days.

Clindamycin is included in both regimens to inhibit toxin synthesis. Cloxacillin is the preferred anti-staphylococcal beta-lactam for MSSA. Vancomycin replaces cloxacillin for MRSA coverage.

**Q:** What organism causes cat scratch disease, and how is it transmitted?

**A:** Bartonella henselae, an intracellular gram-negative bacterium, transmitted via cat scratch or bite.

Cats (especially kittens) are the reservoir and are usually asymptomatic carriers. The bacteria are transmitted via cat flea feces inoculated through a scratch. Knowledge of the causative organism is frequently tested.

**Q:** What is the clinical presentation of cat scratch disease?

**A:** Skin lesion within 3–10 days of inoculation, followed by fever, malaise, and tender regional lymphadenopathy 14–21 days later. Usually self-limited. Dissemination can cause FUO, hepatosplenomegaly, retinitis, encephalopathy, infective endocarditis, or uveitis.

The temporal progression (skin lesion → lymphadenopathy weeks later) is classic. In HIV-positive patients, Bartonella causes bacillary angiomatosis (violaceous nodular skin lesions + bone involvement). This distinction is high-yield.

**Q:** What is bacillary angiomatosis and who is at risk?

**A:** Bacillary angiomatosis is a disseminated Bartonella henselae infection seen in patients with advanced HIV, presenting as violaceous (purple) nodular skin lesions with underlying bone involvement.

In immunocompetent hosts, Bartonella causes cat scratch disease. In severely immunocompromised patients (advanced HIV), dissemination leads to bacillary angiomatosis. The violaceous nodules can be mistaken for Kaposi sarcoma.

**Q:** How is cat scratch disease treated?

**A:** Most lymphadenitis cases are self-limited. Mild-to-moderate: azithromycin. Painful suppurative nodes: needle aspiration. Disseminated disease (endocarditis, neuroretinitis, hepatosplenic): doxycycline or azithromycin + rifampin.

Azithromycin shortens the duration of lymphadenopathy. For serious disseminated disease, combination therapy with rifampin is needed due to poor outcomes with monotherapy.

**Q:** What organism causes Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever (RMSF) and what is the vector in North America?

**A:** Rickettsia rickettsii, an obligate intracellular gram-negative bacterium. Vector in North America: Dermacentor ticks. In Mexico/Central America: Rhipicephalus ticks. Reservoir hosts are rodents and dogs.

RMSF causes endothelial inflammation of small blood vessels leading to widespread vasculitis. Despite the name, RMSF is most common in southeastern United States. Knowledge of the vector and organism is core MCCQE1 content.

**Q:** Describe the characteristic rash of Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever.

**A:** Macular rash appearing on days 2–4 of fever. Begins on the wrists and ankles, spreads centrally to arms, legs, trunk, palms, and soles. Occasionally 'spotless' (in ~10% of patients).

The centripetal spread (periphery → trunk) starting on wrists/ankles is distinctive. Involvement of palms and soles also occurs in secondary syphilis and hand-foot-mouth disease. The 10% spotless variant is a diagnostic challenge.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever?

**A:** Empiric doxycycline, typically 5–7 days (treat for 3 days after defervescence).

Doxycycline is the drug of choice even in children due to the severity of RMSF. Delay in treatment significantly increases mortality. Diagnosis is confirmed by skin biopsy and indirect fluorescent antibody serology, but treatment should not await confirmation.

**Q:** How is RMSF diagnosed?

**A:** Skin biopsy and serology (indirect fluorescent antibody test). Treatment should be started empirically and not delayed for results.

The indirect fluorescent antibody test is the gold standard serologic test. Skin biopsy with immunohistochemistry can provide a rapid diagnosis. Clinical diagnosis and early empiric treatment with doxycycline saves lives.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of West Nile Virus infection?

**A:** 80% asymptomatic. Symptomatic: West Nile fever (acute headache, back pain, myalgia, anorexia, maculopapular non-pruritic rash on chest/back/arms). Severe: encephalitis, meningoencephalitis, acute flaccid paralysis (especially >60 yr).

West Nile virus is transmitted by mosquitoes that have fed on infected birds (crows, blue jays). Most cases are mild. Severe neuroinvasive disease is more common in elderly and immunocompromised patients. Case fatality in severe cases is ~10%.

**Q:** What is the best diagnostic test for West Nile Virus infection?

**A:** IgM antibody in serum or CSF. Note: cross-reactivity with yellow fever and Japanese encephalitis vaccines, dengue, and St. Louis virus. IgM can persist >6 months and may not reflect current illness. PCR from CSF, blood, or tissue has low sensitivity due to transient viremia.

Serology (IgM detection) is the preferred test. CSF PCR has low sensitivity because viremia is brief. The cross-reactivity with other flaviviruses and vaccines is an important limitation to know for MCCQE1.

**Q:** How is West Nile Virus infection treated and prevented?

**A:** Treatment: supportive only. Prevention: mosquito repellent (DEET, picaridin), drain stagnant water, community mosquito control programs.

There is no antiviral treatment for West Nile Virus. Climate change is expanding the geographic range and transmission season of WNV in Canada. Prevention focuses on mosquito control and personal protection.

**Q:** What organism causes syphilis and how is it transmitted?

**A:** Treponema pallidum subspecies pallidum, a thick motile spirochete. Transmitted sexually, vertically (mother to fetus), or parenterally (rare).

T. pallidum cannot survive long outside the human host and cannot be cultured in vitro. Historically detected by dark-field microscopy. Other subspecies cause bejel (T.p. endemicum) and yaws (T.p. pertenue).

**Q:** Describe the stages of syphilis and their key features.

**A:** 1° (3–90 d): painless chancre at inoculation site + regional lymphadenopathy; lasts 3–6 wk. 2° (2–8 wk after chancre): maculopapular non-pruritic rash including palms/soles, generalized lymphadenopathy, fever, malaise, condylomata lata. 3. Latent: asymptomatic; early (<1 yr) vs. late/unknown (>1 yr). 4° Tertiary (1–30 yr): gummas, aortic aneurysm, aortic insufficiency. 5. Congenital. 6. Neurosyphilis.

25% of primary syphilis patients progress to secondary without treatment. Secondary syphilis is highly infectious. Condylomata lata (wart-like, moist lesions on genitals/mouth) differ from HPV condylomata acuminata. The full stage progression is high-yield for MCCQE1.

**Q:** What is the Argyll Robertson pupil and in what condition is it classically seen?

**A:** The Argyll Robertson pupil accommodates but does not react to light ('prostitute's pupil'). Classically seen in neurosyphilis (tertiary/quaternary syphilis).

The light reflex is lost while the accommodation reflex is preserved. This occurs due to damage to the pretectal nucleus in the midbrain. It is considered pathognomonic for neurosyphilis.

**Q:** What are the features of congenital syphilis?

**A:** Early: rhinitis (snuffles), lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, pseudoparalysis (bone pain from osteitis), maculopapular rash on palms/soles. Late (>2 yr): saddle nose, saber shins, Clutton joints, Hutchinson's teeth, mulberry molars, rhagades, CN VIII deafness, interstitial keratitis.

Congenital syphilis can cause spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, malformations, and developmental delay. Most infected newborns are initially asymptomatic. Hutchinson's triad (interstitial keratitis, Hutchinson's teeth, CN VIII deafness) is a late manifestation.

**Q:** What is the Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction?

**A:** A febrile reaction occurring within 10–24 hours of starting penicillin treatment for 2° or 3° syphilis, caused by lysis of organisms releasing pyrogens.

The Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction is self-limited and consists of fever, rigors, and worsening of skin lesions. It is most common in secondary syphilis. It is not an allergic reaction to penicillin and is managed with antipyretics and supportive care.

**Q:** How is syphilis screened and confirmed?

**A:** Screening: non-treponemal tests (RPR, VDRL) or treponemal tests (EIA, CMIA, CLIA). Confirmatory: treponemal tests (TPPA, FTA-ABS, MHA-TP, TPI). In Canada, testing is ordered through Public Health labs.

Non-treponemal tests (RPR, VDRL) reflect disease activity and can be used to monitor treatment response (titers fall with treatment). Treponemal tests remain positive for life and confirm diagnosis but do not indicate disease activity.

**Q:** When should lumbar puncture be performed in syphilis?

**A:** LP for neurosyphilis evaluation if: seropositive + symptoms of neurosyphilis, treatment failure/tertiary symptoms, or HIV+ with late latent/unknown duration syphilis.

Neurosyphilis can occur at any stage of syphilis. CSF findings include elevated lymphocytes and protein, and positive VDRL in CSF (highly specific but insensitive). Early diagnosis and treatment prevent permanent neurological damage.

**Q:** What is the treatment for primary, secondary, and early latent syphilis?

**A:** Benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM × 1 dose. If truly allergic to penicillin: doxycycline 100 mg PO BID × 14 days. IM ceftriaxone 1 g daily is another alternative.

Single-dose IM benzathine penicillin remains the standard of care. Doxycycline is the key second-line agent. Pregnant patients allergic to penicillin should undergo oral desensitization (penicillin remains preferred in pregnancy).

**Q:** What is the treatment for tertiary/late latent syphilis and neurosyphilis?

**A:** Late latent/tertiary: benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM weekly × 3 doses. If penicillin allergic: doxycycline 100 mg PO BID × 28 days. Neurosyphilis: aqueous penicillin G 16–24 million units/day IV × 14 days + single dose benzathine penicillin.

The extended treatment duration for late latent syphilis reflects slower organism replication. Neurosyphilis requires IV penicillin to achieve adequate CNS levels. The distinction in treatment between early and late disease is high-yield for MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the outcomes of Mycobacterium tuberculosis inhalation?

**A:** 1. Immediate clearance. 2. Latent TB infection (asymptomatic, immune-contained) — 90% of infected. 3. Primary TB (symptomatic active disease) — 5%. 4. Secondary TB (reactivation of latent TB) — 5–10% of those with latent TB, usually within 1–2 yr of initial infection.

Latent TB is the most common outcome. Reactivation risk is highest in the first 1–2 years and is greatly increased by immunosuppression (especially HIV), TNF-α inhibitors, diabetes, malnutrition, and extremes of age.

**Q:** What populations in Canada are at highest risk of tuberculosis?

**A:** Canadian-born Indigenous peoples (especially Inuit — TB incidence 25× higher than Canadian-born non-Indigenous people), immigrants from high-prevalence countries (Asia, Latin America, Sub-Saharan Africa, Eastern Europe), people experiencing homelessness, those with low SES, IDU, and people in crowded living conditions.

TB disparities in Indigenous Canadian communities, especially Inuit in Nunavut, are a major public health concern and are specifically highlighted in Canadian guidelines. This social determinants context is important for MCCQE1.

**Q:** What host factors increase the risk of TB reactivation?

**A:** HIV infection (highest risk), TNF-α inhibitors, glucocorticoids, silicosis, CKD requiring dialysis, diabetes, malignancy/chemotherapy, substance use (drugs, alcohol, smoking), and extremes of age.

HIV is the single greatest risk factor for TB reactivation. TNF-α inhibitors (used in RA, IBD, etc.) significantly increase TB risk — patients must be screened before initiating therapy. These host factors should be screened in any TB workup.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of pulmonary tuberculosis?

**A:** Chronic productive cough ± hemoptysis, fever, night sweats, weight loss, chest pain, anorexia. CXR: apical-posterior infiltrates, cavitation, lung volume loss. Non-resolving pneumonia despite standard antimicrobials is a key clinical clue.

Upper lobe predominance reflects the high oxygen tension favoring M. tuberculosis growth. Cavitation indicates extensive tissue destruction and high bacterial burden. Failure to respond to standard antibiotics is a red flag prompting TB workup.

**Q:** What is miliary tuberculosis and what is its chest X-ray appearance?

**A:** Miliary TB is widely disseminated hematogenous TB affecting lungs, abdominal organs, bone marrow, and CNS. CXR shows multiple small 1–5 mm millet seed-like lesions throughout the lungs.

The term 'miliary' derives from the resemblance to millet seeds. Miliary TB occurs when TB bacilli disseminate via the bloodstream, most commonly in immunocompromised hosts or after primary infection. BCG vaccine is ~80% effective against miliary TB in children.

**Q:** What are common sites of extrapulmonary tuberculosis?

**A:** Lymphadenitis, pleurisy, pericarditis, hepatitis, peritonitis, meningitis, osteomyelitis (vertebral = Pott disease), adrenal (Addison disease), renal, and ovarian.

Pott disease (vertebral TB) is the most common form of skeletal TB and can cause spinal cord compression. Adrenal TB causing Addison disease is a classic teaching point. Extrapulmonary TB is more common in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** What is the Ghon complex?

**A:** A parenchymal granuloma (primary focus) combined with a calcified hilar lymph node on the same side, indicating previous primary tuberculosis infection.

The Ghon complex is seen on chest X-ray as a calcified peripheral nodule (Ghon focus) with ipsilateral hilar lymphadenopathy. It indicates prior healed primary TB. Recognition is important for distinguishing prior infection from active disease.

**Q:** Compare TST (tuberculin skin test) and IGRA (interferon-gamma release assay) for TB screening.

**A:** Both diagnose prior TB infection. IGRA has fewer false positives (does not detect BCG vaccine antigens or most NTM). TST preferred for serial testing (e.g., healthcare workers). IGRA preferred if BCG given after age 1 yr, vaccinated >1 time, or unable to return for reading.

False-positive TST results from BCG vaccination are a significant limitation in countries where BCG is routinely used. IGRA (QuantiFERON-TB, T-SPOT.TB) uses specific M. tuberculosis antigens absent from BCG strains. Neither test distinguishes latent from active TB.

**Q:** What are the causes of false-positive and false-negative TST (tuberculin skin test)?

**A:** False-positive: BCG vaccine after age 1 year, NTM infection, booster effect. False-negative: poor technique, anergy (immunosuppression, HIV), recent infection (<10 weeks), remotely distant prior infection.

Anergy is particularly common in severe immunosuppression (HIV with low CD4). The booster effect occurs when a remote positive TST reverts to negative and then re-becomes positive on repeat testing — this can be mistaken for new conversion.

**Q:** What is the standard treatment for active pulmonary tuberculosis?

**A:** Initiation phase: INH + rifampin + pyrazinamide + ethambutol × 2 months. Continuation phase: INH + rifampin × 4 months. Total: 6 months. Extend continuation to 7 months if >65 yr, pregnant, or high risk of hepatotoxicity. (RIPE regimen)

The four-drug RIPE regimen covers the risk of undetected primary resistance. Pyrazinamide has unique bactericidal activity in acidic environments (within macrophages). Ethambutol provides additional coverage until susceptibility results are available.

**Q:** What are the regimens for treatment of latent TB infection (LTBI)?

**A:** Options: (1) Rifampin daily × 4 months. (2) INH + rifapentine weekly × 3 months (3HP). (3) INH + pyridoxine daily × 9 months (previous standard). (4) INH × 6 months. (5) INH + rifampin daily × 3 months. Pyridoxine (B6) added with INH to prevent neuropathy.

Multiple LTBI regimens are recommended by Canadian guidelines. Shorter rifampin-based regimens (4R, 3HP, 3HR) have better completion rates than the 9-month INH regimen. Active TB must be ruled out before starting LTBI therapy.

**Q:** When should pyridoxine (vitamin B6) be added to isoniazid treatment for TB?

**A:** Add pyridoxine with INH in cases of diabetes, renal failure, malnutrition, substance use disorders, seizure disorders, pregnancy/breastfeeding, or risk of peripheral neuropathy.

INH competitively inhibits pyridoxine metabolism, causing peripheral neuropathy through pyridoxine deficiency. High-risk groups have lower pyridoxine stores or higher demand. Routine supplementation is common practice in many guidelines.

**Q:** Define multidrug-resistant (MDR) TB and extensively drug-resistant (XDR) TB.

**A:** MDR-TB: resistance to INH + rifampin ± others. XDR-TB: resistance to INH + rifampin + fluoroquinolone + ≥1 injectable second-line agent. Suspect if prior treatment failed, exposure to known MDR index case, or immigration from high-risk area.

MDR- and XDR-TB are a global public health crisis. There were only 5 documented XDR-TB cases in Canada from 1997–2008, but global travel increases risk. Empiric treatment of suspected MDR/XDR-TB requires specialist referral. TB is a reportable disease to Public Health in Canada.

**Q:** What is the BCG vaccine and who is it recommended for in Canada?

**A:** BCG (Bacille Calmette-Guérin) is ~80% effective against pediatric miliary and meningeal TB. In Canada, recommended for infants in high-incidence communities (especially Indigenous communities) in whom there is no evidence of HIV infection or immunodeficiency.

BCG effectiveness in adults varies widely (0–80%). BCG is not routinely given to all Canadian infants but is targeted at high-risk communities. BCG causes false-positive TST, which is why IGRA is preferred in BCG-vaccinated populations.

**Q:** What are the components of the HIV virion?

**A:** Envelope: gp120 (attaches to host T-cell CD4) and gp41 (fusion and entry). Matrix: p17. Capsid: p24 (marker of acute infection). Core contains 2 copies of single-stranded RNA plus reverse transcriptase, integrase, and protease enzymes.

p24 antigen is detectable during the window period before antibodies develop, making 4th-generation (combined p24/antibody) tests more sensitive in early infection. gp120 binds CD4 and CCR5/CXCR4 co-receptors. This structure underpins the mechanism of each ART drug class.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of HIV entry into CD4+ T lymphocytes?

**A:** gp120 binds CD4 receptor and then CCR5 or CXCR4 co-receptor on the T-cell surface. gp41 mediates fusion of the viral envelope with the host cell membrane. RNA is then converted to dsDNA by reverse transcriptase, and dsDNA integrates into the host genome by integrase.

CCR5 is the co-receptor used by macrophage-tropic (R5) HIV, while CXCR4 is used by T-tropic (X4) HIV, which emerges later in disease. CCR5 antagonists (maraviroc) block this co-receptor, preventing viral entry.

**Q:** What is the significance of the CCR5 delta-32 mutation?

**A:** Homozygosity for the CCR5 Δ32 mutation confers near-complete resistance to HIV infection. Heterozygosity slows HIV disease progression.

Individuals homozygous for CCR5 Δ32 lack functional CCR5 on their T cells, preventing HIV entry. This is the genetic basis for the 'Berlin patient' (Timothy Ray Brown), who was functionally cured of HIV after a bone marrow transplant from a CCR5 Δ32 donor. It supports CCR5 antagonists as ART targets.

**Q:** What are the transmission probabilities of HIV by route of exposure?

**A:** Rectal (receptive anal sex): 1 in 20 to 1 in 300. Vaginal sex: 1 in 200 to 1 in 2000 (female receiving semen). Male receiving: 1 in 700 to 1 in 3000. Oral (receptive): 1 in 2500. Perinatal (intrapartum): 1 in 5 to 1 in 10. Breastmilk: 1 in 5 to 1 in 10. Blood products: 95 in 100.

Rectal receptive anal intercourse has the highest per-act risk due to the thin rectal epithelium. Blood products (before universal screening) had very high transmission rates. Understanding relative risks informs counseling and risk stratification.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of acute retroviral syndrome (primary HIV infection)?

**A:** 40–90% experience an acute non-specific illness 2–6 weeks post-exposure lasting 10–15 days: fever, pharyngitis, lymphadenopathy, rash, arthralgias, myalgias, headache, GI symptoms, oral ulcers, weight loss. Hematologic: lymphopenia, thrombocytopenia. 10–20% develop aseptic meningitis or CN palsies.

Acute retroviral syndrome is the period of highest viral load and transmission risk. HIV RNA and/or p24 may be detected in CSF. The presentation mimics infectious mononucleosis. Testing with 4th-generation assays (p24/antibody) is essential as antibody tests may be negative.

**Q:** What is the natural history of CD4 count decline in untreated HIV infection?

**A:** Normal adult CD4 count: 500–1100 cells/mm³. CD4 drops ~60–100 cells/mm³ per year. By 10 years untreated: 50% have AIDS, 30% milder symptoms, <20% asymptomatic.

The rate of CD4 decline is variable and influenced by viral load, genetic factors, and co-infections. CD4 count is the primary marker of immune function in HIV. ART can reverse CD4 decline and restore immune function.

**Q:** What is the case definition of AIDS?

**A:** HIV-positive AND one or more: opportunistic infections (PJP, esophageal candidiasis, CMV, MAC, TB, toxoplasmosis), malignancy (Kaposi's sarcoma, invasive cervical cancer), wasting syndrome, OR CD4 <200 cells/mm³ (or <15%).

The CD4 <200 threshold is largely historical because ART can reverse the decline. Clinically, recognizing AIDS-defining conditions prompts HIV testing and workup. PJP is the most common AIDS-defining illness in Canada.

**Q:** What opportunistic infections occur at different CD4 count thresholds in HIV?

**A:** <500: constitutional symptoms, seborrheic dermatitis, HSV, VZV, oral hairy leukoplakia, candidiasis, recurrent bacterial pneumonia, TB, lymphoma, HPV malignancies. <200: PJP, Kaposi sarcoma, oral thrush, Cryptococcus, Coccidioides, Histoplasma. <100: PML (JC virus), CNS toxoplasmosis. <50: CMV (retinitis, colitis), MAC, bacillary angiomatosis, primary CNS lymphoma.

Memorizing the CD4 thresholds for specific opportunistic infections is essential for MCCQE1. PJP occurs at <200, MAC and CMV occur at <50. Each OI has specific prophylaxis and treatment regimens.

**Q:** What are the prophylaxis regimens for opportunistic infections in HIV?

**A:** PJP (CD4 <200): TMP/SMX 1SS or DS once daily. Toxoplasma (IgG+ and CD4 <100): TMP/SMX 1DS daily. TB (PPD >5 mm or contact): INH + pyridoxine × 9 months. MAC (CD4 <50): No prophylaxis if started on ART (prophylaxis can be discontinued if CD4 rises above threshold for ≥6 months).

TMP/SMX covers both PJP and toxoplasmosis, making it highly efficient prophylaxis. MAC prophylaxis was previously azithromycin but is no longer recommended if ART is started promptly. Prophylaxis can be discontinued once immune reconstitution occurs on ART.

**Q:** What is the window period for HIV antibody testing and what are the diagnostic tests used?

**A:** Antibody window: ~3 weeks median, virtually all by 3 months. Screening: 3rd-gen ELISA (sensitivity ≥99.5%) or 4th-gen combined p24 antigen/antibody test (sensitivity/specificity approaches 100% for chronic, better for early infection). Confirmatory: Western blot (antibodies to ≥2 HIV protein bands: p24, gp41, gp120/160; specificity >99.99%).

4th-generation tests detect p24 antigen before antibodies develop, reducing the window period to ~2 weeks. All positive screening tests require Western blot confirmation. Rapid (point-of-care) tests have higher false-positive rates and also need confirmation.

**Q:** Why do all infants born to HIV-positive mothers test ELISA-positive for HIV, and how is HIV diagnosed in these infants?

**A:** All infants inherit maternal IgG antibodies (including anti-HIV) that persist for up to 18 months, making ELISA unreliable. Definitive early diagnosis in infants is made by HIV RNA or DNA PCR detection in plasma.

Maternal antibody transfer is the reason standard antibody tests cannot be used in neonates exposed to HIV. HIV RNA/DNA PCR can detect infection as early as the first few weeks of life. Early diagnosis allows prompt ART initiation to prevent disease progression.

**Q:** What are the goals and monitoring parameters for HIV management?

**A:** Goal: viral load <20 copies/mL (undetectable). Viral load should decrease 10-fold within 4–8 weeks and be undetectable by 6 months. Monitor: CD4 count (if non-stable/non-suppressed), HIV RNA viral load q3–6 months, baseline resistance testing, HLA-B*5701, CCR5 tropism testing, TB skin test, serologies (HAV, HBV, HCV, syphilis, toxo, CMV, VZV), annual fasting lipids/glucose.

Undetectable = Untransmittable (U=U): patients with viral load <200 copies/mL do not transmit HIV to sexual partners. CD4 monitoring can be reduced once consistently >500 with suppressed viral load. Annual screening (PAP, STI) and vaccines are part of comprehensive HIV care.

**Q:** What is HLA-B*5701 testing and why is it important in HIV treatment?

**A:** HLA-B*5701 is a genetic allele present in ~5–7% of White individuals (lower in other ethnicities). Individuals carrying this allele are at risk for abacavir hypersensitivity reactions. Screening must be done before starting any abacavir-containing regimen.

Abacavir hypersensitivity is a severe, potentially fatal systemic reaction that includes fever, rash, GI symptoms, and respiratory symptoms. It occurs almost exclusively in HLA-B*5701-positive patients. If rechallenge occurs after stopping abacavir, the reaction can be life-threatening.

**Q:** What is the first-line antiretroviral regimen for treatment-naive HIV-positive adults?

**A:** 2 NRTIs + 1 INSTI or boosted PI. Preferred regimens: (1) bictegravir/TAF/FTC; (2) DTG/ABC/3TC (if HLA-B*5701 negative); (3) DTG + (TAF or TDF)/(FTC or 3TC); (4) raltegravir + TAF/FTC; (5) DTG/3TC.

Integrase inhibitors (INSTIs) are preferred due to high potency, favorable side effect profile, and high barrier to resistance. Single-pill regimens (e.g., Biktarvy®) improve adherence. The goal is viral suppression <20 copies/mL.

**Q:** What are the drug classes of antiretroviral therapy (ART) and their mechanisms?

**A:** NRTIs: competitively inhibit/terminate reverse transcriptase (chain termination). NNRTIs: non-competitively inhibit reverse transcriptase. PIs: inhibit protease, preventing polyprotein cleavage and virion maturation. INSTIs: inhibit integration of viral DNA into host genome. Fusion inhibitors (enfuvirtide): block gp41, preventing viral fusion. CCR5 antagonists (maraviroc): block CCR5 co-receptor.

Each drug class targets a different step in the HIV life cycle. Combination therapy with at least 3 drugs from ≥2 classes is the standard to prevent resistance. Single-pill regimens combining multiple classes have revolutionized HIV treatment.

**Q:** What are key adverse effects of nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs)?

**A:** Class effect: lactic acidosis (mitochondrial toxicity), lipodystrophy, rash, nausea/vomiting/diarrhea. Drug-specific: AZT → bone marrow suppression, myopathy; ddI/d4T → peripheral neuropathy, pancreatitis; ABC → drug-induced hypersensitivity (HLA-B*5701). Thymidine analogues (AZT, d4T) → lipoatrophy.

Lactic acidosis from mitochondrial toxicity is a rare but potentially fatal class effect of NRTIs. Lipodystrophy includes both lipoatrophy (fat loss from face/extremities with thymidine analogues) and lipohypertrophy (fat accumulation centrally). Older NRTIs (AZT, d4T) have worse profiles than newer agents (TAF, TDF).

**Q:** What are key adverse effects of non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NNRTIs)?

**A:** Rash (including Stevens-Johnson syndrome), CNS effects (dizziness, insomnia, somnolence, abnormal dreams — especially efavirenz), hepatotoxicity (nevirapine — avoid in women with CD4 >250 or men with CD4 >400), CYP3A4 interactions.

Efavirenz is notorious for CNS side effects (vivid dreams, psychiatric symptoms) and is taken at bedtime to minimize impact. Nevirapine hepatotoxicity risk is higher at higher baseline CD4 counts, particularly in women. CYP3A4 interactions affect many co-medications.

**Q:** What are key adverse effects of protease inhibitors (PIs)?

**A:** Lipodystrophy, metabolic syndrome, nausea/vomiting/diarrhea, nephrolithiasis (indinavir), rash (amprenavir), hyperbilirubinemia (atazanavir, indinavir), hyperlipidemia, CYP3A4 interactions. Boosting with ritonavir or cobicistat improves pharmacokinetics.

PIs are pharmacologically 'boosted' with low-dose ritonavir (CYP3A4 inhibitor) to increase drug levels and reduce dosing frequency. Metabolic complications (increased LDL, triglycerides, insulin resistance, increased CVD risk) are class effects. Atazanavir causes a benign unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

**Q:** What is post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) for HIV?

**A:** PEP is a 2- or 3-drug ART regimen initiated immediately (<72 hours) after occupational (needle-stick) or non-occupational (consensual sex, sexual assault) HIV exposure, continued for 4 weeks.

PEP efficacy decreases with delay; it should be started as soon as possible and within 72 hours. Pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) is taken before exposure. ART is associated with 96% reduction in HIV transmission risk to sexual partners.

**Q:** What is pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) for HIV and what are the dosing strategies?

**A:** PrEP options: (1) Daily oral TDF/FTC or TAF/FTC. (2) 'On-demand' (2-1-1) PrEP: double dose 12–24 h before exposure, then single doses 24 and 48 h after. (3) Injectable cabotegravir every 2 months. Indicated for high-risk HIV-uninfected individuals (MSM, serodiscordant couples, PWID).

PrEP reduces HIV acquisition by ~49–86% compared to placebo in clinical trials. The 2-1-1 strategy is effective for event-driven use in MSM. Injectable cabotegravir (long-acting) eliminates daily pill burden. PrEP does not protect against other STIs.

**Q:** How can vertical (mother-to-child) HIV transmission be prevented?

**A:** Initiate maternal ART before or as early as possible in pregnancy. If maternal viral load is undetectable prior to delivery, vertical transmission risk is reduced to <1%. Universal precautions, avoidance of breastfeeding in resource-rich settings.

Without intervention, vertical transmission risk is ~25–40% (intrapartum + breastfeeding). With optimal ART and obstetric management, it approaches <1%. Elective cesarean section may be considered if viral load is detectable near delivery.

**Q:** What is the definition of HIV treatment failure?

**A:** Defined primarily by viral load persistently >200 copies/mL. Confirm it is not a transient 'blip.' Assess adherence, drug interactions, and perform resistance testing. Construct a new combination regimen.

A 'blip' is a transient low-level viremia (<200 copies/mL) in a patient with otherwise suppressed viral load — not treatment failure. True treatment failure requires assessment of adherence (the most common cause), pharmacokinetics, and genotypic resistance testing.

**Q:** What vaccines are recommended for HIV-positive patients?

**A:** Annual influenza vaccine, 23-valent pneumococcal vaccine every 5 years, HBV vaccine (if not immune), HAV vaccine (if seronegative), HPV vaccine.

HIV-positive patients are at increased risk for vaccine-preventable diseases. Live vaccines (e.g., live-attenuated influenza) are generally avoided in severely immunocompromised patients (CD4 <200). Pneumococcal vaccination reduces risk of recurrent pneumonia, a common HIV complication.

**Q:** What are the three types of HIV testing in Canada?

**A:** 1. Nominal (name-based): patient identity known, test ordered by name, positive results reported to Public Health, documented in health record. 2. Non-nominal: test ordered by code/initials. 3. Anonymous: available at specialized clinics, unique code known only to patient, not in health record — but patient ID needed to access ART.

HIV testing is regulated provincially in Canada. Anonymous testing protects patient privacy but requires identification when accessing treatment. All nominal and non-nominal positive HIV tests must be reported to Public Health under communicable disease reporting requirements.

**Q:** What are the HIV non-disclosure laws in Ontario?

**A:** Since 2017, non-disclosure of HIV status to a sexual partner is no longer a criminal offense in Ontario if: (1) on ART with viral load <200 copies/mL for ≥6 months, OR (2) viral load 200–1500 copies/mL AND a condom is used properly and does not break.

Prior to 2017, HIV non-disclosure was criminalized regardless of viral load or condom use. The updated law reflects the Undetectable = Untransmittable (U=U) evidence. Healthcare providers should counsel HIV-positive patients on their legal obligations.

**Q:** What is subcutaneous sporotrichosis and how does it present and spread?

**A:** Caused by subcutaneous inoculation of Sporothrix schenckii (a soil fungus), classically in gardeners injured by a rose thorn or splinter. Presents as subcutaneous nodule at entry site that may ulcerate. Fungi migrate up lymphatic vessels creating 'nodular lymphangitis'.

Sporotrichosis is the classic cause of nodular lymphangitis (ascending lymphatic spread). Other causes include Nocardia, atypical mycobacteria, Leishmania, and Francisella (tularemia). Treatment is with oral azole antifungals; IV amphotericin B for severe/disseminated infection.

**Q:** A gardener presents with a nodule at a rose thorn puncture site and additional nodules tracking up the arm toward the axilla. What is the diagnosis, organism, and treatment?

**A:** Sporotrichosis (lymphocutaneous form) caused by Sporothrix schenckii. Treatment: oral azole antifungal (itraconazole is first-line). IV amphotericin B for severe or disseminated disease.

This is the classic presentation of lymphocutaneous sporotrichosis — nodular lymphangitis following a traumatic inoculation in a gardener. Itraconazole for 3–6 months is the standard treatment. Saturated solution of potassium iodide (SSKI) is an older alternative.

**Q:** A patient with HIV and CD4 count of 40 cells/mm³ develops painful periorbital visual changes and CMV retinitis is diagnosed. What else should be screened for at this CD4 level?

**A:** At CD4 <50: screen for CMV (retinitis, colitis, cholangiopathy, CNS), MAC (Mycobacterium avium complex), bacillary angiomatosis (disseminated Bartonella), and primary CNS lymphoma.

CD4 <50 represents profound immunosuppression and is associated with the most severe opportunistic infections. CMV retinitis can cause blindness if untreated. MAC causes fevers, night sweats, weight loss, and cytopenias. Recognizing CD4-threshold-specific OIs guides prophylaxis and treatment decisions.

**Q:** Why is clindamycin added to antibiotic regimens in toxic shock syndrome (TSS)?

**A:** Clindamycin inhibits bacterial protein synthesis at the ribosomal level (50S subunit), thereby suppressing superantigen toxin production even when organisms are not actively dividing (stationary phase). Beta-lactams are only effective against actively dividing bacteria.

In TSS, the clinical syndrome is primarily toxin-mediated. Superantigen toxins cause massive T-cell activation and cytokine release. Clindamycin's ability to suppress toxin synthesis at any growth phase makes it essential. This is why it is combined with penicillin/cloxacillin/vancomycin rather than used alone.

**Q:** How does West Nile Virus epidemiology relate to climate change in Canada?

**A:** Rising temperatures increase mosquito survival, geographical range, biting rates, and viral replication within mosquitoes, shorten reproduction rates, and lengthen transmission seasons. Climate change also alters bird migration patterns, changing virus spread across Canada.

WNV has been detected throughout the US and much of southern Canada (Quebec, Ontario, Manitoba, Saskatchewan, Alberta). Climate-driven northward expansion of mosquito populations increases WNV risk in previously low-risk Canadian regions. This has public health policy implications.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of amphotericin B?

**A:** Amphotericin B is a polyene antimicrobial that inserts into the fungal cytoplasmic membrane, causing altered membrane permeability and cell death.

Amphotericin B binds to ergosterol in the fungal cell membrane, creating pores that disrupt ion gradients and lead to cell death. The liposomal formulation reduces nephrotoxicity while maintaining efficacy and is preferred in many clinical settings.

**Q:** What are the major adverse effects of amphotericin B?

**A:** Nephrotoxicity, hypo/hyperkalemia, infusion reactions (chills, fevers, headache), and peripheral phlebitis.

Nephrotoxicity is the most clinically significant toxicity of conventional amphotericin B; liposomal formulations significantly reduce this risk. Pre-hydration with normal saline can reduce nephrotoxicity. Infusion-related reactions (rigors, fever) occur with IV administration and can be pre-treated with diphenhydramine and acetaminophen.

**Q:** Which infections is amphotericin B used to treat?

**A:** Endemic mycoses (histoplasmosis, blastomycosis, coccidioidomycosis), pulmonary aspergillosis, and CNS cryptococcosis.

Amphotericin B has the broadest antifungal spectrum and remains the drug of choice or alternative for life-threatening systemic fungal infections. Liposomal amphotericin B is particularly used for CNS infections due to better CNS penetration.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of azole antifungals?

**A:** Azoles inhibit ergosterol synthesis by blocking the 14-alpha-demethylase enzyme (CYP51), thereby altering fungal cell membrane permeability.

By inhibiting fungal ergosterol synthesis (the equivalent of mammalian cholesterol), azoles disrupt membrane integrity and function. This is a fungistatic mechanism (not fungicidal like amphotericin B). The azole class includes fluconazole, itraconazole, voriconazole, posaconazole, and isavuconazole.

**Q:** A patient on voriconazole develops visual disturbances. What should the clinician know about this side effect?

**A:** Visual disturbances occur in approximately 30% of patients on voriconazole. This is a common, transient side effect that typically resolves with dose adjustment or discontinuation.

Voriconazole causes transient visual disturbances (blurred vision, photophobia, colour perception changes) in ~30% of patients, most commonly shortly after dosing. Long-term use, especially in immunosuppressed patients, is also associated with cutaneous photosensitivity, cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma, periostitis, and prolonged QT. Hepatotoxicity monitoring is essential.

**Q:** What is the drug of choice for invasive aspergillosis based on landmark trial evidence?

**A:** Voriconazole is the drug of choice for primary therapy of invasive aspergillosis, demonstrating superior outcomes compared to amphotericin B (52.8% vs 31.6% success rate at 12 weeks).

The Herbrecht et al. 2002 NEJM trial showed voriconazole was more clinically effective with fewer side effects than amphotericin B for invasive aspergillosis. Survival at 12 weeks was also superior (70.8% vs 57.9%). This is now standard of care per IDSA guidelines.

**Q:** Which azole antifungal is used for esophageal candidiasis in HIV patients (off-label) and mucormycosis?

**A:** Isavuconazole is used off-label for esophageal candidiasis in HIV patients and is indicated for aspergillosis and mucormycosis.

Isavuconazole (available as isavuconazonium sulfate prodrug) offers coverage for aspergillosis, mucormycosis, and esophageal candidiasis in HIV patients (off-label). It has a favorable safety profile compared to voriconazole, notably with fewer visual side effects and no QTc prolongation (actually shortens QTc — contraindicated in familial short QT syndrome).

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of echinocandins (caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin)?

**A:** Echinocandins inhibit 1,3-beta-D-glucan synthesis, disrupting the fungal cell wall (which mammals lack, reducing host toxicity).

Echinocandins are fungicidal against Candida and fungistatic against Aspergillus. They are used for azole-resistant candidemia, refractory aspergillosis, and empiric antifungal therapy in febrile neutropenic patients. Their cell wall target (absent in mammals) contributes to their favorable safety profile.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of terbinafine?

**A:** Terbinafine (an allylamine) inhibits the enzyme squalene epoxidase, blocking ergosterol synthesis in fungal cells.

Unlike azoles (which inhibit 14-alpha-demethylase), terbinafine inhibits squalene epoxidase at an earlier step of ergosterol synthesis. This leads to squalene accumulation (which is toxic to the fungus) and ergosterol deficiency. Terbinafine is primarily used for dermatophytoses and onychomycosis.
