**Q:** Which antifungal is contraindicated in pregnancy and patients with planned pregnancy, and also inhibits androgen/testosterone synthesis?

**A:** Ketoconazole (Nizoral) — it inhibits fungal ergosterol synthesis and also decreases androgen and testosterone synthesis, and is contraindicated in pregnancy.

Ketoconazole uniquely inhibits steroidogenesis in addition to ergosterol synthesis, making it teratogenic and causing potential endocrine disruption. Due to hepatotoxicity and drug interactions, systemic ketoconazole use has been largely replaced by other azoles; topical forms remain in use for seborrheic dermatitis and dermatomycoses.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of nystatin and why is it not given IV?

**A:** Nystatin (a polyene) inserts into fungal cell membranes causing altered membrane permeability and cell death — the same mechanism as amphotericin B. It is not given IV because it is highly toxic systemically.

Nystatin is not absorbed from the GI tract, making it useful for oral/mucocutaneous candidiasis when taken topically or orally. IV administration would cause severe systemic toxicity. It is primarily used for oropharyngeal, vaginal, and GI candidiasis.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of chloroquine in malaria?

**A:** Chloroquine inhibits parasite heme polymerase, preventing the detoxification of heme (ferriprotoporphyrin IX) and leading to accumulation of toxic heme metabolites in the parasite.

Plasmodium parasites digest hemoglobin, releasing toxic free heme. They normally polymerize heme into nontoxic hemozoin (malaria pigment). Chloroquine inhibits this polymerization. Resistance in P. falciparum and some P. vivax is widespread, limiting its utility in many endemic regions.

**Q:** Why is primaquine contraindicated in G6PD deficiency?

**A:** Primaquine causes oxidative stress in red blood cells; in G6PD-deficient individuals who cannot neutralize this oxidative stress, this leads to hemolytic anemia.

G6PD (glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase) is needed for maintaining reduced glutathione, which protects RBCs from oxidative damage. Primaquine generates reactive oxygen species in erythrocytes. Before prescribing primaquine for P. vivax or P. ovale (hypnozoite eradication), G6PD testing is required — this is standard Canadian practice.

**Q:** Which antimalarial drug targets the liver hypnozoite stage of P. vivax and P. ovale?

**A:** Primaquine — it is the only drug that eradicates the dormant liver stages (hypnozoites) of P. vivax and P. ovale, preventing relapse.

P. vivax and P. ovale form dormant liver hypnozoites that can reactivate months to years later. Chloroquine treats the blood-stage infection but not hypnozoites. Primaquine (or tafenoquine) is required for radical cure. Testing for G6PD deficiency is mandatory before use in Canada.

**Q:** What are the key features of 'Cinchonism' caused by quinine?

**A:** Cinchonism is a toxidrome caused by quinine characterized by tinnitus, vertigo, visual disturbances, nausea/vomiting, diarrhea, and headache/fever.

Cinchonism reflects quinine/quinidine toxicity. Quinine is used for chloroquine-resistant Plasmodium falciparum malaria. Other serious adverse effects include hypoglycemia, prolonged QT, and hemolytic uremic syndrome. It is contraindicated in patients with a history of blackwater fever or thrombocytopenic purpura due to quinine.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone) for malaria treatment?

**A:** Atovaquone inhibits mitochondrial electron transport; proguanil inhibits dihydrofolate reductase. Together they synergistically block parasite metabolism.

Atovaquone/proguanil (Malarone) is used for treatment and prophylaxis of P. falciparum malaria, including chloroquine-resistant strains. It must be taken with food (fatty meal) to maximize absorption. Contraindicated in severe renal impairment.

**Q:** Which antimalarial drug is available in Canada only via Health Canada's Special Access Program?

**A:** Artemisinin derivatives (artemether, artesunate) — they are not licensed or marketed in Canada but are available via the Special Access Program for treatment of all Plasmodium species including severe malaria (IV artesunate).

Artemisinins are the most potent and fast-acting antimalarials. They bind iron and generate free radicals that damage parasite proteins. IV artesunate is preferred over IV quinine for severe malaria in many international guidelines. In Canada, access requires the Special Access Program due to lack of domestic licensing.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action and key side effect of mefloquine for malaria prophylaxis?

**A:** Mefloquine's mechanism is not fully understood but it interferes with heme detoxification. Key side effects are neuropsychiatric: irritability, nightmares, psychoses, depression, and seizures.

Mefloquine (Lariam) is used for prophylaxis in chloroquine-resistant areas. It is contraindicated in patients with a history of seizures, psychosis, anxiety, depression, or other mental health diagnoses due to its neuropsychiatric side effects. The Black Box Warning cautions about potentially permanent neuropsychiatric effects.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of praziquantel and which parasites does it treat?

**A:** Praziquantel increases calcium permeability of helminth cell membranes, causing paralysis and detachment from tissues. It treats schistosomiasis, other flukes (trematodes), and tapeworms (cestodes).

Praziquantel is the drug of choice for schistosomiasis and most tapeworm infections. Increased calcium influx causes tegumental damage, paralysis, and immune-mediated parasite death. Contraindicated in ocular cysticercosis (can worsen inflammation) and with strong CYP450 inducers.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of albendazole and mebendazole?

**A:** Both inhibit microtubule formation (tubulin polymerization) and glucose uptake in susceptible parasites, causing parasite death.

Benzimidazoles (albendazole, mebendazole) bind to beta-tubulin and block microtubule polymerization, disrupting cell division and nutrient uptake. Albendazole has broader systemic absorption and is used for neurocysticercosis, echinococcosis, and hydatid disease. Both are contraindicated in pregnancy and in ocular/intraventricular cysticercosis.

**Q:** What is the treatment for strongyloidiasis and onchocerciasis?

**A:** Ivermectin — it interferes with the polarization of nerve and muscle cells in susceptible parasites, causing paralysis.

Ivermectin acts on glutamate-gated chloride channels (unique to invertebrates), increasing membrane permeability and causing hyperpolarization, paralysis, and death. It is first-line for strongyloidiasis, onchocerciasis (river blindness), and scabies. Contraindicated in pregnancy and hypersensitivity.

**Q:** What were the key results of the EPIC-HR trial (2022) regarding nirmatrelvir/ritonavir for COVID-19?

**A:** Nirmatrelvir/ritonavir (Paxlovid) reduced COVID-19-related hospitalization or death by 89.1% (relative risk reduction) compared to placebo in high-risk, non-hospitalized adults with COVID-19.

The EPIC-HR trial (NEJM 2022) enrolled 2246 participants with confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infection. The primary outcome (COVID-19-related hospitalization or death by day 28) was reduced by 6.32% absolute risk reduction (p<0.0001). Treatment was given orally every 12 hours for 5 days (10 doses total). This established nirmatrelvir/ritonavir as a key early treatment option.

**Q:** What did the RECOVERY trial demonstrate about dexamethasone in COVID-19?

**A:** Dexamethasone (6 mg daily for up to 10 days) reduced 28-day mortality in patients requiring invasive mechanical ventilation (RR 0.64) or supplemental oxygen (RR 0.82), but NOT in patients not requiring respiratory support (where it may have been harmful, RR 1.19).

The RECOVERY trial (NEJM 2021) enrolled 6425 hospitalized COVID-19 patients. Dexamethasone benefit was restricted to those with hypoxic respiratory failure. In patients without respiratory support, dexamethasone showed a trend toward harm. This nuanced finding is critical: steroids should not be used in mild, non-hypoxic COVID-19.

**Q:** What did the PINETREE trial show about remdesivir in COVID-19?

**A:** A 3-day IV course of remdesivir in non-hospitalized, high-risk COVID-19 patients reduced the risk of hospitalization or death by 87% compared to placebo (0.7% vs 5.3%).

The PINETREE trial (NEJM 2022) randomized 562 high-risk outpatients within 7 days of symptom onset to receive remdesivir (200 mg IV day 1, then 100 mg IV on days 2-3) or placebo. This established early outpatient IV remdesivir as an alternative to oral antivirals for high-risk COVID-19 patients.

**Q:** What did the PneumA trial establish about the duration of antibiotic therapy for ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP)?

**A:** 8 days of antibiotic therapy was as effective as 15 days for VAP (comparable mortality and recurrence rates), with the benefit of more antibiotic-free days.

The PneumA trial (JAMA 2003) randomized 401 VAP patients to 8 vs 15 days of antibiotics. No significant difference in mortality (18.8% vs 17.2%) or recurrent infections (28.9% vs 26.0%). Importantly, non-fermenting gram-negative organisms (like Pseudomonas) had higher recurrence with 8-day therapy — suggesting some patient-specific considerations. Current VAP guidelines recommend 7-8 days of treatment.

**Q:** What did the Gans et al. 2002 trial demonstrate about dexamethasone in bacterial meningitis?

**A:** Dexamethasone (10 mg IV every 6 hours for 4 days, given 15-20 minutes before or with the first antibiotic dose) significantly reduced unfavorable outcomes (RR 0.59) and mortality (RR 0.48) in adults with acute bacterial meningitis without increasing GI bleeding risk.

This landmark NEJM 2002 trial led to the standard practice of giving dexamethasone before or with the first dose of antibiotics in suspected bacterial meningitis. The benefit was most pronounced for S. pneumoniae meningitis. Dexamethasone reduces inflammation-mediated CNS injury during bacterial lysis.

**Q:** What did the POET trial demonstrate about oral antibiotic step-down therapy for infective endocarditis?

**A:** Switching from IV to oral antibiotics in stable, left-sided infective endocarditis (after minimum 10 days of IV therapy) was noninferior to continuing IV therapy (composite outcome 9.0% oral vs 12.1% IV).

The POET trial (NEJM 2019) established oral step-down as a safe option in carefully selected IE patients who are stable, have left-sided infection, and have appropriate sensitivities for oral agents. The composite outcome included all-cause mortality, embolic events, unplanned cardiac surgery, and bacteremia relapse. This simplifies management and allows earlier discharge.

**Q:** What did the STOPT trial establish about antibiotic duration for intraabdominal infection?

**A:** A fixed 4-day course of antibiotics was as effective as a variable course (up to 10 days) for complicated intraabdominal infection with adequate source control (21.8% vs 22.3% adverse outcomes, P=0.92).

The STOPT trial (NEJM 2015) demonstrated that once adequate source control is achieved (surgical drainage, etc.), prolonged antibiotic courses do not improve outcomes. This aligns with antibiotic stewardship principles. The experimental group received antibiotics for 4±1 days vs up to 10 days in controls.

**Q:** What did the iPrEx trial establish about HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP)?

**A:** Daily oral FTC-TDF (emtricitabine/tenofovir disoproxil fumarate) reduced HIV acquisition by 44% in HIV-seronegative men and transgender women who have sex with men, compared to placebo.

The iPrEx trial (NEJM 2010) enrolled 2499 participants over a median 1.2-year follow-up. This trial established the foundation for PrEP as a prevention strategy. Real-world effectiveness is significantly higher (~99%) with perfect adherence. In Canada, PrEP is widely available and recommended for high-risk individuals.

**Q:** What did the Cohen et al. 2011 trial demonstrate about early ART initiation in serodiscordant couples?

**A:** Early ART initiation (immediate vs delayed by CD4 decline) reduced HIV-1 transmission by 96% (hazard ratio 0.04) in serodiscordant couples, establishing Treatment as Prevention (TasP).

This landmark trial (NEJM 2011) enrolled 1763 serodiscordant couples. Of 28 linked transmissions, only 1 occurred in the early-therapy group. This established the concept of U=U (Undetectable = Untransmittable) and is foundational to current HIV treatment guidelines recommending immediate ART for all HIV-positive individuals.

**Q:** What did the CORTICUS trial find about hydrocortisone in septic shock?

**A:** Hydrocortisone did not improve survival in septic shock patients, regardless of whether they responded to corticotropin stimulation testing.

The CORTICUS trial (NEJM 2008) randomized 499 patients with septic shock to hydrocortisone 50 mg IV q6h for 5 days (with taper) vs placebo. No significant mortality benefit was found in either corticotropin-responders or non-responders. Current surviving sepsis guidelines recommend hydrocortisone only for patients with refractory septic shock not responding to vasopressors.

**Q:** What did the OVIVA trial establish about oral vs IV antibiotics for bone and joint infections?

**A:** Oral antibiotics were noninferior to IV antibiotics for complex orthopedic infections when used for 6 weeks (treatment failure difference -1.4%; 95% CI -5.6 to 2.9).

The OVIVA trial (NEJM 2019) randomized 1054 patients within 1 week post-surgery to 6 weeks of IV or oral antibiotics. No significant difference in serious adverse events. This supports early oral step-down therapy for osteomyelitis and prosthetic joint infections in appropriate patients, enabling earlier discharge and reduced IV-related complications.

**Q:** What did the DATIPO trial find about antibiotic duration for prosthetic joint infection?

**A:** 12 weeks of antibiotics was superior to 6 weeks for prosthetic joint infection — the 6-week group had significantly higher rates of persistent infection (18.1% vs 9.4%).

The DATIPO trial (NEJM 2021) randomized 410 patients with microbiologically confirmed prosthetic joint infection to 6 vs 12 weeks of antibiotics. Unlike native bone and joint infections (OVIVA), prosthetic joint infections require longer treatment duration (12 weeks is standard). Shorter duration led to unacceptably higher failure rates.

**Q:** What did the BENEFIT trial conclude about benznidazole for Chagas cardiomyopathy?

**A:** Benznidazole reduced serum Trypanosoma cruzi PCR levels (66.2% vs 33.5% conversion to negative at end of treatment) but did NOT improve cardiac outcomes in patients with Chagas cardiomyopathy.

The BENEFIT trial (NEJM 2015) enrolled 2854 patients with Chagas cardiomyopathy randomized to benznidazole vs placebo for up to 80 days. While trypanocidal therapy reduced parasitemia, PCR conversion did not correlate with clinical outcomes. This suggests that by the time cardiomyopathy develops, cardiac damage may be immune-mediated and not reversible by antiparasitic treatment.

**Q:** What is the significance of the RTS,S malaria vaccine trial combined with seasonal malaria chemoprevention (SMC)?

**A:** Combining the RTS,S vaccine with seasonal malaria chemoprevention (sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine + amodiaquine) was markedly superior to either intervention alone, resulting in ~70% further reduction in malaria deaths and hospitalizations and ~60% reduction in uncomplicated malaria compared to SMC alone.

This NEJM 2021 trial enrolled 6861 children aged 5-17 months in areas with highly seasonal malaria. The combination approach showed synergistic benefit over either RTS,S alone or SMC alone. This has implications for malaria prevention programs in sub-Saharan Africa and underscores the value of combined pharmacological and vaccine strategies.

**Q:** A patient with severe septic shock is not responding to vasopressors. According to trial evidence, what is the role of corticosteroids?

**A:** Hydrocortisone can be considered in patients with refractory septic shock not responding to adequate fluid resuscitation and vasopressors. However, the CORTICUS trial showed no mortality benefit from routine use — current guidelines reserve corticosteroids for vasopressor-refractory cases.

The CORTICUS trial (NEJM 2008) showed corticotropin stimulation testing is not needed to guide steroid use. The Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines recommend hydrocortisone (200 mg/day IV continuous infusion or 50 mg q6h) only in refractory septic shock. The ADRENAL and APROCCHSS trials (2018) provided additional evidence supporting corticosteroids in selected patients.

**Q:** Iodoquinol (Diodoquin) is used for which parasitic infections and what are its key contraindications?

**A:** Iodoquinol treats intestinal amebiasis (E. histolytica), Dientamoeba fragilis, Balantidium coli, and Blastocystis hominis. Contraindicated in: iodine or 8-hydroxy-quinoline hypersensitivity, hepatic damage, optic neuropathy, and pregnancy.

Iodoquinol acts as a contact amebicide in the intestinal lumen by an uncertain mechanism. It treats luminal (not invasive) amebiasis. For invasive or extraintestinal amebiasis, metronidazole is the drug of choice, often followed by a luminal agent like iodoquinol or paromomycin. Neurological toxicity (headache, seizures) can occur with excessive doses.

**Q:** What is diethylcarbamazine (DEC) used for and what is the Mazzotti reaction?

**A:** DEC treats Wuchereria bancrofti (lymphatic filariasis) and Loa loa. The Mazzotti reaction is a severe systemic inflammatory reaction that occurs when DEC is given to patients coinfected with Onchocerca volvulus, due to rapid killing of microfilariae releasing inflammatory mediators.

DEC immobilizes microfilariae and disrupts their surface membranes, enhancing killing by the host immune system. It is contraindicated in onchocerciasis due to the Mazzotti reaction (fever, urticaria, hypotension, and potentially fatal encephalitis from inflammatory destruction of dying microfilariae). Ivermectin is the drug of choice for onchocerciasis.

**Q:** What is posaconazole (Posanol) used for and which drug interactions are most clinically important?

**A:** Posaconazole is used for candidiasis, aspergillosis, and mucormycosis. Key interactions: contraindicated with cisapride, ergot alkaloids, sirolimus, and QT-prolonging agents (pimozide, quinidine); inhibits CYP3A4 — increases statin levels (atorvastatin, lovastatin, simvastatin).

Posaconazole is a broad-spectrum triazole with activity against mucormycosis (one of few antifungals with this coverage). It is a potent CYP3A4 inhibitor, leading to clinically significant drug interactions. The HMG-CoA reductase inhibitor interaction is important — concurrent statin use requires dose reduction or avoidance to prevent myopathy/rhabdomyolysis.

**Q:** What is the incubation period for malaria caused by Plasmodium falciparum, and what are its severe complications?

**A:** Incubation: 1–30 days. Severe P. falciparum complications: kidney failure, seizures, mental confusion, prostration, coma, death, respiratory failure.

P. falciparum is the most dangerous Plasmodium species and must be identified promptly. Diagnosis is by thick and thin blood smear x3, rapid diagnostic test, antigen detection, or PCR. Severe disease is treated with IV artesunate.

**Q:** A returned traveller from sub-Saharan Africa presents with fever, shaking chills, headache, anemia, and jaundice. What is the first-line diagnostic test and initial treatment for severe disease?

**A:** Diagnosis: thick and thin blood smear x3 (confirm with rapid diagnostic test, antigen detection, or PCR). Treatment of severe malaria: IV artesunate + Malarone®, doxycycline, or clindamycin.

Malaria must be excluded in any febrile returned traveller, especially from Africa. Blood smear is the gold standard. IV artesunate is preferred over quinine for severe falciparum malaria per current Canadian guidelines.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of dengue fever, and what analgesic is contraindicated?

**A:** Features: sudden fever, headache, retro-orbital pain, myalgias, arthralgias, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, and rarely hemorrhagic manifestations. NSAIDs and ASA are contraindicated due to antiplatelet properties; use acetaminophen.

Dengue is spread by day-biting Aedes mosquitoes in Southeast Asia, Caribbean, and tropical regions. Incubation is 3 days to 2 weeks. Diagnosis is by anti-dengue IgM or PCR. NSAIDs increase bleeding risk given thrombocytopenia.

**Q:** What is the incubation period of typhoid fever, what organism causes it, and what are the treatment options?

**A:** Incubation: 3–60 days. Causative organism: Salmonella enterica serotype Typhi or Paratyphi. Treatment: quinolone (e.g., ciprofloxacin), ceftriaxone, or macrolide.

Typhoid (enteric fever) presents with sustained fever 39–40°C, abdominal pain, headache, loss of appetite, cough, and constipation. Diagnosed by stool, urine, or blood culture. Most common on the Indian subcontinent.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of tick typhus, and what is the treatment?

**A:** Classic triad: fever, headache, spotted rash + eschar at site of tick bite. Treatment: doxycycline.

Tick typhus is caused by Rickettsia species and is common in the Mediterranean, South Africa, and India. Incubation is 1–2 weeks. Elevated liver enzymes and thrombocytopenia are also seen. Doxycycline is the treatment of choice for all Rickettsial infections.

**Q:** What are the four major etiologic categories of classical Fever of Unknown Origin (FUO), and what temperature threshold defines FUO?

**A:** Temperature ≥38.3°C/101°F on several occasions. Four categories: (1) Infectious (~30%), (2) Neoplastic (~20%), (3) Collagen vascular diseases (~30%), (4) Miscellaneous (~20%). Up to 30–50% remain undiagnosed.

Classical FUO requires fever >3 weeks, diagnosis uncertain after 3 outpatient visits or 3 days in hospital or 1 week of intensive investigation. The most common infectious cause is extrapulmonary TB; most common neoplastic cause is lymphoma.

**Q:** What are the four types of Fever of Unknown Origin (FUO), and how does the definition differ for each?

**A:** 1) Classical FUO: >3 weeks, uncertain after 3 outpatient visits or 3 days in hospital. 2) Nosocomial FUO: hospitalized patient, not present on admission, uncertain after 3 days of investigation. 3) Neutropenic FUO: ANC <500/mL, uncertain after 3 days of investigation. 4) HIV-associated FUO: HIV-positive, >4 weeks outpatient or >3 days hospitalized, uncertain after 3 days of investigation.

Each FUO category has distinct definitions and epidemiology. Nosocomial FUO commonly involves catheter infections, drugs, and emboli. Neutropenic FUO involves bacterial and fungal infections. HIV-associated FUO involves opportunistic infections.

**Q:** What are the most common infectious causes of classical FUO?

**A:** Extrapulmonary TB (most common single infectious cause), abscesses (subphrenic, liver, splenic, pancreatic, perinephric, diverticular, pelvic, psoas), osteomyelitis, culture-negative infective endocarditis, viral (CMV, EBV, HIV), bacterial (brucellosis, bartonellosis), fungal (histoplasmosis, cryptococcosis), parasitic (toxoplasmosis, leishmaniasis, amoebiasis, malaria).

Infectious causes account for ~30% of classical FUO. Extrapulmonary TB is the single most common infectious cause. Culture-negative endocarditis is an important consideration requiring echocardiography.

**Q:** What is the most common neoplastic cause of FUO, and what solid tumour most commonly causes FUO?

**A:** Most common neoplastic causes overall: lymphomas (especially non-Hodgkin lymphoma) and leukemias. Most common solid tumour causing FUO: renal cell carcinoma.

Neoplastic causes account for ~20% of classical FUO. Other solid tumours include breast, liver (HCC), colon, pancreas, and liver metastases. Multiple myeloma and myelodysplastic syndrome also cause FUO.

**Q:** What collagen vascular and miscellaneous conditions cause classical FUO?

**A:** Collagen vascular (~30%): SLE, RA, rheumatic fever, vasculitis (temporal arteritis, polyarteritis nodosa), juvenile RA, Still disease. Miscellaneous (~20%): drugs (antimicrobials, antiarrhythmics), sarcoidosis, granulomatous hepatitis, IBD, hereditary periodic fever syndromes (e.g., familial Mediterranean fever), PE/DVT, thyroiditis, thyroid storm, adrenal insufficiency, pheochromocytoma.

Collagen vascular diseases are a major and often overlooked cause of FUO, accounting for ~30%. Still disease (adult-onset juvenile RA) classically presents with quotidian fever, salmon rash, and arthritis. Drug fever is an important reversible cause.

**Q:** What types of infection are patients with defective cell-mediated immunity most vulnerable to?

**A:** Latent viruses (e.g., CMV, EBV, VZV), fungi (e.g., Candida, Cryptococcus, Aspergillus, PCP), parasites, and non-tuberculous mycobacteria (NTM).

Cell-mediated immunity defects occur with HIV, Hodgkin lymphoma, hairy cell leukemia, cytotoxic drugs, SCID, and DiGeorge syndrome. These patients cannot control intracellular pathogens. Encapsulated bacteria are better handled by humoral immunity.

**Q:** Which organisms are encapsulated bacteria that cause infection in patients with humoral immune defects (e.g., asplenia, splenectomy)?

**A:** S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, N. meningitidis, Salmonella enterica serotype Typhi, Group B Streptococcus (GBS).

Humoral immunity defects (CLL, multiple myeloma, asplenia, splenectomy, sickle cell, nephrotic syndrome) predispose to encapsulated bacteria because these organisms evade phagocytosis without opsonizing antibodies. Vaccination against S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, and N. meningitidis is critical in asplenic patients.

**Q:** What organisms are neutrophil-deficient patients (e.g., chronic granulomatous disease) most vulnerable to?

**A:** Catalase-producing organisms: Staphylococcus, Serratia, Nocardia, Aspergillus.

Neutrophil dysfunction occurs with chemotherapy, myelodysplasia, CGD, C3/C5 deficiencies. Catalase-positive organisms break down H₂O₂, which is the main neutrophil killing mechanism in CGD patients. Aspergillus is a major threat in prolonged neutropenia.

**Q:** What is the definition of febrile neutropenia?

**A:** Fever (≥38.3°C/101°F or ≥38.0°C/100.4°F for ≥1 hour) PLUS neutropenia: ANC <1.0 × 10⁹/L. Severe neutropenia is ANC <0.5 × 10⁹/L. ANC = WBC × (% neutrophils + % bands).

Febrile neutropenia is the most common life-threatening complication of cancer therapy. Causative organisms are identified only 1/3 of the time. Gram-negative organisms (especially Pseudomonas) were historically most common; Gram-positive organisms are more common now.

**Q:** What examination should NOT be performed in febrile neutropenia, and why?

**A:** Digital rectal examination (DRE) should NOT be performed. The perianal region should be examined visually for perianal abscess, but instrumentation risks bacteremia in a patient with compromised mucosal integrity.

In febrile neutropenia, the perianal area and GI tract are common infection sites due to mucositis. DRE can introduce organisms into the bloodstream. Perianal abscesses can be serious and require careful visual inspection.

**Q:** When is prophylaxis with G-CSF or GM-CSF indicated in febrile neutropenia?

**A:** Indicated when: (1) risk of febrile neutropenia ≥20%, or (2) febrile neutropenia has occurred in a previous chemotherapy cycle. G-CSF/GM-CSF decreases hospitalization but does NOT affect mortality.

Granulocyte colony-stimulating factors (G-CSF, GM-CSF) stimulate myeloid proliferation and shorten neutropenic periods. They are used prophylactically, not to treat established febrile neutropenia.

**Q:** What fungal infections are most common in prolonged neutropenia or concurrent antibiotic use?

**A:** Candida and Aspergillus. Typhlitis (inflammation of the cecum) is a rare but serious bacterial complication.

Fungal superinfection occurs especially when neutropenia is prolonged or when broad-spectrum antibiotics disrupt normal flora. Aspergillus causes invasive pulmonary disease. Typhlitis can lead to serious infection and poor outcomes in febrile neutropenia.

**Q:** What infections are most common in solid organ transplant recipients in the first month post-transplant versus after 1 month?

**A:** < 1 month: donor-derived infections, bacterial wound/line/lung infections, herpetic stomatitis. > 1 month: viral (CMV, EBV, VZV), fungal (Aspergillus, Cryptococcus, P. jirovecii), protozoan (Toxoplasma), unusual bacterial/mycobacterial (TB, Nocardia, Listeria).

The timing of infection in transplant recipients reflects the degree and type of immunosuppression. CMV is the most important pathogen after 1 month and can present as direct disease or predispose to other infections. Prophylaxis is tailored accordingly.

**Q:** What is Immune Reconstitution Inflammatory Syndrome (IRIS), and what is the most common clinical setting?

**A:** IRIS is a harmful inflammatory response directed against a previously acquired (often subclinical) infection following recovery of the immune system. Most cases occur in patients with advanced HIV starting antiretroviral therapy (ART), or immunosuppressed patients discontinuing immunosuppressive therapy.

IRIS affects ~10% of HIV-positive patients starting ART. It represents paradoxical worsening of a treated/partially treated OI, or a new response to a previously unidentified OI. It is thought to be worse with rapid CD4 increase and lower pre-treatment CD4 count.

**Q:** What infections most commonly trigger IRIS, and how is IRIS managed?

**A:** Common triggers: Mycobacteria (TB, MAC), Cryptococcus, Pneumocystis, Toxoplasma, HBV/HCV, herpes viruses (VZV, HSV, CMV), JC virus (PML), Molluscum contagiosum, COVID-19. Management: continue ARV for mild-moderate symptoms; consider stopping ARV if life-threatening; treat underlying infection; consider corticosteroids/NSAIDs for inflammation. IRIS is a diagnosis of exclusion.

Before diagnosing IRIS, rule out drug reactions, medication non-adherence, and drug resistance. Treatment initiation for some OIs (e.g., cryptococcal meningitis) should precede ART initiation to reduce IRIS risk.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of penicillins, and what are their major adverse effects?

**A:** Mechanism: bactericidal; β-lactam binds penicillin-binding proteins (PBP) preventing cross-linking of peptidoglycan in the cell wall. Adverse effects: immediate (IgE-mediated) allergy — anaphylaxis, urticaria; late-onset (IgG) allergy — urticaria, rash, serum sickness; interstitial nephritis; dose-related seizures; diarrhea.

Penicillins are the prototype β-lactam antibiotics. Cross-reactivity with cephalosporins occurs in ~10% of penicillin-allergic patients (type I hypersensitivity cross-reactivity is rare). Allergy history must be carefully characterized.

**Q:** What is the spectrum of coverage of cloxacillin, and what is its primary indication?

**A:** Coverage: methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus (MSSA) and streptococci. Primary indication: bacterial infections caused by staphylococci and streptococci, especially skin and soft-tissue infections.

Cloxacillin (isoxazolyl penicillin) is penicillinase-resistant, making it the first-line treatment for MSSA infections. It does NOT cover MRSA. For MRSA, vancomycin, daptomycin, or linezolid must be used.

**Q:** What is the advantage of amoxicillin-clavulanate over amoxicillin alone, and what is a major contraindication?

**A:** Advantage: the β-lactamase inhibitor clavulanate prevents inactivation of amoxicillin by bacterial β-lactamases, extending coverage to β-lactamase-producing organisms including Staphylococcus, H. influenzae, Enterococcus, and anaerobes. Contraindication: history of amoxicillin-clavulanate–associated jaundice or hepatic dysfunction.

Amoxicillin-clavulanate (Clavulin®/Augmentin®) is used for URTI, sinusitis, AOM, skin/soft-tissue infections, UTI, and intra-abdominal/pelvic infections. Cholestatic jaundice is an idiosyncratic reaction more common in older males and those on prolonged therapy.

**Q:** What is the coverage spectrum and clinical use of ceftriaxone (3rd-generation cephalosporin)?

**A:** Coverage: broad Gram-negative (including Enterobacteriaceae), Streptococcus pneumoniae, limited Staphylococcus aureus (MSSA), NO MRSA, NO Pseudomonas (except ceftazidime), NO Enterococcus. Uses: community-acquired pneumonia, gonorrhea, bacterial meningitis, abdominal/pelvic infections (with metronidazole), convenient once-daily outpatient IV therapy.

Third-generation cephalosporins have enhanced Gram-negative coverage compared to first/second generation. Ceftriaxone's long half-life (8 hours) allows once-daily dosing. It is the drug of choice for gonorrhea and community-acquired bacterial meningitis.

**Q:** Which cephalosporins cover MRSA, and what is their generation?

**A:** 5th-generation cephalosporins: ceftobiprole (Zevtera®) and ceftaroline (Teflaro™). Both cover MRSA. Ceftaroline is available in Canada through the Special Access Program. Note: ceftobiprole does NOT cover Pseudomonas; ceftaroline does not either.

Fifth-generation cephalosporins have expanded Gram-positive activity including MRSA, while maintaining broad Gram-negative coverage. They are used for acute bacterial skin and skin structure infections and community-acquired pneumonia.

**Q:** What is the major toxicity of aminoglycosides, and which toxicity is the most important clinically?

**A:** Toxicities: nephrotoxicity (reversible), vestibular toxicity (irreversible), ototoxicity (irreversible). Most important: vestibular toxicity (irreversible). Contraindicated in patients with pre-existing hearing loss or renal dysfunction.

Aminoglycosides (gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin) inhibit the 30S ribosomal subunit. They are used for Gram-negative infections (including Pseudomonas) and as low-dose synergy with β-lactams/vancomycin for enterococcal endocarditis. Drug levels and renal function must be monitored.

**Q:** What is the spectrum of vancomycin, its mechanism, and its major adverse effects?

**A:** Coverage: Gram-positive organisms including MRSA; NOT VRE. Oral vancomycin covers C. difficile (GI only, not systemic). Mechanism: glycopeptide sterically inhibits cell wall synthesis. Adverse effects: Red Man syndrome (infusion-related, not true allergy), nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, thrombocytopenia.

Vancomycin is the cornerstone of MRSA treatment. Red Man syndrome is an infusion reaction (flushing, erythema, hypotension) prevented by slowing infusion rate. It is NOT an IgE-mediated allergy. Oral vancomycin is non-absorbable and used only for severe C. difficile infection.

**Q:** Why is daptomycin NOT used to treat MRSA pneumonia?

**A:** Daptomycin is inactivated by pulmonary surfactant, making it ineffective for pulmonary infections. It should not be used for MRSA pneumonia.

Daptomycin (a lipopeptide) binds to the cell wall forming channels leading to intracellular K⁺ depletion. It is effective for bacteremia, endocarditis, and skin/soft-tissue infections caused by MRSA and VRE, but pulmonary surfactant deactivates it in the lungs.

**Q:** What are the major adverse effects of fluoroquinolones, and which populations should avoid them?

**A:** Adverse effects: headache, dizziness, allergy, seizures, prolonged QT, dysglycemia (levofloxacin/moxifloxacin), tendonitis, tendon rupture. Avoid in: pregnancy, lactation, children under 18 years, and patients on other QT-prolonging medications.

Fluoroquinolones inhibit DNA gyrase (topoisomerase II). Respiratory quinolones (levofloxacin, moxifloxacin) cover S. pneumoniae, making them useful for CAP. Tendon rupture risk is highest in the elderly and those on corticosteroids. Dysglycemia can cause both hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of metronidazole, and what is the disulfiram-like reaction?

**A:** Mechanism: forms toxic metabolites in bacterial cells that damage microbial DNA. Disulfiram-like reaction: co-ingestion with alcohol causes flushing, nausea, vomiting, tachycardia. Alcohol must be avoided during treatment and for 3 days after completion.

Metronidazole (Flagyl®) covers anaerobes and protozoa (Trichomonas, Giardia, Entamoeba). It is first-line for bacterial vaginosis, Trichomonas, C. difficile (now less preferred than vancomycin/fidaxomicin for severe disease), and intra-abdominal infections with anaerobes.

**Q:** What organisms does TMP-SMX cover, and what are its major adverse effects and contraindications?

**A:** Coverage: GP (S. aureus including most MRSA), GN enteric bacteria, Nocardia, Pneumocystis jirovecii, Toxoplasma. Adverse effects: hepatitis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, bone marrow suppression, hyperkalemia, drug interactions (increases free warfarin, glyburide). Contraindications: sulfa allergy, infants <4 weeks, hepatic/renal dysfunction, pregnancy/lactation.

TMP-SMX (Septra®/Bactrim®) inhibits the folic acid pathway: SMX competes with PABA and TMP inhibits DHFR. It is used for UTI, RTI, skin/soft-tissue infections (MRSA), and prophylaxis/treatment of PCP in immunocompromised patients.

**Q:** Why is nitrofurantoin NOT appropriate for pyelonephritis or bacteremia?

**A:** Nitrofurantoin achieves adequate concentrations only in the urine (lower urinary tract) and does not achieve therapeutic levels in renal tissue, blood, or systemic tissues. It is indicated only for lower UTI. It is contraindicated with significant renal impairment (reduced urinary concentration and risk of peripheral neuropathy).

Nitrofurantoin (MacroBID®) inhibits bacterial ribosomal protein synthesis via reactive metabolites. It covers Enterococcus, S. saprophyticus, and GN coliforms. It must not be used in oliguria, anuria, or renal impairment, during labor, or in infants <1 month.

**Q:** What are the four drugs in the standard RIPE regimen for active tuberculosis, and what are the key adverse effects of each?

**A:** RIPE: Rifampin (hepatotoxicity, P450 induction, orange secretions), Isoniazid/INH (hepatotoxicity, peripheral neuropathy, drug-induced SLE), Pyrazinamide/PZA (hepatotoxicity, gout, GI), Ethambutol (optic neuropathy — loss of central and colour vision).

All four drugs inhibit mycobacterial pathways. Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) is given with INH to prevent peripheral neuropathy. Rifampin is a potent P450 inducer causing multiple drug interactions. Ethambutol toxicity requires monitoring visual acuity and colour vision.

**Q:** What is unique about the mechanism of isoniazid and ethambutol compared to other antibiotics?

**A:** Both inhibit mycolic acid synthesis, a component unique to the mycobacterial cell wall. This makes them specific to mycobacteria and inactive against other bacteria.

Mycolic acids are long-chain fatty acids essential for the mycobacterial cell wall. INH inhibits InhA (enoyl-ACP reductase) in the mycolic acid synthesis pathway. Ethambutol inhibits arabinosyltransferase. This specificity explains why they are reserved for TB/NTM treatment.

**Q:** What are the key adverse effects of linezolid, and what important drug interaction must be avoided?

**A:** Adverse effects with prolonged use: myelosuppression, optic neuropathy, peripheral neuropathy. Drug interaction: linezolid acts as an MAO inhibitor and causes hypertension; concurrent use with or within 2 weeks of MAO inhibitors is contraindicated. Also avoid serotonergic drugs (serotonin syndrome risk).

Linezolid (Zyvoxam®) binds to the 50S ribosome and prevents formation of the functional 70S initiation complex. It covers VRE and MRSA, making it useful when vancomycin cannot be used. CBC monitoring is required with prolonged use due to myelosuppression.

**Q:** What is the coverage of clindamycin, and what is its most serious adverse effect?

**A:** Coverage: Gram-positive (except Enterococcus), most community-acquired MRSA, anaerobes. Most serious adverse effect: pseudomembranous colitis (C. difficile infection). Also causes GI upset.

Clindamycin (Dalacin®) inhibits peptide bond formation at the 50S ribosome. It is used for skin/skin-structure infections, oropharyngeal infections, and in combination with Gram-negative coverage for intra-abdominal/pelvic infections. C. difficile risk is higher than with many other antibiotics.

**Q:** What are the reasons for using combination antibiotic therapy?

**A:** 1) Polymicrobial infection requiring broad coverage. 2) Empiric therapy pending culture results. 3) Synergy for difficult-to-treat pathogens (e.g., Enterococcus spp. causing endocarditis). 4) To prevent emergence of resistance.

Combination therapy is not routinely indicated but has specific applications. Enterococcal endocarditis requires synergistic therapy (e.g., ampicillin + gentamicin). TB therapy uses multiple agents to prevent resistance. De-escalation to monotherapy based on cultures reduces toxicity and resistance.

**Q:** What antibiotic classes are bactericidal vs. bacteriostatic? (Use the mnemonics 'Very Finely Proficient At Cell MurDer' and 'ECSTaTiC')

**A:** Bactericidal ('Very Finely Proficient At Cell MurDer'): Vancomycin, Fluoroquinolones, Penicillins, Aminoglycosides, Cephalosporins, Metronidazole, Daptomycin. Bacteriostatic ('ECSTaTiC'): Erythromycin (macrolides), Clindamycin, Sulfonamides, Trimethoprim, Tetracyclines, Chloramphenicol.

Bactericidal agents kill bacteria; bacteriostatic agents inhibit growth and rely on host immunity to clear infection. In immunocompromised patients and endocarditis, bactericidal agents are generally preferred. This distinction is clinically important for serious infections.

**Q:** What are the antiviral drugs used for herpes simplex and varicella-zoster infections, and what is the IV adverse effect of acyclovir?

**A:** Drugs: acyclovir (IV/PO), valacyclovir (prodrug of acyclovir, PO), famciclovir (PO). Mechanism: guanosine analogue inhibiting viral DNA polymerase. IV acyclovir adverse effect: nephrotoxicity and CNS toxicity. PO forms are well-tolerated.

Acyclovir is activated by viral thymidine kinase (selective for infected cells). Valacyclovir has better oral bioavailability. IV acyclovir nephrotoxicity is prevented by adequate hydration. Famciclovir is an alternative with similar efficacy.

**Q:** What is the treatment for CMV infection, and what is the main hematologic toxicity of ganciclovir?

**A:** Treatment: ganciclovir (IV) or valganciclovir (PO prodrug). Mechanism: guanosine analogue inhibiting viral DNA polymerase. Main hematologic toxicity: neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, anemia.

Ganciclovir/valganciclovir is first-line for CMV disease (retinitis, esophagitis, colitis, pneumonitis, especially in transplant and HIV patients). It also covers HSV-1/2, VZV, HHV-6, and EBV. Foscarnet is used for ganciclovir-resistant CMV.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of neuraminidase inhibitors (oseltamivir, zanamivir), and what is the key adverse effect of zanamivir?

**A:** Mechanism: inhibit neuraminidase, an enzyme required for release of influenza virus from infected cells, preventing viral aggregation and spread. Both treat and prevent Influenza A and B. Key adverse effect of zanamivir: bronchospasm (inhaled formulation; avoid in asthma/COPD).

Oseltamivir (Tamiflu®) is the most commonly used neuraminidase inhibitor. It should be started within 48 hours of symptom onset for best effect. Zanamivir (Relenza®) is inhaled and not appropriate for patients with reactive airway disease.

**Q:** What is nirmatrelvir/ritonavir (Paxlovid®), and what is an important drug interaction concern?

**A:** Nirmatrelvir/ritonavir (Paxlovid®) is used for COVID-19 infection within 5 days of symptom onset. Nirmatrelvir inhibits SARS-CoV-2 protease; ritonavir is a CYP3A inhibitor boosting nirmatrelvir concentrations. Key interaction: contraindicated with highly CYP3A-dependent substrates (e.g., certain statins, antiarrhythmics) and potent CYP3A inducers (e.g., rifampin).

Ritonavir is a potent CYP3A4 inhibitor that increases nirmatrelvir levels. This creates significant drug interactions requiring medication review before prescribing. Side effects include hypertension, diarrhea, dysgeusia, and myalgia.

**Q:** What are the first-line antibiotics for Pseudomonas aeruginosa infections?

**A:** First-line options: ciprofloxacin, gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin, piperacillin/tazobactam, ceftazidime, cefepime, imipenem, meropenem. NOT ertapenem (no Pseudomonas coverage).

Pseudomonas aeruginosa is an intrinsically resistant Gram-negative rod. Combination therapy is often used for severe infections. Ertapenem is a carbapenem that lacks Pseudomonas coverage, which is a critical distinction on exams.

**Q:** What antibiotics cover VRE (vancomycin-resistant Enterococcus)?

**A:** For VRE: linezolid, daptomycin, tigecycline. Nitrofurantoin can be used for lower UTI caused by VRE. Vancomycin does NOT cover VRE.

VRE is a significant nosocomial pathogen in immunocompromised patients. Linezolid and daptomycin are the main systemic agents. Tigecycline has broad activity including VRE. Infection control measures are critical to prevent VRE spread.

**Q:** What antibiotics cover anaerobic infections, and which is the drug of choice for intra-abdominal anaerobic infections?

**A:** Anaerobic coverage: metronidazole, clindamycin, amoxicillin-clavulanate, cefoxitin, piperacillin/tazobactam, carbapenems (imipenem, meropenem, ertapenem), moxifloxacin. Drug of choice for intra-abdominal anaerobes: metronidazole (often combined with a Gram-negative agent).

Anaerobes are important in intra-abdominal, pelvic, and polymicrobial infections. B. fragilis is the most common anaerobic pathogen in bowel flora and is resistant to penicillin alone. Metronidazole has excellent anaerobic activity and CNS penetration (useful for brain abscesses).

**Q:** What is the coverage of carbapenems, and which carbapenem does NOT cover Pseudomonas or Enterococcus?

**A:** Carbapenems cover most Gram-positive (except MRSA), Gram-negative (including ESBL-producing organisms), and anaerobes. Ertapenem is the only carbapenem that does NOT cover Pseudomonas aeruginosa or Enterococcus.

Carbapenems (imipenem, meropenem, ertapenem) are broad-spectrum β-lactams used for ESBL-producing Enterobacteriaceae. Imipenem can lower the seizure threshold. Ertapenem's once-daily dosing is convenient for outpatient IV therapy but its narrower spectrum is a critical distinction.

**Q:** What are the adverse effects and key drug interaction of rifampin?

**A:** Adverse effects: hepatic dysfunction, orange tears/saliva/urine (harmless), P450 enzyme induction (major drug interaction reducing efficacy of many drugs). Key rule: NEVER use rifampin as monotherapy (except prophylaxis) due to rapid resistance development.

Rifampin inhibits bacterial RNA polymerase. As a potent CYP450 inducer, it significantly reduces concentrations of drugs including warfarin, oral contraceptives, antiretrovirals, and immunosuppressants. It is always used in combination for active TB.

**Q:** What is the treatment for latent tuberculosis in Canada?

**A:** Options for latent TB treatment: isoniazid (INH) alone (9 months), or rifampin alone (4 months), or combined INH + rifapentine regimens. INH alone is most common. Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) is given with INH to prevent peripheral neuropathy.

Latent TB treatment reduces the risk of reactivation disease. Rifampin for 4 months has comparable efficacy to INH for 9 months with better tolerability. Baseline and monitoring liver function tests are recommended, especially with INH.

**Q:** What antiviral agents are used for chronic hepatitis B, and what is the role of pegylated interferon?

**A:** Antivirals for chronic hepatitis B: tenofovir (first-line), entecavir (first-line), lamivudine (less commonly used due to resistance). Pegylated interferon α-2a or α-2b: inhibits viral protein synthesis; used less commonly now; adverse effects include flu-like syndrome, depression, bone marrow suppression.

Tenofovir and entecavir have high genetic barriers to resistance and are preferred. Entecavir is contraindicated as monotherapy in HIV co-infection (risk of HIV resistance). Lamivudine has high resistance rates with long-term use.

**Q:** What is the treatment regimen for chronic hepatitis C, and what are the key drug interactions with glecaprevir/pibrentasvir?

**A:** Hepatitis C treatment: direct-acting antivirals. Glecaprevir/pibrentasvir (Maviret®): NS3/4A protease inhibitor + NS5A inhibitor. Sofosbuvir/velpatasvir (Epclusa®): NS5B polymerase inhibitor + NS5A inhibitor. Contraindications for glecaprevir/pibrentasvir: moderate/severe hepatic impairment, co-administration with atazanavir, rifampin, atorvastatin, dabigatran, ethinyl estradiol, or simvastatin.

Modern HCV regimens achieve >95% sustained virologic response (cure). Treatment duration is 8–12 weeks depending on genotype and prior treatment. Ribavirin is rarely needed with current DAA regimens. Drug interactions are a key clinical concern.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and indication of remdesivir for COVID-19?

**A:** Mechanism: adenosine triphosphate analogue that inhibits SARS-CoV-2 RNA-dependent RNA polymerase, blocking viral RNA synthesis. Indication: hospitalized COVID-19 patients. Adverse effects: bradycardia, hypotension, increased ALT/AST, hypersensitivity reactions (anaphylaxis, angioedema), skin rash, GI effects, prolonged prothrombin time, seizure.

Remdesivir was the first antiviral approved for COVID-19 in Canada. It is given IV for 5 days in hospitalized patients. Its benefit is most pronounced when started early in the disease course. It is not used in outpatient settings.

**Q:** What are the major toxicities of dapsone, and in which patient population is it contraindicated?

**A:** Toxicities: rash, drug fever, agranulocytosis, methemoglobinemia. Contraindicated in: G6PD deficiency (causes hemolytic anemia). Dapsone is used for leprosy (M. leprae), PCP prophylaxis and treatment (with TMP), and toxoplasmosis prophylaxis (with pyrimethamine).

Dapsone (a sulfone) inhibits folic acid synthesis by competing with PABA, similar to sulfonamides. G6PD testing should be performed before initiating dapsone. It is an alternative to TMP-SMX for PCP prophylaxis in patients with sulfa allergy.

**Q:** What tetracyclines are contraindicated in children under 12 years and in pregnancy, and why?

**A:** All tetracyclines (tetracycline, doxycycline, minocycline) are contraindicated in children under 12 years and in pregnancy/lactation. Reason: yellow teeth staining and stunted bone growth in children; teratogenic effects in pregnancy.

Tetracyclines chelate calcium in developing teeth and bone, causing permanent discolouration and growth retardation. They are also hepatotoxic in pregnancy, especially at high doses. Doxycycline is used for malaria prophylaxis, Rickettsial infections, PID (step-down), Chlamydia, Lyme disease, and acne.

**Q:** What are the 'respiratory fluoroquinolones,' and why are they preferred over ciprofloxacin for community-acquired pneumonia (CAP)?

**A:** Respiratory fluoroquinolones: levofloxacin and moxifloxacin. They cover S. pneumoniae (unlike ciprofloxacin) and atypicals (Legionella, Mycoplasma, Chlamydophila). Moxifloxacin also has additional anaerobic coverage. Ciprofloxacin has poor pneumococcal coverage and should NOT be used for CAP unless a susceptible organism is isolated.

The distinction between ciprofloxacin and respiratory fluoroquinolones is a common exam topic. Ciprofloxacin has excellent Gram-negative and Pseudomonas coverage but inadequate pneumococcal coverage. Levofloxacin and moxifloxacin are appropriate for CAP monotherapy.

**Q:** What are the chloramphenicol adverse effects that limit its use, and when is it still indicated?

**A:** Adverse effects: aplastic anemia (rare, idiosyncratic, irreversible), Grey Baby syndrome (neonates — cardiovascular collapse due to immature glucuronyl transferase). Indications: serious infections by susceptible organisms when suitable alternatives are not available, including meningococcal disease in patients with anaphylaxis to β-lactams.

Chloramphenicol inhibits peptidyl transferase at the 50S ribosome. Despite broad coverage (GP, GN, anaerobes), its toxicity profile limits use to last-resort situations. Grey Baby syndrome occurs because neonates cannot conjugate chloramphenicol, leading to toxic accumulation.
